
---
## Section: seg_5

#### LO 1: Define statistics as the science dealing with the collection, analysis, interpretation, and presentation of data.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Statistics): Statistics is the science dealing with the ______.
**Answer**: collection, analysis, interpretation, and presentation of data

#### LO 2: Interpret basic data displays (such as dot plots) to discuss clustering and differences in the data as part of statistical thinking.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Statistics): A dot plot of 20 values shows most dots between 6 and 7, with a few near 2 and a few near 10. Which statement best interprets this display?
A. The data cluster around 6–7, with possible differences between low and high groups.
B. The data are perfectly uniform across all values.
C. Every value appears equally often.
D. There is only one unique value in the dataset.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: Summarize data by graphing (e.g., dot plots) and by using numbers such as an average.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Descriptive Statistics): Which action best illustrates descriptive statistics?
A. Create a dot plot of the data and compute an average.
B. Use the sample to claim the entire population prefers option A.
C. Test a hypothesis about a population parameter.
D. Randomly assign treatments in an experiment.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 4: Calculate a simple average to numerically summarize a small dataset.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Descriptive Statistics): What is the average of 5, 7, and 8?
A. 5.0
B. 6.7
C. 7.5
D. 8.0
**Answer**: B

#### LO 5: Define inferential statistics as formal methods for drawing conclusions from good data using probability.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Inferential Statistics): Inferential statistics are ______.
**Answer**: formal methods for drawing conclusions from data

#### LO 6: Describe how inferential statistics use probability to assess confidence in conclusions.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Inferential Statistics): Which best describes the role of probability in inferential statistics?
A. It is used to assess how confident we can be in conclusions drawn from data.
B. It is used to graph and organize raw data.
C. It identifies variables before data collection.
D. It replaces the need to collect data.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: Differentiate inferential statistics from descriptive statistics by their goals of drawing conclusions versus organizing and summarizing data.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Inferential Statistics): Which statement correctly contrasts inferential and descriptive statistics?
A. Inferential draws conclusions from data; descriptive organizes and summarizes data.
B. Inferential organizes and summarizes data; descriptive draws conclusions from data.
C. Both only organize data without conclusions.
D. Both only draw conclusions without organizing data.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 8: Define statistical inference as using probability to determine how confident we can be that conclusions from sample data are correct.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Statistical Inference): Statistical inference is ______.
**Answer**: using probability to determine how confident we can be that conclusions are correct

#### LO 9: Describe the role of probability in supporting valid interpretation of data.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Statistical Inference): In statistical inference, probability primarily helps us to:
A. Quantify how confident we can be that conclusions are correct.
B. Eliminate all error from data collection.
C. Avoid taking a sample at all.
D. Ensure that every observation is identical.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 10: Differentiate statistical inference from descriptive summarization of data in study examples.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Statistical Inference): Which statement illustrates statistical inference (not just descriptive summarization)?
A. Based on the sample average of 7, we use probability to state how confident we are that the population average is near 7.
B. The sample has an average value of 7.
C. The data are shown in a dot plot.
D. The dataset contains 50 observations.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: Define probability as a mathematical tool used to study randomness and the likelihood of events.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Probability): Probability is a mathematical tool used to study ______.
**Answer**: randomness

#### LO 12: Describe the long-run pattern of outcomes in repeated random trials using coin-toss examples.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Probability): In many repeated coin tosses, which statement best describes the long-run pattern?
A. The fraction of heads tends to settle near the chance (likelihood) of heads.
B. Heads and tails must strictly alternate.
C. After 100 tosses, the number of heads becomes certain.
D. The coin’s past outcomes change the chance of future outcomes.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 13: Define a population as the entire collection of persons, things, or objects under study.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Population): A population is the ______ under study.
**Answer**: collection of persons, things, or objects

#### LO 14: Differentiate a population from a sample in applied study scenarios.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Population): A city has 200,000 households. Researchers randomly select 1,000 households to survey. What is the population?
A. All 200,000 households in the city.
B. The 1,000 surveyed households.
C. All households in the researchers’ neighborhood.
D. Any 1,000 households selected in the future.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: Define sampling as selecting a portion (subset) of a larger population to study to gain information about the population.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sampling): Sampling is ______.
**Answer**: selecting a portion (subset) of a larger population to study to gain information about the population

#### LO 16: Differentiate sampling from a census (examining an entire population) in terms of feasibility and purpose.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sampling): Which statement correctly distinguishes sampling from a census?
A. Sampling studies a subset of the population; a census examines the entire population.
B. Sampling examines the entire population; a census studies only a subset.
C. Both sampling and a census always examine the entire population.
D. Both sampling and a census always examine only a subset.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 17: Define a sample as the selected subset of the population used to study the population.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sample): A sample is ______.
**Answer**: the selected portion (subset) of the population used to study the population

#### LO 18: Differentiate a representative sample from a non-representative one using given scenarios.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sample): To study all college students’ sleep hours, which sample is more likely to be representative?
A. Randomly selecting students across different majors and class times.
B. Surveying only students from one early-morning fitness class.
C. Asking for volunteers at a single club meeting.
D. Surveying only students in one dorm hallway.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 19: Define a statistic as a number that represents a property of a sample and estimates a population parameter.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Statistic): A statistic is ______.
**Answer**: a number that represents a property of the sample; an estimate of a population parameter

#### LO 20: Calculate a sample mean from provided data as an example of a statistic.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Statistic): For the sample data 10, 12, 14, and 10, what is the sample mean?
A. 10.5
B. 11.0
C. 11.5
D. 12.0
**Answer**: C

#### LO 21: Define a parameter as a numerical characteristic of a population that can be estimated by a statistic.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Parameter): A parameter is ______.
**Answer**: a numerical characteristic of the whole population that can be estimated by a statistic

#### LO 22: Differentiate a parameter from a statistic in study contexts.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Parameter): Which is a parameter (not a statistic)?
A. The mean income of all households in a city.
B. The mean income of 200 surveyed households.
C. The median income of a sample of 200 households.
D. The proportion computed from a sample survey.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 23: Define a representative sample as one that contains the characteristics of the population.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Representative Sample): A representative sample is ______.
**Answer**: a sample that contains the characteristics of the population

#### LO 24: Define a variable as a characteristic or measurement determined for each member of a population.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Variable): A variable is ______.
**Answer**: a characteristic or measurement that can be determined for each member of a population

#### LO 25: Classify variables in examples as numerical or categorical.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Variable): Which classification is correct?
A. Number of siblings: numerical; Eye color: categorical.
B. Number of siblings: categorical; Eye color: numerical.
C. Number of siblings: categorical; Eye color: categorical.
D. Number of siblings: numerical; Eye color: numerical.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 26: Define a numerical variable as one that takes on values with equal units such as weight in pounds or time in hours.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Numerical Variable): A numerical variable is ______.
**Answer**: a variable that takes on values with equal units, such as weight in pounds or time in hours

#### LO 27: Classify measurements like points earned or hours of sleep as numerical variables.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Numerical Variable): Which list contains only numerical variables?
A. Points earned, hours of sleep, weight in pounds
B. Party affiliation, favorite color, hometown
C. Party affiliation, hours of sleep, weight in pounds
D. Eye color, shirt size (S/M/L), hometown
**Answer**: A

#### LO 28: Differentiate numerical variables from categorical variables by whether arithmetic operations are meaningful.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Numerical Variable): Which statement correctly distinguishes numerical from categorical variables?
A. Numerical variables have equal units so arithmetic operations (like averaging) are meaningful; categorical variables do not.
B. Categorical variables have equal units so averaging is meaningful; numerical variables do not.
C. Neither numerical nor categorical variables can be averaged.
D. Categorical variables include values like hours and pounds with equal units.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 29: Define a categorical variable as one that places a person or thing into a category.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Categorical Variable): A categorical variable is ______.
**Answer**: a variable that places a person or thing into a category

#### LO 30: Differentiate categorical variables from numerical variables using examples such as party affiliation.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Categorical Variable): Which is a categorical variable?
A. Party affiliation (e.g., A, B, C)
B. Weight in pounds
C. Time in hours
D. Points earned on a test
**Answer**: A

#### LO 31: Define data as the actual values of variables resulting from sampling, and datum as a single value.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Data): Data are ______; they may be numbers or words.
**Answer**: the actual values of the variable resulting from sampling

#### LO 32: Exemplify data with values such as 5, 6.5, and 7 hours of sleep or $150, $200, and $225 spent.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Data): Which of the following are data?
A. The recorded values 5, 6.5, and 7 from a sample
B. The idea of measuring time in hours (without values)
C. The definition of a variable
D. A plan to conduct a survey next month
**Answer**: A

#### LO 33: Define the mean as the arithmetic average found by adding values and dividing by the number of values.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Mean): The mean is the ______ of a set of numbers, computed by adding the values and dividing by the number of values.
**Answer**: arithmetic average

#### LO 34: Calculate the mean of a small dataset (e.g., three exam scores) to one decimal place.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Mean): What is the mean of exam scores 82, 90, and 88 (to one decimal place)?
A. 86.0
B. 86.7
C. 87.0
D. 87.5
**Answer**: B

#### LO 35: Explain the common use of the word 'average' to refer to the arithmetic mean.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Mean): In common use, the word “average” often refers to the ______, found by adding values and dividing by how many there are.
**Answer**: arithmetic average

#### LO 36: Calculate a proportion as the count in a category divided by the total (e.g., 22 out of 40).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Proportion): If 22 out of 40 students prefer option A, what is the proportion who prefer A?
A. 0.55
B. 0.45
C. 1.82
D. 0.22
**Answer**: A

#### LO 37: Identify the category count and total needed to compute a proportion in a study scenario.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Proportion): A survey finds 18 students prefer online notes out of 50 surveyed. Which two numbers are needed to compute the proportion who prefer online notes?
A. 18 and 50
B. 50 and the total population size
C. 18 and the number who do not prefer online notes
D. 18 and 100
**Answer**: A


---
## Section: seg_7

#### LO 1: define qualitative data as data resulting from categorizing or describing attributes of a population
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Qualitative Data): Qualitative data result from __________; often described by words or letters.
**Answer**: categorizing or describing attributes

#### LO 2: classify data examples as qualitative when they are categorical labels such as hair color or blood type
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Qualitative Data): Which example is qualitative?
A. Hair color
B. Weight in pounds
C. Number of students in a class
D. Time in seconds
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: define quantitative data as numerical data obtained by counting or measuring attributes
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Quantitative Data): Quantitative data are numerical data resulting from __________.
**Answer**: counting or measuring attributes

#### LO 4: differentiate quantitative data from qualitative data by recognizing that quantitative values are numbers amenable to mathematical analysis
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Quantitative Data): Which statement indicates data are quantitative?
A. The values are numbers that can be averaged.
B. The values are labels or names only.
C. The data are categories described by words or letters.
D. The values cannot be used in mathematical analysis.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: define quantitative discrete data as count-based numerical values that take on only certain numbers
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Quantitative Discrete Data): Quantitative discrete data are counting-based numerical data that __________.
**Answer**: take on only certain values

#### LO 6: classify data as discrete when they arise from counting, such as number of phone calls or books
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Quantitative Discrete Data): Which is an example of discrete data?
A. Number of books on a shelf
B. Length of a table
C. Weight of a package
D. Time to run a mile
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: define quantitative continuous data as measurement-based numerical values that may include fractions or decimals
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Quantitative Continuous Data): Quantitative continuous data are measurement-based numerical data that may include __________.
**Answer**: fractions, decimals, or irrational numbers

#### LO 8: classify data as continuous when they result from measurements like length, weight, or time
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Quantitative Continuous Data): Which is an example of continuous data?
A. Time to complete a task
B. Number of siblings
C. Count of emails received
D. Number of cars in a lot
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: differentiate continuous from discrete data by recognizing the possibility of fractional values produced by measurement
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Quantitative Continuous Data): Which feature best distinguishes continuous data from discrete data?
A. Possibility of fractional values produced by measurement
B. Values are counts only
C. Values take only certain whole numbers
D. Values are labels or categories
**Answer**: A

#### LO 10: define a pie chart as a circular graph with wedges proportional to category percentages that sum to 100% with no missing categories
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Pie Chart): A pie chart shows categories as wedges proportional to category percentages and requires __________.
**Answer**: percentages that sum to 100%

#### LO 11: explain when a pie chart is appropriate and when it is not, such as when percentages exceed or fall short of 100% or categories are missing
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Pie Chart): When is a pie chart appropriate?
A. When all categories are included and percentages sum to 100%
B. When percentages exceed 100% due to overlapping categories
C. When some categories are missing
D. When the total is unknown and categories may not add to 100%
**Answer**: A

#### LO 12: define a bar graph as a graph with bars whose lengths represent the number or percent in each category
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Bar Graph): A bar graph displays __________; bars may be vertical or horizontal.
**Answer**: bars whose lengths are proportional to the number or percent in each category

#### LO 13: interpret bar graphs to compare category sizes, including cases with percentages exceeding 100% or with missing categories
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Bar Graph): Which is a correct interpretation of a bar graph?
A. Longer bars indicate larger numbers or percents in their categories
B. Bars in a bar graph must sum to 100% like a pie chart
C. Bars must be vertical to compare categories
D. A bar graph cannot be used if some categories are missing
**Answer**: A

#### LO 14: define relative frequency as the percentage or proportion for a category
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Relative Frequency): Relative frequency refers to __________ used alongside counts to compare categories.
**Answer**: percentages or proportions

#### LO 15: compare categories across groups using relative frequencies to account for different totals
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Relative Frequency): Why use relative frequencies when comparing categories across two groups of different sizes?
A. They account for different totals by using proportions or percentages
B. They eliminate the need for counts entirely
C. They ensure totals sum to exactly 100% in each bar graph
D. They convert qualitative data into quantitative measurements
**Answer**: A

#### LO 16: define a sample as a subset intended to represent a population
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sample): A sample is __________ and intended to have the same characteristics as the population.
**Answer**: a subset used to represent a population

#### LO 17: classify scenarios as involving a sample versus a population based on whether a subset or the entire group is used
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sample): Which scenario uses a sample rather than the entire population?
A. Surveying 200 students from a university of 10,000
B. Measuring every household in a city
C. Recording the temperature of each item produced on a single day
D. Asking all employees in a company to respond
**Answer**: A

#### LO 18: define a simple random sample as one where every group of n individuals has an equal chance of selection
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Simple Random Sample): In a simple random sample, __________.
**Answer**: any group of n individuals is equally likely to be chosen

#### LO 19: classify sampling methods as random or non-random based on their selection process
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Random Sampling): Which method is random sampling?
A. Using a random number generator to select members from a complete list
B. Asking for volunteers at a meeting
C. Selecting the first 50 names on a list
D. Surveying people who happen to be nearby
**Answer**: A

#### LO 20: define a stratified sample as one obtained by dividing the population into strata and selecting a proportionate number from each
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Stratified Sample): A stratified sample is obtained by __________.
**Answer**: dividing the population into groups (strata) and taking a proportionate number from each

#### LO 21: classify examples as stratified sampling when selection is made proportionately within groups
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Stratified Sample): Which scenario describes stratified sampling?
A. Dividing students by class year and randomly selecting a proportionate number from each class
B. Selecting every 10th name on a roster after a random start
C. Randomly choosing three classrooms and surveying everyone in them
D. Surveying volunteers who respond to an online post
**Answer**: A

#### LO 22: define sampling bias as occurring when some population members are not as likely to be chosen as others
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sampling Bias): Sampling bias occurs when __________.
**Answer**: some population members are not as likely to be chosen as others

#### LO 23: differentiate nonsampling error from sampling error using provided examples
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Nonsampling Error): Which is an example of a nonsampling error?
A. A defective counting device misrecords totals
B. A small sample size leads to unstable estimates
C. A selection method excludes certain groups
D. Two random samples differ due to chance
**Answer**: A

#### LO 24: explain why a relationship between variables does not imply causation and how other variables may be involved
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Causality): Which statement correctly reflects causality?
A. Correlation alone does not establish a cause-and-effect relationship
B. Any strong relationship implies one variable causes the other
C. If two variables move together, confounding cannot be present
D. Causality is established whenever a bar graph shows differences
**Answer**: A


---
## Section: seg_9

#### LO 1: Define levels of measurement and their role in selecting appropriate statistical procedures.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Level Of Measurement): Complete the definition: “Level of measurement” is “the way a set of data is measured” and ______.
**Answer**: determines which statistical procedures are appropriate.

#### LO 2: Describe the four levels of measurement (nominal, ordinal, interval, ratio) and allowable statistical operations at each level.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Level Of Measurement): Which statement correctly matches a level with allowable statistical operations?
A. Nominal: ratios are meaningful because a true zero exists.
B. Ordinal: differences between values are measurable and calculations are allowed.
C. Interval: calculations are allowed but ratio comparisons are not meaningful.
D. Ratio: no true zero, so ratios are not meaningful.
**Answer**: C

#### LO 3: Define the nominal scale level as unordered qualitative categories that are not used in calculations.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Nominal Scale Level): Nominal scale level consists of qualitative categories or labels with ______; cannot be used in calculations.
**Answer**: no inherent order

#### LO 4: Describe examples of nominal data and the lack of meaningful order or arithmetic.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Nominal Scale Level): A dataset of car colors (red, blue, black, etc.) is best described how?
A. An ordered scale with measurable differences between values.
B. Qualitative labels with no inherent order; cannot be used in calculations.
C. Data with a true zero so ratios of colors are meaningful.
D. Quantitative values where subtraction of colors measures differences.
**Answer**: B

#### LO 5: Define the ordinal scale level as ordered categories without measurable differences and not used in calculations.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Ordinal Scale Level): Ordinal scale level has ordered categories where ______; cannot be used in calculations.
**Answer**: differences between values are not measurable

#### LO 6: Compare ordinal and nominal scales with respect to order and measurability of differences.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Ordinal Scale Level): Which comparison is correct?
A. Nominal has order; ordinal has no order but measurable differences.
B. Both nominal and ordinal have measurable differences but no order.
C. Ordinal has order but differences are not measurable; nominal has no inherent order.
D. Ordinal has no order; nominal has order and measurable differences.
**Answer**: C

#### LO 7: Define the interval scale level as ordered data with measurable differences, no true zero, and meaningless ratio statements.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Interval Scale Level): Interval scale level is ordered with meaningful differences but has ______; ratio comparisons are not meaningful.
**Answer**: no true zero starting point

#### LO 8: Compare interval and ratio scales regarding the presence of a true zero and legitimacy of ratio comparisons.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Interval Scale Level): Which statement correctly contrasts interval and ratio scales?
A. Both have a true zero and allow meaningful ratio statements.
B. Interval has no true zero so “twice as much” is not meaningful; ratio has a true zero and allows meaningful ratios.
C. Interval has a true zero but does not allow calculations; ratio lacks a true zero.
D. Neither allows calculations, but interval allows ratios.
**Answer**: B

#### LO 9: Define the ratio scale level as ordered data with meaningful differences and a true zero that permits ratios and calculations.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Ratio Scale Level): Ratio scale level has ordered data with meaningful differences and ______; ratios and calculations are meaningful.
**Answer**: a true zero point

#### LO 10: Compare ratio and interval scales for the meaningfulness of statements like 'twice as much'.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Ratio Scale Level): For which scale is a statement like “twice as much” meaningful?
A. Nominal
B. Ordinal
C. Interval
D. Ratio
**Answer**: D

#### LO 11: Define frequency as the number of occurrences of a data value in a dataset.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Frequency): Frequency is ______; the sum of frequencies equals the total number of observations.
**Answer**: the number of times a data value occurs

#### LO 12: Calculate frequencies from data with a total equal to the number of observations.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Frequency): A dataset has categories A, B, C with frequencies 4, 3, and 3. What is the total number of observations?
A. 9
B. 10
C. 7
D. 4
**Answer**: B

#### LO 13: Define relative frequency as a value’s frequency divided by the total number of outcomes, expressible as a fraction, percent, or decimal.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Relative Frequency): Relative frequency is ______; expressible as a fraction, percent, or decimal.
**Answer**: the ratio of a value’s frequency to the total number of outcomes

#### LO 14: Calculate relative frequencies from a frequency table with sums approximately 1 when rounding occurs.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Relative Frequency): In a table with total 20 observations, a category appears 5 times. What is its relative frequency?
A. 0.25
B. 0.20
C. 4
D. 25
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: Interpret relative frequencies in context, including fraction, percent, and decimal forms.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Relative Frequency): A category has relative frequency 0.30. Which is NOT an equivalent way to express this?
A. 3/10
B. 30%
C. 0.30
D. 3%
**Answer**: D

#### LO 16: Define cumulative relative frequency as the accumulation of relative frequencies up to a given value.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Cumulative Relative Frequency): Cumulative relative frequency is ______; the final entry equals 1 (approximately when rounding occurs).
**Answer**: the accumulation of relative frequencies up to a given value

#### LO 17: Calculate cumulative relative frequencies by sequential addition of relative frequencies with a final entry of about 1.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Cumulative Relative Frequency): Relative frequencies for ordered values are 0.10, 0.25, 0.15. What is the cumulative relative frequency up to the third value?
A. 0.40
B. 0.50
C. 0.25
D. 0.15
**Answer**: B

#### LO 18: Interpret cumulative relative frequencies for percentages below, between, or above specified values.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Cumulative Relative Frequency): A cumulative relative frequency of 0.72 at value x means:
A. About 72% of observations are at or below x.
B. About 72% of observations are above x.
C. Exactly 72% of observations equal x.
D. About 72% of observations are between x and the maximum.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 19: Construct a frequency table from raw data, including optional relative and cumulative relative frequency columns.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Frequency Table): Given raw data: 2, 3, 2, 1, 3. Which frequency listing is correct?
A. 1:2, 2:1, 3:2
B. 1:1, 2:2, 3:2
C. 1:1, 2:3, 3:1
D. 1:2, 2:2, 3:1
**Answer**: B

#### LO 20: Interpret a frequency table for counts, relative percentages, and cumulative percentages over values or intervals.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Frequency Table): A frequency table shows counts: value 1 → 2, value 2 → 1, value 3 → 1 (total 4). What percent of observations are at or below 2?
A. 25%
B. 50%
C. 75%
D. 100%
**Answer**: C

#### LO 21: State the rounding rule for final answers and intermediate results, including required decimal places.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Rounding Off Rules): According to the rounding rule, round only the final answer, carrying it ______; avoid rounding intermediate results.
**Answer**: one more decimal place than the original data

#### LO 22: Describe conditions for carrying extra decimal places in intermediate results.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Rounding Off Rules): If you must round intermediate results, how many decimal places should you carry?
A. Equal to the final answer’s decimal places.
B. The same as in the original data.
C. One more than the original data.
D. At least twice as many as in the final answer.
**Answer**: D

#### LO 23: Use the rounding rules when computing averages, relative frequencies, and cumulative relative frequencies.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Rounding Off Rules): Original data have 2 decimal places. Which approach follows the rounding rules when computing an average?
A. Round each intermediate sum to 2 decimals; round the final average to 2 decimals.
B. Avoid rounding intermediate results; round the final average to one more decimal place than the original data.
C. Round each data point to 3 decimals before computing; round the final average to 2 decimals.
D. Round intermediate steps to 1 decimal; round the final average to 1 decimal.
**Answer**: B


---
## Section: seg_11

#### LO 1: explain how randomized experiments, using random assignment and appropriate controls, can isolate the explanatory variable and establish cause-and-effect
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Randomized Experiment): Which feature of a randomized experiment most directly isolates the explanatory variable to support cause-and-effect?
A. Random assignment that spreads lurking variables equally and isolates the explanatory variable.
B. Measuring the response variable without defining treatments.
C. Using a placebo instead of any active treatments.
D. Allowing only researchers to be blinded.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 2: define the explanatory variable as the variable manipulated or used to explain change in the response variable
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Explanatory Variable): An explanatory variable is the ______.
**Answer**: variable that causes change in another variable

#### LO 3: define the response variable as the outcome measured for experimental units that is affected by the explanatory variable
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Response Variable): The response variable is the ______.
**Answer**: variable that is affected by changes in the explanatory variable

#### LO 4: define treatments as the different values or conditions of the explanatory variable applied to experimental units
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Treatments): In an experiment, treatments are the ______.
**Answer**: different values of the explanatory variable

#### LO 5: define an experimental unit as a single object or individual being measured in an experiment
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Experimental Unit): An experimental unit is a ______.
**Answer**: single object or individual to be measured

#### LO 6: explain how lurking variables can bias comparisons and how random assignment helps distribute them equally across treatment groups
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Lurking Variables): How do random assignment and lurking variables relate in an experiment?
A. Random assignment spreads lurking variables equally across groups, reducing bias in comparisons.
B. Lurking variables define the treatments being compared.
C. Random assignment measures lurking variables directly.
D. Lurking variables only affect the control group.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: define random assignment as randomly allocating experimental units to treatment groups to balance lurking variables
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Random Assignment): Random assignment means ______.
**Answer**: assigning experimental units to treatment groups randomly

#### LO 8: explain how a control group with a placebo helps separate the effect of the active treatment from expectation effects
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Control Group): What is the main purpose of including a control group that receives a placebo?
A. To balance the effects of being in an experiment with the effects of the active treatments.
B. To eliminate the need for random assignment.
C. To ensure no one receives any treatment at all.
D. To define the response variable.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: define a placebo as a treatment that cannot influence the response variable
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Placebo): A placebo is a ______.
**Answer**: treatment that cannot influence the response variable

#### LO 10: define blinding as keeping participants or researchers unaware of who receives active treatments or a placebo
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Blinding): Blinding is the condition in which a person involved in the study ______.
**Answer**: does not know who is receiving active treatments or a placebo

#### LO 11: describe how double-blind procedures control expectation effects for both participants and researchers
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Double-Blind Experiment): How do double-blind procedures control expectation effects?
A. Both subjects and researchers interacting with them are blinded, reducing expectation biases on measurement.
B. Only subjects are blinded; researchers control expectations by setting treatments.
C. Only researchers are blinded; subjects control expectations by self-reporting.
D. Neither subjects nor researchers are blinded; the placebo alone controls expectations.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 12: define an Institutional Review Board (IRB) as an oversight committee that must approve human-subject studies in advance to ensure participant safety
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Institutional Review Board (IRB)): An Institutional Review Board (IRB) is an ______.
**Answer**: oversight committee established by research institutions to ensure the safety of human subjects

#### LO 13: define risk minimization as the requirement that risks to participants be minimized and reasonable relative to projected benefits
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Risk Minimization): Risk minimization requires that ______.
**Answer**: risks to participants be minimized and reasonable relative to projected benefits

#### LO 14: describe the required elements of informed consent, including clear communication of risks, written consent, and maintained documentation
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Informed Consent): Which set best describes required elements of informed consent?
A. Clear explanation of risks, written consent, and kept documentation.
B. Random assignment, a control group, and blinding.
C. Approval by the IRB alone before data collection.
D. Privacy protection only, without any written consent.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: explain practical challenges in protecting privacy, such as whether removing names is sufficient and the possibility of re-identification
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Privacy Protection): Which statement best reflects a practical challenge in privacy protection?
A. Removing names may not be sufficient because re-identification is possible.
B. Privacy is guaranteed if a placebo is used.
C. Privacy protection requires random assignment.
D. Blinding eliminates all privacy concerns.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 16: identify forms of data falsification such as creating or altering data, changing instruments without reporting, or misrepresenting the number of subjects
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Falsifying Data): Which action is an example of falsifying data?
A. Changing measuring instruments without reporting.
B. Randomly assigning experimental units to treatment groups.
C. Obtaining written consent from participants.
D. Guarding collected data carefully.
**Answer**: A


---
## Section: seg_13

#### LO 1: Demonstrate the systematic sampling technique by selecting a random starting name and moving down four names repeatedly on the class list to gather the sample.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Systematic Sampling Technique): Which procedure correctly demonstrates the described systematic sampling technique?
A. Randomly select one starting name, then choose every 4th name on the list until the sample size is reached.
B. Start at the first name and choose the first names in order until the sample size is reached.
C. Randomly select names with replacement without using any fixed step.
D. Ask for volunteers until the sample size is reached.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 2: Construct relative frequency tables that include frequency, relative frequency, and cumulative relative frequency for the class movie-count data using specified categories.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Relative Frequency Table): To construct a relative frequency table for specified categories, what must be included for each category?
A. Only the frequency
B. Frequency and relative frequency, but not cumulative relative frequency
C. Frequency, relative frequency, and cumulative relative frequency
D. Only cumulative relative frequency
**Answer**: C

#### LO 3: Interpret relative frequency tables to determine the percent at most or more than a given movie count using the appropriate grouping.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Relative Frequency Table): When interpreting "at most" or "more than" from a table, recall that a Relative Frequency Table summarizes data categories with their ________.
**Answer**: frequency, relative frequency, and cumulative relative frequency

#### LO 4: Construct frequency counts for each movie-count category from the collected class data.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Frequency): When constructing frequency counts, what does the frequency for a category represent?
A. The percent of the dataset in a category
B. The number of data values in a category
C. The running total of relative frequencies up to a category
D. The number of categories used
**Answer**: B

#### LO 5: Interpret frequency counts to compare how often different movie-count categories occur.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Frequency): To compare how often categories occur using counts, remember that Frequency is ________.
**Answer**: the number of data values in a category

#### LO 6: Construct relative frequencies (percents) for each movie-count category from the class data.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Relative Frequency): To construct relative frequencies for each category, which description best matches what you should compute?
A. The percent of the dataset in the category
B. Only the raw count in the category
C. The accumulated percent up to and including the category
D. The number of categories used
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: Interpret relative frequencies as the percent of observations in each movie-count category to compare categories.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Relative Frequency): When comparing categories, Relative Frequency should be interpreted as ________.
**Answer**: the percent of the dataset in a category

#### LO 8: Construct cumulative relative frequencies across ordered movie-count categories.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Cumulative Relative Frequency): When constructing cumulative relative frequencies across ordered categories, each cumulative value should represent:
A. Only the category’s raw count
B. The running total of relative frequencies up to and including the category
C. The difference between successive relative frequencies
D. The relative frequency of the largest category only
**Answer**: B

#### LO 9: Interpret cumulative relative frequencies to find the percent of data at most a given number of movies.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Cumulative Relative Frequency): To find the percent of data at most a value, use Cumulative Relative Frequency, which is ________.
**Answer**: the running total of relative frequencies up to and including a category

#### LO 10: Construct data groupings using single-value categories and combined intervals (for example, 0–1, 2–3, 4–5, 6–7+) for tabulating the movie-count data.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Data Grouping): Which is a correct way to construct data groupings for tabulating movie-count data?
A. Use only one combined category for all values
B. Organize data into single values or combined intervals such as 0–1, 2–3, 4–5, 6–7+
C. Assign categories based on student names instead of counts
D. Change categories after counting to force equal frequencies
**Answer**: B

#### LO 11: Interpret how different data groupings affect frequencies and percents and why one grouping may be preferred for a given question.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Data Grouping): When evaluating why one grouping may be preferred, recall that Data Grouping is ________.
**Answer**: organizing data into different category groupings (such as single values or combined intervals) for tabulation and interpretation


---
## Section: seg_15

#### LO 1: Define simple random sampling as assigning labels to all population members and using a random number generator to select the sample so every member has an equal chance.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Simple Random Sampling): Simple random sampling is a straightforward method: assign numbers to all population members and use a _____ to select labels identifying the sample.
**Answer**: random number generator

#### LO 2: Explain the procedure for simple random sampling, including labeling the population and using a random number generator to select the required number of labels.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Simple Random Sampling): Which sequence best describes simple random sampling?
A. Assign numbers to all population members; use a random number generator to select the required labels.
B. Divide the population into strata; use simple random sampling within each stratum proportionately.
C. Compute k, choose a random start, and select every kth individual from the list.
D. Randomly select clusters and include all individuals within them.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: Demonstrate simple random sampling by executing a step-by-step selection of individuals from a numbered population list using a random number generator.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Simple Random Sampling): You have a population list numbered 1–100 and need a sample of 5. What is a valid next step for simple random sampling?
A. Use a random number generator to select five distinct labels between 1 and 100.
B. Choose a random start and select every 20th individual.
C. Split the list into strata and sample proportionately from each stratum.
D. Randomly choose two clusters and include everyone in them.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 4: Demonstrate systematic sampling by computing k = (population size)/(desired sample size), choosing a random start, and selecting every kth individual from a population list.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Systematic Sampling): For a population of 200 and desired sample size of 20, which plan demonstrates systematic sampling?
A. Randomly choose a start between 1 and 10, compute k = 10, then select every 10th individual on the list.
B. Assign numbers and use a random number generator to select 20 labels.
C. Divide the population into strata and sample proportionately from each.
D. Randomly select 20 clusters and include all members of those clusters.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: Explain how to list the population, choose a random starting point, calculate k, and select every kth individual, wrapping to the beginning if needed.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Systematic Sampling): Which description correctly explains systematic sampling, including what to do when the end of the list is reached?
A. List the population, compute k = N/n, choose a random start, then select every kth individual, wrapping to the beginning if needed.
B. List the population, choose the first person, then select every kth individual without a random start or wrap.
C. Divide the population into strata, choose a random start, and select every kth individual in each stratum.
D. Form clusters, select them randomly, and include all members of selected clusters.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: Demonstrate stratified sampling by dividing the population into strata and using simple random sampling to select a proportionate number from each stratum.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Stratified Sampling): Which plan demonstrates stratified sampling?
A. Divide the population into strata and use simple random sampling to select a proportionate number from each stratum.
B. Randomly select clusters and include all individuals in those clusters.
C. Assign numbers to all population members and randomly select labels across the entire population.
D. Choose a random start and select every kth individual from the population list.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: Explain how stratified sampling ensures adequate representation of subgroups and outline the steps to implement it.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Stratified Sampling): Stratified sampling ensures _____ by dividing the population into strata and using simple random sampling to select a proportionate number from each stratum.
**Answer**: adequate representation of subgroups

#### LO 8: Demonstrate cluster sampling by randomly selecting clusters from a defined set of clusters and including all individuals within the selected clusters.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Cluster Sampling): Which action correctly completes a cluster sampling plan after clusters have been selected by simple random sampling?
A. Include all individuals within the selected clusters in the sample.
B. Randomly select a few individuals from each selected cluster.
C. Select every kth individual across the whole population list.
D. Re-divide selected clusters into strata and sample proportionately.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: Explain the steps of cluster sampling, including defining clusters, using simple random sampling to select clusters, and sampling all members within chosen clusters.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Cluster Sampling): Which statement best describes the steps of cluster sampling?
A. Assign numbers to all population members and use a random number generator to pick labels.
B. Divide the population into clusters, use simple random sampling to select clusters, and include all individuals in selected clusters.
C. Divide the population into strata and sample proportionately from each stratum.
D. Compute k, choose a random start, and select every kth individual.
**Answer**: B

#### LO 10: Demonstrate random sampling by implementing a selection process that gives every population member an equal chance, such as using a random number generator.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Random Sampling): Which method demonstrates random sampling by giving every member an equal chance of selection?
A. Assign numbers to all population members and use a random number generator to select labels.
B. Choose every kth individual from a list starting at a random point.
C. Divide the population into strata and sample proportionately.
D. Select clusters randomly and include all their members.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: Explain the principle of random sampling and its role in producing representative samples.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Random Sampling): Random sampling is a method of selection that gives every member of the population an _____ of being chosen.
**Answer**: equal chance

#### LO 12: Describe the population under study by specifying the individuals, objects, or measurements whose properties are being examined.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Population): The population is all _____ whose properties are being studied.
**Answer**: individuals, objects, or measurements

#### LO 13: Define a sample as a subset of the population used to draw conclusions about the population.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sample): A sample is a _____ of the population studied.
**Answer**: subset

#### LO 14: Construct a valid sample from a defined population using an appropriate sampling method and briefly document the selection procedure.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sample): Which choice constructs a valid sample from a defined population and documents the selection procedure?
A. From a numbered population list, use a random number generator to select the required labels and record the steps used.
B. Include all population members to avoid selection procedures.
C. Choose a convenient group without numbering or recording how they were chosen.
D. Select every kth person but do not state the starting point or k.
**Answer**: A


---
## Section: seg_19

#### LO 1: Construct a stem-and-leaf graph for a small data set by dividing each observation into a stem and a leaf (final significant digit), listing stems vertically from smallest to largest, and ordering leaves for each stem.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Stem-And-Leaf Graph (Stemplot)): Which sequence of steps correctly constructs a stem-and-leaf graph for a small data set?
A. Divide each observation into a stem and a leaf (final significant digit), list stems vertically from smallest to largest, place leaves in increasing order next to stems.
B. Group data into equal-width intervals, draw connected line segments, and label axes.
C. Draw separated bars for categories, then sort their heights.
D. Round all observations to tens, list stems horizontally, and place leaves in random order.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 2: Identify the stem and leaf for a given numerical observation, including decimals, in the context of a stem-and-leaf graph.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Stem-And-Leaf Graph (Stemplot)): In a stemplot, what are the stem and leaf for 12.7?
A. Stem 12, leaf 7
B. Stem 1, leaf 27
C. Stem 127, leaf 0
D. Stem 12.7, leaf (none)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: Interpret a stem-and-leaf graph to describe concentrations of values and note any observations that appear to be outliers.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Stem-And-Leaf Graph (Stemplot)): Given the stemplot: 1 | 2 3 3 4; 2 | 2 2 3 9; 3 | 0 1; 8 | 9. Which statement best describes the data?
A. Values concentrate around stems 1–2, with a possible outlier near 89.
B. Values are evenly spread with no outliers.
C. Most values cluster in the 80s, with a low outlier near 12.
D. There are two equal clusters at stems 3 and 8.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 4: Define a stem as the part of a number that precedes the final significant digit in a stemplot.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Stem): In a stemplot, the stem is the portion of each observation that ____.
**Answer**: precedes the final significant digit

#### LO 5: Construct the stem column by listing stems in a vertical line from smallest to largest for a given data set.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Stem): For the data 12, 19, 23, 21, which list correctly gives the stems from smallest to largest?
A. 1, 2
B. 12, 19, 23, 21
C. 2, 1, 2
D. 1 only
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: Define a leaf as the final significant digit of an observation in a stemplot.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Leaf): In a stemplot, the leaf is the ____ of each observation.
**Answer**: final significant digit

#### LO 7: Construct the leaves for each stem by placing the final digits in increasing order next to their corresponding stem.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Leaf): For stem 4 and observations 41, 44, and 47, how should the leaves be listed?
A. 1 4 7
B. 7 4 1
C. 44 47 41
D. 1 7 4
**Answer**: A

#### LO 8: Define a side-by-side stem-and-leaf plot as two sets of leaves sharing the same stems with leaves displayed on both sides.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Side-By-Side Stem-And-Leaf Plot): A side-by-side stem-and-leaf plot is a comparative stemplot in which two data sets ____.
**Answer**: share the same stems

#### LO 9: Compare two data sets using a side-by-side stem-and-leaf plot by examining differences in how leaves cluster around shared stems.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Side-By-Side Stem-And-Leaf Plot): Shared stems: 2 and 3. Set A (left leaves): 2|1 2 and 3|0 2. Set B (right leaves): 2|8 9 and 3|1 1 4. Which comparison is supported?
A. Set B tends to have larger values than Set A at the same stems.
B. Both sets show identical distributions.
C. Set A tends to have larger values than Set B.
D. Neither set shows any clustering of leaves.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 10: Define an outlier as an observation that does not fit the pattern of the rest of the data and may indicate a mistake or an unusual event.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Outlier): An outlier is an observation that ____.
**Answer**: does not fit the rest of the data

#### LO 11: Define a line graph as a display with data values on the x-axis, frequencies on the y-axis, and frequency points connected by line segments.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Line Graph): In a line graph, the points are connected by ____.
**Answer**: line segments

#### LO 12: Construct a line graph from a frequency table by plotting data values on the x-axis, frequencies on the y-axis, and connecting the points with line segments.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Line Graph): Which procedure constructs a line graph from a frequency table?
A. Plot data values on the x-axis, frequencies on the y-axis, and connect the points with line segments.
B. Draw separated bars for each category and leave gaps between bars.
C. Divide observations into stems and leaves and order the leaves.
D. Use shared stems and place leaves on both sides of the stem.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 13: Describe the frequency pattern displayed by a line graph for specific data values.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Line Graph): A line graph shows frequencies for values 1, 2, 3 as 2, 5, 2. Which description fits the pattern?
A. The frequency peaks at the data value 2.
B. The frequency is constant across values.
C. The highest frequency is at the data value 3.
D. Frequencies decrease steadily as values increase.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 14: Construct a bar graph with separated vertical or horizontal bars to display counts or proportions for categories.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Bar Graph): Which is a defining feature when constructing a bar graph?
A. Bars are separated and may be vertical or horizontal.
B. Points are connected by line segments.
C. Leaves are ordered next to stems.
D. Shared stems display leaves on both sides.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: Describe how bar height and orientation represent counts or proportions for categories in a bar graph.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Bar Graph): In a bar graph, what does bar height represent, and how may bars be oriented?
A. Height shows counts or proportions; bars may be vertical or horizontal.
B. Height shows the data values; bars must be vertical only.
C. Height shows time; bars are connected.
D. Height shows frequencies; bars must be 3D boxes only.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 16: Define a bar graph as a display of separated bars that may be rectangular or rectangular boxes (in three-dimensional plots) and oriented vertically or horizontally.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Bar Graph): A bar graph is a display of ____ that may be rectangular and oriented vertically or horizontally.
**Answer**: separated bars


---
## Section: seg_21

#### LO 1: Define a histogram as a graph of contiguous boxes with the horizontal axis labeled by what the data represent and the vertical axis labeled frequency or relative frequency, used to display large data sets and show shape, center, and spread.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Histogram): A histogram is a graph of _____ boxes with the horizontal axis labeled by what the data represent and the vertical axis labeled frequency or relative frequency.
**Answer**: contiguous boxes

#### LO 2: Construct a histogram by choosing an appropriate number of classes, selecting a starting point with additional decimal precision, determining class width, setting boundaries, and plotting frequencies or relative frequencies with left-inclusive, right-exclusive class intervals.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Histogram): Which procedure correctly constructs a histogram per the stated guidelines?
A. Choose 5–15 classes; pick a starting point less than the minimum with one more decimal place; compute class width as (ending − starting)/#bars; use left-inclusive, right-exclusive boundaries; plot frequencies or relative frequencies.
B. Choose any number of classes; set the starting point equal to the minimum; compute width as (ending + starting)/2; include values on both boundaries; plot only cumulative frequencies.
C. Use 2–3 classes; choose a starting point greater than the maximum; pick an arbitrary width; use right-inclusive boundaries; plot proportions only.
D. Set classes equal to the square of the sample size; keep the same decimal precision as the data; compute width as (ending − starting) × #bars; plot medians.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: Define frequency as the number of times an answer occurs.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Frequency): Frequency is the _____ .
**Answer**: number of times an answer occurs

#### LO 4: Calculate frequency counts by tallying how many times each observed value occurs in the data.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Frequency): To calculate frequency counts for a data set, you should:
A. Tally how many times each observed value occurs.
B. Divide each observed value by the total sample size.
C. Measure class widths for each interval.
D. Plot points in chronological order.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: Define relative frequency as the ratio of a value’s frequency to the total number of data values (RF = f/n).
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Relative Frequency): Relative frequency is the _____ divided by the total number of data values in the sample (RF = f/n).
**Answer**: frequency for an observed value

#### LO 6: Calculate relative frequency for each observed value by dividing its frequency by the total sample size and, if desired, express it as a percentage.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Relative Frequency): How do you compute the relative frequency for a category?
A. Divide its frequency by the total sample size; optionally express as a percentage.
B. Subtract the starting point from the ending value.
C. Count how many times the value occurs.
D. Multiply its frequency by the number of classes.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: Define a class interval (class) as one of the bars or grouped intervals that represent ranges of data values in a histogram.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Class Interval (Class)): In a histogram, a class interval (class) is one of the _____ that represent grouped data.
**Answer**: bars or intervals

#### LO 8: Calculate class intervals by applying the chosen starting point and class width across the data range to establish contiguous boundaries.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Class Interval (Class)): To calculate class intervals for a histogram, you should:
A. Apply the chosen starting point and class width across the data range to establish contiguous boundaries.
B. Choose arbitrary midpoints regardless of the starting point and width.
C. Use relative frequencies themselves as boundaries.
D. Set boundaries only at observed data values.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: Define class width as the quantity (ending value − starting point) divided by the number of bars.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Class Width): Class width is computed as (_____ ) divided by the number of bars.
**Answer**: ending value − starting point

#### LO 10: Calculate class width from the starting point, ending value, and chosen number of classes, rounding appropriately to avoid boundary issues.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Class Width): How should you calculate class width?
A. Use (ending value − starting point)/number of classes and round to avoid boundary issues.
B. Use the square root of the sample size.
C. Divide total frequency by the number of classes.
D. Add starting and ending values, then divide by two.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: Define the starting point as a first class boundary chosen to be less than the smallest data value and carried to one more decimal place than the most precise data value.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Starting Point (Histogram)): The starting point is a first class boundary chosen to be _____ and carried to one more decimal place than the most precise data value.
**Answer**: less than the smallest data value

#### LO 12: Explain how choosing a starting point with additional decimal precision less than the minimum helps prevent data values from falling on class boundaries.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Starting Point (Histogram)): Why choose a starting point less than the minimum with extra decimal precision?
A. It prevents data values from falling on class boundaries.
B. It reduces the number of classes to fewer than five.
C. It ensures boundaries are right-inclusive.
D. It makes relative frequencies sum to more than one.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 13: Define the class boundaries convention as carrying boundaries to one additional decimal place and counting values on the left boundary but not on the right boundary.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Class Boundaries Convention): The class boundaries convention is to carry boundaries to _____ and count a value in a class if it falls on the left boundary but not if it falls on the right boundary.
**Answer**: one additional decimal place

#### LO 14: Use the left-inclusive, right-exclusive boundary convention with extra decimal precision when assigning data to histogram classes.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Class Boundaries Convention): Using left-inclusive, right-exclusive classes [10.0, 12.0) and [12.0, 14.0), which class contains 12.0?
A. [10.0, 12.0)
B. [12.0, 14.0)
C. Both classes
D. Neither class
**Answer**: B

#### LO 15: Calculate an appropriate number of classes using the square-root-of-n guideline with rounding and by ensuring the choice falls within a clear range such as five to 15 bars.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Number Of Classes): How should you choose the number of classes for a histogram?
A. Take the square root of the number of data values, round, and ensure it is between five and 15.
B. Always use exactly 20 classes regardless of sample size.
C. Match the number of classes to the number of distinct values.
D. Use one class per observation.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 16: State that many histograms use five to 15 classes and that a guideline for choosing the number of intervals is the square root of the number of data values, rounded.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Number Of Classes): Many histograms use _____ classes, and a guideline is to choose the square root of the number of data values, rounded.
**Answer**: five to 15

#### LO 17: Classify data as continuous when values are obtained by measurement.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Continuous Data): Continuous data are _____ .
**Answer**: data obtained by measurement

#### LO 18: Differentiate continuous data from discrete data by noting that continuous values come from measurement whereas discrete values come from counting.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Continuous Data): Which statement correctly differentiates continuous from discrete data?
A. Continuous values come from measurement whereas discrete values come from counting.
B. Continuous values come only from counting.
C. Discrete values are measurements like height.
D. Both types arise only from surveys.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 19: Define discrete data as data obtained by counting.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Discrete Data): Discrete data are _____ .
**Answer**: data obtained by counting

#### LO 20: Calculate class boundaries for integer data by subtracting 0.5 from the smallest value and adding 0.5 to the largest value, and choose a width that centers integers within intervals.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Discrete Data Class Boundaries): How should class boundaries be set for integer (count) data?
A. Subtract 0.5 from the smallest value and add 0.5 to the largest; choose a width that centers integers within intervals.
B. Add 1 to the smallest value and subtract 1 from the largest; choose the narrowest possible width.
C. Use the integers themselves as class boundaries with no adjustments.
D. Make all classes right-inclusive only and ignore centering.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 21: Construct a frequency polygon by choosing class intervals, plotting the data points, and connecting them with line segments, adding end intervals as needed to touch the x-axis.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Frequency Polygon): Which steps construct a frequency polygon?
A. Choose class intervals, plot the data points, connect them with line segments, and add end intervals as needed to touch the x-axis.
B. Draw contiguous boxes for each class and label axes by frequency.
C. Plot time on the horizontal axis and connect points in chronological order.
D. Plot cumulative frequencies only and leave points unconnected.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 22: Compare distributions by overlaying multiple frequency polygons on the same axes to assess differences in shape and center.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Frequency Polygon): How can you compare distributions using frequency polygons?
A. Overlay multiple frequency polygons on the same axes to assess differences in shape and center.
B. Overlay histograms with different class boundaries on separate axes.
C. Compare only total counts from each distribution.
D. Use pie charts to compare proportions across categories.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 23: Construct a time series graph by plotting time on the horizontal axis, the measured values on the vertical axis, and connecting points in chronological order.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Time Series Graph): How do you construct a time series graph?
A. Plot time on the horizontal axis, measured values on the vertical axis, and connect points in chronological order.
B. Plot measured values on the horizontal axis and time on the vertical axis without connecting points.
C. Group values into classes and draw contiguous boxes.
D. Plot class midpoints and connect them with line segments without regard to time.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 24: Define a time series graph as a display that recognizes chronological ordering by plotting time on the x-axis and measured values on the y-axis, with points connected in order.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Time Series Graph): A time series graph recognizes chronological ordering by plotting _____ on the x-axis and measured values on the y-axis, with points connected in order.
**Answer**: time

#### LO 25: Describe the use of time series graphs for making trends in data recorded over time easy to spot.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Uses Of A Time Series Graph): Time series graphs are important for data recorded over time because the graphical display makes _____ easy to spot.
**Answer**: trends

#### LO 26: Identify situations where time series graphs are appropriate, such as tracking monthly or daily measurements over an extended period.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Uses Of A Time Series Graph): Which situation is appropriate for a time series graph?
A. Tracking monthly or daily measurements over an extended period.
B. Counting categories without any inherent ordering.
C. Displaying a single summary number from a sample.
D. Assigning values to left-inclusive histogram bins.
**Answer**: A


---
## Section: seg_23

#### LO 1: define measures of location as quartiles and percentiles that describe the position of data in an ordered set
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Measures Of Location): Measures of location are common measures that describe data position, specifically _____.
**Answer**: quartiles and percentiles

#### LO 2: calculate quartiles and percentiles for ordered data sets to summarize data position
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Measures Of Location): For the ordered data [2, 4, 6, 8, 10], what are Q1 and the 80th percentile?
A. (2, 10)
B. (3, 8)
C. (4, 8)
D. (3, 10)
**Answer**: B

#### LO 3: interpret measures of location to describe relative standing within a data set and compare values across contexts
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Measures Of Location): In one class, Alex’s test score is at Q3; in another class, Blair’s is at Q1. Which statement best compares their relative standings?
A. Alex ranks higher relative to classmates than Blair.
B. Blair ranks higher than Alex in both classes.
C. Both have the same relative standing.
D. Alex’s standing cannot be compared to Blair’s using quartiles.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 4: define percentiles as values that divide ordered data into hundredths, with the pth percentile having at most p percent of observations at or below it
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Percentiles): Percentiles are values that _____; the pth percentile has at most p% of data at or below it.
**Answer**: divide ordered data into hundredths

#### LO 5: calculate specified percentiles from ordered or tabulated data using appropriate indexing procedures
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Percentiles): For the ordered data [3, 5, 7, 9, 11], the 60th percentile is:
A. 5
B. 7
C. 9
D. 8
**Answer**: B

#### LO 6: interpret a given percentile as indicating the percentage of observations at or below a value and the percentage above it in context
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Percentiles): A score at the 90th percentile means:
A. About 90% of observations are at or below the score and about 10% are above.
B. About 10% are at or below the score and about 90% are above.
C. The score is 90 points above average.
D. Exactly 90% of observations equal the score.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: define quartiles as special percentiles that divide ordered data into four equal parts (Q1, median, Q3)
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Quartiles): Quartiles are special percentiles that divide ordered data into _____.
**Answer**: quarters

#### LO 8: calculate Q1, the median, and Q3 from an ordered data set by locating the medians of the lower and upper halves
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Quartiles): For the ordered data [1, 2, 4, 7, 9, 10, 12], what are (Q1, Median, Q3)?
A. (2, 7, 10)
B. (1, 7, 12)
C. (2, 6, 10)
D. (3, 7, 11)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: interpret quartiles as markers of the proportions of data at or below and above these values in an ordered set
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Quartiles): If Q1 = 20 in an ordered data set, which is true?
A. About 25% of observations are at or below 20.
B. About 50% of observations are at or below 20.
C. About 75% of observations are at or below 20.
D. Exactly 25% of observations equal 20.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 10: define the first quartile (Q1) as the 25th percentile and the median of the lower half of ordered data
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: First Quartile (Q1)): The first quartile (Q1) is the _____ and the median of the lower half of ordered data.
**Answer**: 25th percentile

#### LO 11: calculate Q1 for an ordered data set by identifying the median of the lower half
**Multiple Choice** (concept: First Quartile (Q1)): For the ordered data [4, 5, 9, 13, 15, 18, 21, 30], Q1 is:
A. 5
B. 7
C. 9
D. 8
**Answer**: B

#### LO 12: define the median as the 50th percentile that splits ordered data into two halves and may not be an observed value
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Median (Second Quartile, 50th Percentile)): The median is the _____ that splits ordered data into two halves and may not be an observed value.
**Answer**: 50th percentile

#### LO 13: calculate the median for data sets of even or odd size by ordering values and locating the middle position
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Median (Second Quartile, 50th Percentile)): What is the median of the ordered data [2, 3, 7, 8]?
A. 3
B. 5
C. 7
D. 4
**Answer**: B

#### LO 14: define the third quartile (Q3) as the 75th percentile and the median of the upper half of ordered data
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Third Quartile (Q3)): The third quartile (Q3) is the _____ and the median of the upper half of ordered data.
**Answer**: 75th percentile

#### LO 15: calculate Q3 for an ordered data set by identifying the median of the upper half
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Third Quartile (Q3)): For the ordered data [1, 2, 5, 7, 9, 11, 13, 15, 18], Q3 is:
A. 11
B. 13
C. 15
D. 14
**Answer**: B

#### LO 16: calculate the interquartile range as IQR = Q3 − Q1 to measure the spread of the middle 50 percent of data
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Interquartile Range (IQR)): If Q1 = 12 and Q3 = 20, what is the IQR?
A. 8
B. 32
C. 12
D. 10
**Answer**: A

#### LO 17: compare IQRs across groups to judge which data set shows greater variability in the middle half of the distribution
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Interquartile Range (IQR)): Group A has IQR = 4 and Group B has IQR = 7. Which group shows greater middle-50% variability?
A. Group A
B. Group B
C. Both are equal
D. Cannot be determined without means
**Answer**: B

#### LO 18: detect potential outliers using the rule based on Q1 − 1.5(IQR) and Q3 + 1.5(IQR)
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Interquartile Range (IQR)): With Q1 = 10, Q3 = 22, and IQR = 12, which value is suspected as a potential outlier?
A. −5
B. 5
C. 35
D. 41
**Answer**: D

#### LO 19: define a potential outlier as a data point notably different from the rest that warrants further investigation
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Potential Outlier): A potential outlier is a data point notably different from others that _____.
**Answer**: requires further investigation

#### LO 20: calculate lower and upper fences using Q1 − 1.5(IQR) and Q3 + 1.5(IQR) to flag suspected outliers
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Potential Outlier): If Q1 = 12, Q3 = 20, and IQR = 8, what are the lower and upper fences?
A. (4, 28)
B. (0, 32)
C. (−4, 36)
D. (2, 30)
**Answer**: B

#### LO 21: define the five-number summary as the minimum, Q1, median, Q3, and maximum of a data set
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Five-Number Summary): The five-number summary is the set of _____.
**Answer**: Minimum, Q1, Median, Q3, and Maximum

#### LO 22: calculate the five-number summary for an ordered data set
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Five-Number Summary): For the ordered data [1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 11, 13, 15], what is the five-number summary (Min, Q1, Median, Q3, Max)?
A. (1, 3, 8, 13, 15)
B. (1, 4, 8, 12, 15)
C. (1, 5, 8, 11, 15)
D. (1, 4, 7, 12, 15)
**Answer**: B

#### LO 23: interpret the five-number summary to describe a distribution’s center and spread
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Five-Number Summary): A data set has five-number summary (10, 20, 30, 50, 100). Which statement is supported?
A. Half of the data lie between 20 and 50.
B. The mean is 30.
C. Exactly 50% of values equal 30.
D. No values exceed 50.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 24: state the kth percentile index formula i = (k/100)(n + 1) and its integer versus non-integer cases
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Kth Percentile Index Formula): The kth percentile index formula is _____; use the ith value if i is an integer, otherwise average the surrounding values.
**Answer**: i = (k/100)(n + 1)

#### LO 25: calculate a kth percentile by computing i = (k/100)(n + 1) and, if needed, averaging the surrounding ordered values
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Kth Percentile Index Formula): For ordered data [10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60, 70, 80] and k = 25, use i = (k/100)(n + 1). What is the 25th percentile?
A. 20
B. 25
C. 22.5
D. 30
**Answer**: B

#### LO 26: explain how the index i = (k/100)(n + 1) locates a percentile’s position and when averaging of adjacent data values is required
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Kth Percentile Index Formula): Which statement best explains the role of i = (k/100)(n + 1) in locating a percentile?
A. It gives a position in the ordered data; if i is not an integer, average the adjacent ordered values.
B. It gives a value to add to each data point to reach the percentile.
C. It divides data into hundredths regardless of order.
D. It requires weighting by the decimal part of i rather than averaging.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 27: calculate the percentile rank of a value using ((x + 0.5y)/n) × 100 and round to the nearest integer
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Percentile Rank Formula): In a data set of n = 20, a value has x = 12 observations below it and y = 2 equal to it. What is its percentile rank?
A. 60
B. 65
C. 70
D. 75
**Answer**: B

#### LO 28: interpret a computed percentile rank as the proportion of observations at or below a given value within the data set
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Percentile Rank Formula): A percentile rank of 65 means:
A. About 65% of observations are at or below the value.
B. The value is 65 points above the average.
C. 65% of observations equal the value.
D. Exactly 35 observations are above the value.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 29: interpret percentiles as indicators of relative standing, recognizing that whether a percentile is good or bad depends on context
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Interpreting Percentiles): Which statement correctly interprets percentiles?
A. Higher percentiles are always better.
B. Percentiles indicate relative standing; whether they are “good” or “bad” depends on context.
C. Percentiles measure absolute performance.
D. Low percentiles always mean poor performance.
**Answer**: B

#### LO 30: define the meaning of a percentile as the percentage of data at or below a value rather than an absolute performance measure
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Interpreting Percentiles): A percentile represents the _____, not an absolute performance measure.
**Answer**: percentage of data at or below a value

#### LO 31: list the essential elements of a percentile interpretation: context, the data value, percent below, and percent above
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Percentile Interpretation Elements): A complete percentile interpretation should include context, the data value, and the _____.
**Answer**: percent below and percent above

#### LO 32: interpret a percentile by explicitly stating the context, the value, and the percentages below and above it
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Percentile Interpretation Elements): Which sentence correctly interprets a 80th percentile with all required elements?
A. The score is high.
B. In a class, Pat’s score is at the 80th percentile, meaning about 80% of scores are at or below Pat’s and about 20% are above.
C. Pat’s score is better than average by 80 points.
D. In a class, Pat’s score is at the top.
**Answer**: B
