## Section: seg_25

#### LO 1: construct a box plot on a scaled number line from the minimum, first quartile, median, third quartile, and maximum
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Box Plot (Box-And-Whisker Plot)): When constructing a box plot from a five-number summary, which description is correct?
A. The box spans Q1 to Q3 with a median line inside; whiskers extend to the minimum and maximum.
B. The box spans the minimum to maximum; whiskers extend to Q1 and Q3.
C. The box spans Q1 to the maximum; whiskers extend to the quartiles.
D. The box spans the median to Q3; whiskers extend only across the interquartile range.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 2: interpret a box plot to describe data concentration, quartile spreads, and the distance of extreme values from most of the data
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Box Plot (Box-And-Whisker Plot)): Which interpretation aligns with a box plot’s features?
A. The box shows the middle 50% where data are concentrated; longer whiskers indicate extreme values farther from most data.
B. The whiskers show the middle 50%; the box shows only outliers.
C. The median line indicates the mean, and whisker length measures sample size.
D. Equal box widths mean all data values are equal.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: list the five values used to construct a box plot: minimum, first quartile, median, third quartile, and maximum
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Five Values For Box Plots): Fill in the blank: A box plot is constructed from the five values: ______.
**Answer**: the minimum value, first quartile, median (second quartile), third quartile, and maximum value

#### LO 4: calculate the minimum, first quartile, median, third quartile, and maximum for a given dataset, for example using a graphing calculator
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Five Values For Box Plots): Which could be a valid set of five values to construct a box plot for some dataset?
A. min=2, Q1=5, median=7, Q3=10, max=14
B. min=2, Q1=10, median=7, Q3=5, max=14
C. min=2, mean=7, Q3=10, max=14, mode=5
D. min=2, Q1=5, median=7, Q3=10, max=9
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: describe how the five values determine the edges of the box, the whiskers, and the median line in a box plot
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Five Values For Box Plots): Which statement correctly describes how the five values define a box plot?
A. The box extends from Q1 to Q3, the median is shown inside the box, and whiskers reach the minimum and maximum.
B. The box extends from the minimum to maximum; whiskers mark Q1 and Q3.
C. The median defines the box edges; whiskers mark the quartiles.
D. The whiskers show the interquartile range; the box shows outliers.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: identify the minimum value in a dataset and locate it on a box plot
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Minimum Value): On a horizontal box plot, where is the minimum value located?
A. At the endpoint of the lower whisker
B. At the left edge of the box (Q1)
C. At the median line inside the box
D. At the endpoint of the upper whisker
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: use the minimum value to set the lower axis endpoint and the lower whisker limit when constructing a box plot
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Minimum Value): When constructing a box plot, how is the minimum value used?
A. It sets the lower axis endpoint and the lower whisker limit.
B. It sets the left edge of the box and the median line.
C. It sets the upper whisker limit and right edge of the box.
D. It sets the center of the box and the scale tick spacing.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 8: describe the role of the minimum value in positioning the lower whisker and scaling the axis
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Minimum Value): Fill in the blank: In a box plot, the minimum value is the ______ used as an axis endpoint and lower whisker limit.
**Answer**: smallest data value

#### LO 9: define the maximum value as the largest data value used as an axis endpoint and upper whisker limit in a box plot
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Maximum Value): Fill in the blank: In a box plot, the maximum value is the ______ used as an axis endpoint and upper whisker limit.
**Answer**: largest data value

#### LO 10: use the maximum value to set the upper axis endpoint and the upper whisker limit when constructing a box plot
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Maximum Value): When drawing a box plot, the maximum value is used to:
A. Set the upper axis endpoint and the upper whisker limit
B. Set the lower axis endpoint and the left edge of the box
C. Determine the median line location and IQR
D. Set the left edge of the box and mark outliers
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: define the first quartile (Q1) as the value marking one end of the box, with approximately 25% of the data between the minimum and Q1
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: First Quartile (Q1)): Fill in the blank: The first quartile (Q1) marks one end of the box; approximately ______ of the data lie between the minimum and Q1.
**Answer**: 25%

#### LO 12: use the first quartile to locate the left edge of the box and to help determine the interquartile range
**Multiple Choice** (concept: First Quartile (Q1)): Which use of Q1 is correct when working with a box plot?
A. Q1 locates the left edge of the box and helps determine the interquartile range as Q3 − Q1.
B. Q1 locates the median line and sets the lower whisker endpoint.
C. Q1 sets the right edge of the box and defines outliers.
D. Q1 is the smallest data value and sets the axis origin.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 13: define the median (Q2) as the second quartile shown inside the box that may coincide with the first or third quartile
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Median (Q2)): Fill in the blank: The median (Q2) is the ______ shown inside the box that may lie between or coincide with the first and third quartiles.
**Answer**: second quartile

#### LO 14: compute the median for a given dataset
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Median (Q2)): For the dataset {3, 4, 5, 7, 9}, what is the median (Q2)?
A. 4
B. 5
C. 6
D. 7
**Answer**: B

#### LO 15: calculate the third quartile (Q3) for a given dataset
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Third Quartile (Q3)): A box plot has a box from 8 to 14 and whiskers from 2 to 20. What is the third quartile (Q3)?
A. 8
B. 14
C. 2
D. 20
**Answer**: B

#### LO 16: identify the third quartile on a box plot as the right edge of the box
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Third Quartile (Q3)): On a box plot, the right edge of the box corresponds to:
A. The third quartile (Q3)
B. The first quartile (Q1)
C. The median (Q2)
D. The maximum value
**Answer**: A

#### LO 17: interpret the third quartile as a value above which approximately 25% of the data lie
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Third Quartile (Q3)): Fill in the blank: The third quartile (Q3) is a value above which approximately ______ of the data lie.
**Answer**: 25%

#### LO 18: define whiskers as line segments extending from the ends of the box to the smallest and largest data values, unless outliers are marked with dots
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Whiskers): Fill in the blank: In a box plot, whiskers are ______.
**Answer**: line segments extending from the ends of the box to the smallest and largest data values

#### LO 19: describe how whiskers indicate the distance to the extreme values and how they change when outliers are plotted
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Whiskers): Which statement about whiskers is correct?
A. Whiskers indicate the distance to the extreme values; when outliers are marked with dots, whiskers do not extend to the minimum and maximum.
B. Whiskers always extend to Q1 and Q3 regardless of outliers.
C. Longer whiskers always mean a larger sample size.
D. Marking outliers with dots lengthens the whiskers beyond the maximum.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 20: define the interquartile range (IQR) as Q3 − Q1, representing the spread of the middle 50% of the data
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Interquartile Range (IQR)): Fill in the blank: The interquartile range (IQR) is ______, representing the spread of the middle 50% of the data.
**Answer**: Q3 − Q1

#### LO 21: calculate the interquartile range from given Q1 and Q3 values
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Interquartile Range (IQR)): If Q1 = 12 and Q3 = 19, what is the interquartile range (IQR)?
A. 7
B. 31
C. 12
D. 19
**Answer**: A

#### LO 22: compare interquartile ranges across box plots to determine which dataset has greater variability in the middle 50%
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Interquartile Range (IQR)): Two datasets have box plots with these quartiles. Dataset A: Q1 = 4, Q3 = 10. Dataset B: Q1 = 6, Q3 = 9. Which has greater variability in the middle 50%?
A. Dataset A
B. Dataset B
C. Both are equal
D. Cannot be determined from Q1 and Q3
**Answer**: A

#### LO 23: identify the middle 50% of the data as the portion between the first and third quartiles inside the box
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Middle 50%): Fill in the blank: The middle 50% of the data is the portion ______.
**Answer**: between the first and third quartiles that falls inside the box

#### LO 24: calculate the spread of the middle 50% using Q3 − Q1
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Middle 50%): Given Q1 = 15 and Q3 = 22, the spread of the middle 50% is:
A. 7
B. 37
C. 15
D. 22
**Answer**: A

#### LO 25: compare the widths of the boxes in two box plots to determine which has a larger spread for the middle 50%
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Middle 50%): Two box plots show boxes spanning 5 to 15 (Plot A) and 8 to 12 (Plot B). Which has the larger spread for the middle 50%?
A. Plot A
B. Plot B
C. Both are equal
D. Neither; spread depends on whisker length only
**Answer**: A

#### LO 26: identify when the minimum, quartiles, median, or maximum are equal in a dataset or box plot
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Tied Values In Box Plots): Which scenario indicates tied values in a box plot?
A. The median line coincides with the right edge of the box.
B. The whiskers are of equal length.
C. The box is centered on zero.
D. The left whisker is shorter than the right whisker.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 27: describe how tied values, such as the median equaling the third quartile, affect the appearance of the box plot
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Tied Values In Box Plots): If the median equals the third quartile in a box plot, how does the display change?
A. There is no separate median line; it coincides with the right edge of the box.
B. The whiskers swap positions.
C. The minimum and maximum switch labels.
D. The box disappears and only whiskers remain.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 28: state that each quartile contains approximately 25% of the data
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Quartile Proportions): Fill in the blank: Each quartile contains approximately ______ of the data.
**Answer**: 25%

#### LO 29: describe how data are partitioned into four quarters between the minimum, first quartile, median, third quartile, and maximum
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Quartile Proportions): Which statement best describes how data are partitioned by a box plot’s five values?
A. The minimum, Q1, median, Q3, and maximum split the data into four quarters, each containing about 25% of the data.
B. The minimum and maximum split the data into two halves; quartiles show outliers only.
C. Quartiles divide the data into three equal parts; the median marks the smallest value.
D. The median and the maximum divide the data into four unequal quarters.
**Answer**: A

## Section: seg_27

#### LO 1: Calculate the arithmetic mean of a data set by summing all values and dividing by the number of values.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Arithmetic Mean (Average)): What is the arithmetic mean of 4, 7, 9, 10?
A. 7
B. 7.5
C. 8
D. 9.5
**Answer**: B

#### LO 2: Define the arithmetic mean (average) as the sum of all data values divided by the number of values.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Arithmetic Mean (Average)): The arithmetic mean (average) is the ____.
**Answer**: sum of all data values divided by the number of values

#### LO 3: Compare the mean and the median as measures of center, including their behavior in the presence of outliers.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Arithmetic Mean (Average)): Which statement best compares the mean and the median regarding outliers?
A. The median splits ordered data into two equal parts and is robust to outliers; the mean is the sum divided by the number of values.
B. Both the mean and the median are robust to outliers.
C. The mean splits ordered data into two equal parts; the median is the sum divided by the number of values.
D. The mean is denoted μ for samples; the median is denoted x̄.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 4: Calculate the sample mean x̄ from given sample data.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sample Mean (X̄)): For the sample 2, 5, 5, 8, what is x̄?
A. 4.5
B. 5
C. 5.5
D. 6
**Answer**: B

#### LO 5: Define the sample mean x̄ as the arithmetic mean of a sample and a statistic used to estimate the population mean μ when the sample is truly random.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sample Mean (X̄)): The sample mean, denoted x̄, is the ____; a good estimate of μ when the sample is truly random.
**Answer**: arithmetic mean of a sample

#### LO 6: Identify μ as the symbol denoting the population mean.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Population Mean (Μ)): The population mean is denoted by ____.
**Answer**: μ

#### LO 7: Calculate the population mean when all population data values are provided by summing the values and dividing by the population size.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Population Mean (Μ)): For the population 3, 3, 4, 6, 9, what is the population mean?
A. 4
B. 4.5
C. 5
D. 6
**Answer**: C

#### LO 8: Differentiate between the sample mean x̄ and the population mean μ in notation and the scope of data they summarize.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Population Mean (Μ)): Which statement correctly contrasts x̄ and μ?
A. x̄ summarizes a sample; μ summarizes an entire population; they use different symbols.
B. x̄ and μ both summarize entire populations.
C. x̄ denotes the population mean; μ denotes the sample mean.
D. x̄ and μ summarize the same data and use the same notation.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: Calculate a mean using frequencies by multiplying each distinct value by its frequency, summing the products, and dividing by the total number of data values.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Mean Calculation Using Frequencies): Given values 1 (freq 2), 3 (freq 1), 5 (freq 3), what is the mean?
A. 3
B. 3.33
C. 3.5
D. 4
**Answer**: B

#### LO 10: Define mean calculation using frequencies as computing the mean from distinct values and their frequencies.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Mean Calculation Using Frequencies): To compute a mean using frequencies, you ____ and then divide by the total number of data values.
**Answer**: multiply each distinct value by its frequency, summing these products

#### LO 11: Describe when and how to compute a mean from repeated values using their frequencies.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Mean Calculation Using Frequencies): When is it appropriate to use frequencies to compute a mean, and how is it done?
A. When values repeat: multiply each distinct value by its frequency, sum the products, then divide by the total number of data values.
B. Only when all values are unique; otherwise compute the median.
C. Only for qualitative data without counts; ignore frequencies.
D. When the total number of values is unknown; use interval midpoints instead.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 12: Define the median as the value that splits ordered data into two equal parts, using the middle value for odd n and the average of the two middle values for even n.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Median): The median is the ____ in ordered data.
**Answer**: value that splits ordered data into two equal parts

#### LO 13: Calculate the median of an ordered data set for both odd and even sample sizes.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Median): What are the medians of A: [1, 3, 7] and B: [2, 4, 6, 10]?
A. 3 and 5
B. 3 and 6
C. 2 and 5
D. 4 and 5
**Answer**: A

#### LO 14: Compare the median and the mean regarding robustness to outliers and suitability as measures of center.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Median): Which statement is correct about robustness to outliers?
A. The median is robust to outliers and is often preferred when extreme values are present.
B. The mean is robust to outliers; the median is not.
C. Both mean and median are equally affected by outliers.
D. The median equals the mean whenever there are outliers.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: Calculate the location of the median in ordered data using (n + 1) / 2.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Median Location Formula): For n = 9 ordered values, what is the median’s location?
A. 4
B. 4.5
C. 5
D. 5.5
**Answer**: C

#### LO 16: State that the median’s location in ordered data is (n + 1) / 2 and is not the same as the median’s value.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Median Location Formula): The median’s location in ordered data is ____ and is not the same as its value.
**Answer**: (n + 1) / 2

#### LO 17: Differentiate between the location of the median and the value of the median.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Median Location Formula): For ordered data [2, 4, 9, 10, 12], which statement is correct?
A. The median’s location is the 3rd position; the median’s value is 9.
B. The median’s location is 9; the median’s value is 3.
C. The median’s location is n/2 = 2.5; the value is (4 + 9)/2 = 6.5.
D. The median’s location and value are both 3.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 18: Define the mode as the most frequent value in a data set, noting that multiple modes can occur and that it applies to qualitative and quantitative data.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Mode): The mode is the ____ in a data set; multiple modes can occur and it applies to qualitative and quantitative data.
**Answer**: most frequent value

#### LO 19: Calculate the mode or modes of a data set, including identifying bimodal cases.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Mode): What is/are the mode(s) of 1, 2, 2, 3, 3, 4?
A. 2
B. 3
C. 2 and 3
D. 1, 2, 3
**Answer**: C

#### LO 20: Classify a data set as unimodal or bimodal based on the frequencies of its values.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Mode): Classify 5, 5, 5, 6, 7, 7, 8.
A. Unimodal
B. Bimodal
C. Multimodal (3+ modes)
D. No mode
**Answer**: A

#### LO 21: Define a frequency table as a grouped data representation listing intervals and their corresponding frequencies.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Frequency Table): A frequency table is a ____ listing intervals and their corresponding frequencies.
**Answer**: representation of grouped data

#### LO 22: Calculate the total number of data values represented in a frequency table by summing the interval frequencies.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Frequency Table): A grouped table has interval frequencies 4, 7, and 9. How many data values are represented?
A. 18
B. 19
C. 20
D. 21
**Answer**: C

#### LO 23: Define the midpoint of an interval as the average of its lower and upper boundaries.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Midpoint Of An Interval): The midpoint of an interval is the ____.
**Answer**: average of its lower and upper boundaries

#### LO 24: Calculate the midpoint of a class interval from its lower and upper boundaries.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Midpoint Of An Interval): What is the midpoint of the interval 10 to 14?
A. 11
B. 12
C. 12.5
D. 13
**Answer**: B

#### LO 25: Calculate the estimated mean of grouped data as Σ(fm) / Σf using interval midpoints and frequencies.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Mean Of A Grouped Frequency Table): Given midpoints m: 5, 15 with frequencies f: 4, 6, what is the estimated mean Σ(fm)/Σf?
A. 10
B. 11
C. 12
D. 13
**Answer**: B

#### LO 26: Describe why the mean computed from a grouped frequency table is an estimate and how midpoints approximate unknown individual values.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Mean Of A Grouped Frequency Table): The mean from a grouped frequency table is an estimate because ____.
**Answer**: midpoints approximate unknown individual values


---
## Section: seg_29

#### LO 1: Define a symmetrical distribution as one in which a vertical line can be drawn so the left and right shapes are mirror images of each other.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Symmetrical Distribution): A symmetrical distribution is one in which a vertical line can be drawn so the left and right shapes are _____.
**Answer**: mirror images of each other

#### LO 2: Describe a symmetrical distribution by noting that its histogram has mirror-image shapes on either side of a vertical line.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Symmetrical Distribution): Which best describes a symmetrical distribution’s histogram?
A. Mirror-image shapes on either side of a vertical line
B. A tail pulled to the left
C. A tail pulled to the right
D. A vertical line divides it into unequal shapes
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: Define a left-skewed distribution as one that is pulled out to the left.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Skewed To The Left): A left-skewed distribution is one that is _____.
**Answer**: pulled out to the left

#### LO 4: Describe a left-skewed distribution as having a tail pulled to the left.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Skewed To The Left): Which feature indicates a left-skewed distribution?
A. A tail pulled to the left
B. A tail pulled to the right
C. Mirror-image shapes about a vertical line
D. The mean and the median are the same
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: Define a right-skewed distribution as one that is pulled out to the right.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Skewed To The Right): A right-skewed distribution is one that is _____.
**Answer**: pulled out to the right

#### LO 6: Describe a right-skewed distribution as having a tail pulled to the right.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Skewed To The Right): Which feature indicates a right-skewed distribution?
A. A tail pulled to the right
B. A tail pulled to the left
C. Mirror-image shapes on both sides
D. The mean equals the median
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: State that in a perfectly symmetrical distribution, the mean and the median are the same.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Mean And Median In Perfectly Symmetrical Distribution): In a perfectly symmetrical distribution, the mean and the median are _____.
**Answer**: the same

#### LO 8: Describe that both the mean and the median reflect skewness, with the mean reflecting it more.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Mean Versus Median Sensitivity To Skewness): Both the mean and the median reflect skewing, but the _____ reflects it more.
**Answer**: mean

#### LO 9: State that, generally, in a left-skewed distribution, the mean is less than the median, which is often less than the mode.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Relative Order In Left-Skewed Distribution): Generally, in a left-skewed distribution, which ordering is correct?
A. mean < median < mode
B. mode < median < mean
C. median < mean < mode
D. mean = median = mode
**Answer**: A

#### LO 10: State that, generally, in a right-skewed distribution, the mode is often less than the median, which is less than the mean.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Relative Order In Right-Skewed Distribution): Generally, in a right-skewed distribution, which ordering is correct?
A. mode < median < mean
B. mean < median < mode
C. mode = median = mean
D. mode > median > mean
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: Describe that in skewed distributions, the median is closest to the high point (the mode), while the mean lies farther out on the tail.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Median And Mean Positions Relative To Mode In Skewed Distributions): In skewed distributions, the median is _____, while the mean tends to be farther out on the tail.
**Answer**: closest to the high point (the mode)

#### LO 12: State that in a symmetrical distribution, the mean and the median are centrally located close to the high point of the distribution.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Mean And Median Location In Symmetrical Distribution): In a symmetrical distribution, the mean and the median are centrally located close to the _____.
**Answer**: high point of the distribution

## Section: seg_31

#### LO 1: define the standard deviation as a nonnegative measure of overall variation indicating how far data values are from the mean
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Standard Deviation): The standard deviation is a number that measures how far data values are from their mean and is ____.
**Answer**: always zero or positive

#### LO 2: calculate the standard deviation for a data set using the appropriate sample or population formula or technology
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Standard Deviation): Treating {2, 4, 4} as a sample, what is the standard deviation (to two decimals)?
A. 0.94
B. 1.15
C. 1.63
D. 0.67
**Answer**: B

#### LO 3: interpret the size of the standard deviation to judge whether data values are concentrated near or spread out from the mean
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Standard Deviation): Two data sets share the same mean. Set A has SD = 2; Set B has SD = 10. Which statement is true?
A. A is more spread out than B.
B. B is more spread out than A.
C. A and B are equally concentrated around the mean.
D. A has the higher mean.
**Answer**: B

#### LO 4: define a deviation as the difference between a value and the mean (x − x̄ for a sample, x − μ for a population)
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Deviation): A deviation is ____.
**Answer**: the difference between a value and the mean

#### LO 5: calculate deviations for all observations in a data set
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Deviation): For the sample {3, 5, 7}, what are the deviations x − x̄ (in the order of the data)?
A. {−2, 0, 2}
B. {2, 0, −2}
C. {−1, 0, 1}
D. {−2, −0, −2}
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: interpret the sign and magnitude of a deviation to indicate whether a value is above or below the mean and how far
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Deviation): Given x̄ = 50 and x = 62, what does the deviation indicate?
A. +12 meaning 12 below the mean
B. +12 meaning 12 above the mean
C. −12 meaning 12 above the mean
D. 0 meaning equal to the mean
**Answer**: B

#### LO 7: define variance as the average of the squared deviations (s² for a sample, σ² for a population), with standard deviation as its square root
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Variance): Variance is the ____; the standard deviation is its square root.
**Answer**: average of the squared deviations

#### LO 8: calculate sample or population variance from data, using frequencies when provided and dividing by n − 1 or N as appropriate
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Variance): Treating {1, 2, 3} as a population, what is the variance σ² (to two decimals)?
A. 0.67
B. 1.00
C. 2.00
D. 0.33
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: differentiate sample variance from population variance in notation and in the divisor used (n − 1 versus N)
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Variance): Which statement correctly distinguishes sample from population variance?
A. Sample variance uses s² and divides by n − 1; population variance uses σ² and divides by N.
B. Sample variance uses σ² and divides by N; population variance uses s² and divides by n − 1.
C. Both use s² and divide by N.
D. Both use σ² and divide by n − 1.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 10: define the sample standard deviation s as the square root of the sample variance computed with denominator n − 1
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sample Standard Deviation): The sample standard deviation s is the ____ of the sample variance computed with denominator n − 1.
**Answer**: square root

#### LO 11: calculate the sample standard deviation s from raw or frequency data using formulas or a calculator
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sample Standard Deviation): For the sample {2, 2, 5}, what is s (to two decimals)?
A. 1.00
B. 1.41
C. 1.73
D. 2.00
**Answer**: C

#### LO 12: differentiate when to use s versus σ and how their computations differ
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sample Standard Deviation): When should you use s versus σ, and how do their computations differ?
A. Use s for samples dividing by n − 1; use σ for populations dividing by N.
B. Use s for populations dividing by N; use σ for samples dividing by n − 1.
C. Use s only for bell-shaped data; use σ otherwise.
D. Use s when the mean is unknown; use σ when the mean is known.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 13: define the population standard deviation σ as the square root of the population variance computed with denominator N
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Population Standard Deviation): The population standard deviation σ is the ____ of the population variance computed with denominator N.
**Answer**: square root

#### LO 14: calculate the population standard deviation σ for a census of all values using formulas or technology
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Population Standard Deviation): For the population {0, 0, 2}, what is σ (to two decimals)?
A. 0.67
B. 0.82
C. 0.94
D. 1.41
**Answer**: C

#### LO 15: differentiate the population standard deviation from the sample standard deviation in notation and computation
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Population Standard Deviation): Which statement correctly distinguishes σ from s?
A. σ uses denominator N and different notation than s; s uses denominator n − 1.
B. σ and s both use denominator n − 1, just different symbols.
C. σ always equals s for any data set.
D. σ is computed without squaring deviations; s squares deviations.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 16: calculate the number of standard deviations a value is from the mean using (#ofSTDEVs) = (value − mean)/(standard deviation) or by rearranging value = mean + (#ofSTDEVs)(standard deviation)
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Number Of Standard Deviations (#ofSTDEVs)): Mean = 50, standard deviation = 5, value = 60. What is #ofSTDEVs?
A. 2
B. -2
C. 10
D. 0.5
**Answer**: A

#### LO 17: interpret a #ofSTDEVs value to describe how far and in which direction a data point lies from the mean, noting the rule of thumb that values beyond two standard deviations may be far
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Number Of Standard Deviations (#ofSTDEVs)): A data point has #ofSTDEVs = −1.5. Which interpretation is correct?
A. It is 1.5 standard deviations above the mean.
B. It is 1.5 standard deviations below the mean.
C. It is 1.5 units below the mean.
D. It is far from the mean because it exceeds two standard deviations.
**Answer**: B

#### LO 18: calculate a z-score for a sample or a population using z = (x − x̄)/s or z = (x − μ)/σ
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Z-Score): In a sample with x̄ = 20 and s = 4, what is the z-score of x = 26?
A. 1.5
B. -1.5
C. 6
D. 0.24
**Answer**: A

#### LO 19: compare z-scores across different data sets to determine which value is relatively higher or lower with respect to its distribution
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Z-Score): Alice has z = 0.9 on Test A; Ben has z = 1.2 on Test B. Which value is relatively higher?
A. Alice, because 0.9 > 1.2
B. Ben, because 1.2 indicates further above his mean
C. Neither; z-scores are not comparable across data sets
D. Alice, because any positive z is higher than any other positive z
**Answer**: B

#### LO 20: calculate an estimated standard deviation from a grouped frequency table by using interval midpoints and appropriate technology
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Grouped Data Standard Deviation): Grouped data: intervals 0–10 and 10–20 with frequencies 1 and 1. Using interval midpoints and treating the grouped data as a population, estimate the standard deviation.
A. 0
B. 5
C. 10
D. 7.07
**Answer**: B

#### LO 21: explain why the grouped-data standard deviation is an estimate based on midpoints because individual data values are unknown
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Grouped Data Standard Deviation): In grouped frequency data, the standard deviation is an estimate based on ____ because individual data values are unknown.
**Answer**: interval midpoints

#### LO 22: describe the variability of a data set using the standard deviation and appropriate graphs, noting when the standard deviation is more or less informative
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Variability): Which statement best describes variability using the standard deviation?
A. Variability refers to describing data in terms of its spread; the standard deviation quantifies that spread.
B. Variability refers only to the mean of the data.
C. Variability is only about shape, not spread.
D. Variability is unrelated to standard deviation.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 23: calculate the standard deviation to quantify the variability in a data set
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Variability): Treat {1, 3, 5} as a population. What is the standard deviation (to two decimals)?
A. 1.63
B. 2.00
C. 1.15
D. 0.82
**Answer**: A

#### LO 24: state Chebyshev's Rule that at least 75% of data lie within two standard deviations, at least 89% within three, and at least 95% within 4.5 of the mean for any distribution
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Chebyshev's Rule): By Chebyshev's Rule, for any data set, at least ____ of values lie within two standard deviations of the mean.
**Answer**: 75%

#### LO 25: compare Chebyshev's minimum bounds to the Empirical Rule percentages and distributional assumptions
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Chebyshev's Rule): Which comparison between Chebyshev's Rule and the Empirical Rule is correct?
A. Chebyshev: at least 75%, 89%, 95% within 2, 3, 4.5 for any distribution; Empirical: approximately 68%, 95%, >99% within 1, 2, 3 for bell-shaped, symmetric distributions.
B. Chebyshev requires a bell-shaped distribution; Empirical applies to any data set.
C. Both give exact percentages for any distribution.
D. Chebyshev gives 68%, 95%, >99% within 1, 2, 3; Empirical gives at least 75%, 89%, 95% within 2, 3, 4.5.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 26: state the Empirical Rule that approximately 68%, 95%, and more than 99% of data lie within one, two, and three standard deviations of the mean for bell-shaped, symmetric distributions
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Empirical Rule): The Empirical Rule applies to ____.
**Answer**: bell-shaped, symmetric distributions

#### LO 27: compare the Empirical Rule to Chebyshev's Rule in terms of percentages and its requirement of a bell-shaped, symmetric distribution
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Empirical Rule): Which statement correctly compares the Empirical Rule to Chebyshev's Rule?
A. The Empirical Rule gives approximately 68%, 95%, and >99% within 1, 2, and 3 SDs but requires a bell-shaped, symmetric distribution; Chebyshev's Rule gives at least 75%, 89%, and 95% within 2, 3, and 4.5 for any distribution.
B. The Empirical Rule applies to any distribution with at least 75% within 2 SDs; Chebyshev applies only to bell-shaped distributions.
C. Both rules provide the same percentages for all distributions.
D. The Empirical Rule gives minimum bounds; Chebyshev gives approximate percentages for bell-shaped distributions.
**Answer**: A


---
## Section: seg_33

#### LO 1: construct a box plot from the minimum, first quartile (Q1), median, third quartile (Q3), and maximum to display the middle 50% of the data
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Box Plot): Which summary is sufficient to construct a box plot?
A. Minimum, Q1, median, Q3, and maximum
B. Mean and standard deviation only
C. Mode, range, and sample size
D. All individual data values in order are required
**Answer**: A

#### LO 2: define the first quartile as the median of the lower half of an ordered data set
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: First Quartile): The first quartile (Q1) is the _____ of the lower half of the ordered data set.
**Answer**: median

#### LO 3: calculate the first quartile (Q1) from ordered data or a frequency distribution
**Multiple Choice** (concept: First Quartile): Given ordered data 2, 4, 5, 7, 9, 10, 12, 14, what is Q1?
A. 4
B. 4.5
C. 5
D. 6
**Answer**: B

#### LO 4: interpret the first quartile as the 25th percentile in context
**Multiple Choice** (concept: First Quartile): In context, Q1 corresponds to which percentile?
A. 10th
B. 25th
C. 50th
D. 75th
**Answer**: B

#### LO 5: define frequency as the number of times a data value occurs
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Frequency): Frequency is the number of times a data value _____.
**Answer**: occurs

#### LO 6: explain how frequency differs from relative frequency and cumulative relative frequency
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Frequency): Which statement correctly distinguishes frequency from relative frequency?
A. Frequency counts occurrences; relative frequency is that count divided by the total number of outcomes.
B. Frequency is always a percentage; relative frequency is always a whole number.
C. Frequency accumulates across classes; relative frequency cannot be cumulative.
D. Frequency and relative frequency are identical measures.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: define a frequency table as grouped data displayed with corresponding frequencies
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Frequency Table): A frequency table displays grouped data with corresponding _____.
**Answer**: frequencies

#### LO 8: construct a histogram with appropriate class intervals, scaled axes, and labeled units
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Histogram): Which statement about a histogram is correct?
A. The bars are contiguous, with the x-axis showing data classes and the y-axis showing frequency or relative frequency.
B. The bars are separated to emphasize categories with no order.
C. The x-axis shows individual data values, and the y-axis shows the mean.
D. It plots points connected by lines to show change over time.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: define the interquartile range as Q3 minus Q1, representing the spread of the middle 50%
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Interquartile Range (IQR)): The interquartile range (IQR) is computed as Q3 minus _____.
**Answer**: Q1

#### LO 10: calculate the lower and upper fences using Q1 − 1.5(IQR) and Q3 + 1.5(IQR)
**Multiple Choice** (concept: IQR Outlier Rule): Given Q1 = 20 and Q3 = 40, what are the IQR fences using Q1 − 1.5(IQR) and Q3 + 1.5(IQR)?
A. (0, 60)
B. (-10, 70)
C. (10, 50)
D. (20, 40)
**Answer**: B

#### LO 11: define left-skewed and right-skewed distributions based on the spread of lower versus greater values
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Skewed): Data with more spread in greater values indicates _____ skew.
**Answer**: right

#### LO 12: define relative frequency as the ratio of a value’s frequency to the total number of outcomes
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Relative Frequency): Relative frequency is the ratio of a value’s frequency to the _____.
**Answer**: total number of outcomes

#### LO 13: interpret standard deviation to discuss concentration around the mean and unusual values
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Standard Deviation): Standard deviation measures how far data values are from their _____.
**Answer**: mean

#### LO 14: calculate the sample standard deviation s using s = sqrt[∑(x − x̄)²/(n − 1)] or its frequency form
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sample Standard Deviation): For the sample data 3, 3, 3, 3, what is s?
A. 0
B. 3
C. 1.5
D. undefined
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: explain when to use σ versus s and what σ conveys about population spread
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Population Standard Deviation): When should σ be used instead of s?
A. When the data represent the entire population, to describe the population’s spread.
B. When the data are a sample, to estimate the population mean.
C. Always; σ and s are interchangeable.
D. Only when the mean is zero.
**Answer**: A

## Section: seg_37

#### LO 1: Define probability as a measure of certainty for outcomes of a chance experiment.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Probability): Probability is a _____ of how certain we are of outcomes of a particular experiment or activity.
**Answer**: measure

#### LO 2: Calculate probabilities for simple events in equally likely sample spaces such as coins and dice.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Probability): You roll a fair six-sided die. What is P(rolling a 4)?
A. 1/4
B. 1/6
C. 1/3
D. 1/2
**Answer**: B

#### LO 3: Interpret a probability value as the long-term relative frequency of an outcome over many repetitions.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Probability): A game has probability 0.3 of a win on each play. Interpreted over many plays, about how many wins would you expect in 100 plays?
A. About 30 wins
B. About 70 wins
C. Exactly 30 wins
D. About 3 wins
**Answer**: A

#### LO 4: Define an experiment as a planned operation carried out under controlled conditions.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Experiment): An experiment is a _____ carried out under controlled conditions.
**Answer**: planned operation

#### LO 5: Exemplify experiments using contexts like flipping coins or rolling dice.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Experiment): Which is an example of an experiment?
A. Rolling a die 20 times and recording results
B. Thinking about a number
C. Guessing a friend's favorite color without recording
D. Making a wish
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: Define a chance experiment as one whose result is not predetermined.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Chance Experiment): A chance experiment is an experiment whose result is _____.
**Answer**: not predetermined

#### LO 7: Exemplify chance experiments such as repeated coin flips or single die rolls.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Chance Experiment): Which is a chance experiment?
A. Flipping a coin once
B. Calculating 2 + 2
C. Setting an alarm for 7:00 AM
D. Alphabetically assigning seats
**Answer**: A

#### LO 8: Define an outcome as a single result of an experiment.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Outcome): An outcome is a _____ of an experiment.
**Answer**: result

#### LO 9: Define the sample space S as the set of all possible outcomes of an experiment.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sample Space): The sample space S is the _____ of all possible outcomes of an experiment.
**Answer**: set

#### LO 10: Describe sample spaces by listing outcomes, drawing a tree diagram, or using a Venn diagram.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sample Space): Which is NOT a way to represent a sample space S?
A. A list of outcomes
B. A tree diagram
C. A Venn diagram
D. A histogram of past data
**Answer**: D

#### LO 11: Define an event as any combination of outcomes, denoted by uppercase letters like A or B.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Event): An event is any _____ of outcomes, typically denoted by uppercase letters such as A or B.
**Answer**: combination

#### LO 12: Compute event probabilities from a known sample space in equally likely situations.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Event): Let S = {1,2,3,4} with equally likely outcomes and A = {2,4}. What is P(A)?
A. 1/4
B. 1/2
C. 2/3
D. 3/4
**Answer**: B

#### LO 13: Define the probability range as 0 to 1 inclusive, with 0 impossible and 1 certain.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Probability Range): The probability range is from _____ to _____, inclusive.
**Answer**: zero and one

#### LO 14: Define equally likely outcomes as outcomes that occur with equal probability.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Equally Likely Outcomes): Equally likely outcomes are outcomes that occur with _____ probability.
**Answer**: equal

#### LO 15: Calculate probabilities in experiments with equally likely outcomes by counting favorable and total outcomes.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Equally Likely Outcomes): You roll a fair die. What is P(rolling an odd number)?
A. 1/6
B. 1/3
C. 1/2
D. 2/3
**Answer**: C

#### LO 16: Compute P(A) as the number of outcomes in A divided by the total number of outcomes in S when outcomes are equally likely.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Probability For Equally Likely Outcomes): Let S = {a,b,c,d,e} be equally likely and A = {a,b}. What is P(A)?
A. 2/5
B. 2/3
C. 3/5
D. 1/5
**Answer**: A

#### LO 17: Identify the favorable outcomes and the total number of outcomes needed to apply P(A) = count(A)/count(S) in equally likely cases.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Probability For Equally Likely Outcomes): Two coin tosses have S = {HH, HT, TH, TT}. Let A be outcomes with exactly one head. Which pair (count(A), count(S)) is correct?
A. (1, 4)
B. (2, 4)
C. (2, 3)
D. (3, 4)
**Answer**: B

#### LO 18: Define long-term relative frequency as the proportion of times an outcome occurs over many repetitions.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Long-Term Relative Frequency): Long-term relative frequency is the _____ of times an outcome occurs over many repetitions.
**Answer**: proportion

#### LO 19: Describe how observed relative frequency estimates a probability and stabilizes with many repetitions.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Long-Term Relative Frequency): As trials increase, the observed relative frequency of an outcome tends to:
A. Approach a stable value that estimates its probability
B. Fluctuate wildly without settling
C. Become zero
D. Become one
**Answer**: A

#### LO 20: Define the law of large numbers as the principle that relative frequency approaches theoretical probability as repetitions increase.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Law Of Large Numbers): With increasing repetitions of an experiment, the observed relative frequency approaches the _____ probability.
**Answer**: theoretical

#### LO 21: Describe how increasing the number of trials makes empirical probabilities approach theoretical probabilities.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Law Of Large Numbers): Flipping a fair coin many times, the proportion of heads will tend to approach:
A. 0.5
B. 0.25
C. 0
D. 1
**Answer**: A

#### LO 22: Define biased outcomes as cases where outcomes are not equally likely due to unfair devices or processes.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Biased (Unfair) Outcomes): Biased (unfair) outcomes occur when outcomes are not _____ likely due to bias in the device or process.
**Answer**: equally

#### LO 23: Contrast biased devices with fair ones, noting how unequal outcome probabilities differ from equally likely outcomes.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Biased (Unfair) Outcomes): Which observation suggests a biased die?
A. Over many rolls, 6 appears much more often than other faces
B. In 60 rolls, 5 appeared 9 times
C. In 100 rolls, each face appears between 12 and 20 times
D. The die is labeled 1 through 6
**Answer**: A

#### LO 24: Explain how factors like coin or die construction can introduce bias and affect probabilities.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Biased (Unfair) Outcomes): Which factor could introduce bias into outcomes?
A. A coin with uneven weight distribution
B. Using many repetitions
C. Drawing a tree diagram
D. Randomly assigning digits 0–9
**Answer**: A

#### LO 25: Define the event A OR B as containing outcomes in A, in B, or in both.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Or Event): The event A OR B contains outcomes in A, in B, or in _____.
**Answer**: both A and B

#### LO 26: Compute A OR B and its probability by combining outcomes from A and B without double-counting their overlap.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Or Event): Let S = {1,2,3,4,5,6} with equally likely outcomes, A = {2,4,6}, and B = {3,4,5}. What is P(A OR B)?
A. 1/6
B. 2/3
C. 5/6
D. 1
**Answer**: C

#### LO 27: Define the event A AND B as containing outcomes common to both A and B.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: And Event): The event A AND B contains outcomes that are in _____.
**Answer**: both A and B

#### LO 28: Compute A AND B and its probability by identifying outcomes in both A and B.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: And Event): Let S = {1,2,3,4,5,6} with equally likely outcomes, A = {2,4,6}, and B = {3,4,5}. What is P(A AND B)?
A. 1/6
B. 1/3
C. 2/3
D. 5/6
**Answer**: A

#### LO 29: Define the complement A′ as all outcomes not in event A.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Complement Of An Event): The complement A′ is the set of all outcomes _____ in event A.
**Answer**: not

#### LO 30: Calculate P(A′) as the proportion of outcomes not in A in a given sample space.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Complement Of An Event): Let S = {1,2,3,4} with equally likely outcomes and A = {1,2,3}. What is P(A′)?
A. 3/4
B. 1/4
C. 1/3
D. 0
**Answer**: B

#### LO 31: State the complement rule P(A) + P(A′) = 1.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Complement Rule): The complement rule states P(A) + P(A′) = ____.
**Answer**: 1

#### LO 32: Compute P(A) or P(A′) using the relationship P(A) + P(A′) = 1.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Complement Rule): If P(A) = 0.3, what is P(A′)?
A. 0.3
B. 0.5
C. 0.7
D. 1.3
**Answer**: C

#### LO 33: Define conditional probability P(A|B) as the probability of A given B, with the sample space reduced to B.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Conditional Probability): Conditional probability P(A|B) is calculated as P(A AND B) divided by P(_____); the condition reduces the sample space to B.
**Answer**: B

#### LO 34: Calculate conditional probabilities using P(A|B) = P(A AND B) / P(B) when P(B) > 0.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Conditional Probability): If P(A AND B) = 0.20 and P(B) = 0.50, what is P(A|B)?
A. 0.10
B. 0.20
C. 0.40
D. 2.50
**Answer**: C


---
## Section: seg_39

#### LO 1: Define independent events using the criteria P(A|B) = P(A), P(B|A) = P(B), or P(A AND B) = P(A)P(B).
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Independent Events): Fill in the blank to complete a valid criterion for independent events: P(A AND B) = ____.
**Answer**: P(A)P(B)

#### LO 2: Compute probabilities for independent events, such as P(A AND B) = P(A)P(B) or P(A|B) = P(A).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Independent Events): If P(A) = 0.3 and P(B) = 0.5 and A and B are independent, what is P(A AND B)?
A. 0.15
B. 0.8
C. 0.2
D. 0.3
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: Justify that two events are independent by verifying one of the equalities P(A|B) = P(A), P(B|A) = P(B), or P(A AND B) = P(A)P(B).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Independent Events): Given P(A) = 0.4, P(B) = 0.5, and P(A AND B) = 0.2, which single check is sufficient to justify A and B are independent?
A. Verify that P(A AND B) = P(A)P(B).
B. Verify both P(A|B) = P(A) and P(B|A) = P(B).
C. Verify that P(A) + P(B) = 0.9.
D. Verify that P(A AND B) = 0.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 4: Define dependent events as events where the occurrence of one changes the probability of the other.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Dependent Events): Fill in the blank: Two events are dependent if the occurrence of one ____ the probability of the other.
**Answer**: affects the probability of the other

#### LO 5: Classify situations as dependent when earlier outcomes affect later probabilities, such as sampling without replacement.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Dependent Events): You draw two items from a box without replacing the first. Are the draws dependent or independent?
A. Dependent, because the first draw affects the second.
B. Independent, because each draw has the same probability.
C. Mutually exclusive, because both draws cannot occur together.
D. Independent, because replacement is not needed for independence.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: Justify that events are dependent by showing that P(A|B) ≠ P(A), P(B|A) ≠ P(B), or P(A AND B) ≠ P(A)P(B).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Dependent Events): Given P(A) = 0.4, P(B) = 0.5, and P(A AND B) = 0.3, which statement justifies that A and B are dependent?
A. P(A AND B) ≠ P(A)P(B).
B. P(A|B) = P(A).
C. P(A AND B) = 0.
D. P(B|A) = P(B).
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: Define sampling with replacement as selecting and returning each item so it can be chosen again, making selections independent.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sampling With Replacement): Fill in the blank: In sampling with replacement, each selected member is ____.
**Answer**: replaced before the next pick

#### LO 8: Classify a sampling process as with replacement when repeated selections of the same item are possible and draws are independent.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sampling With Replacement): A name is drawn, recorded, returned to the hat, and then another name is drawn. How is this sampling classified?
A. Sampling with replacement
B. Sampling without replacement
C. Mutually exclusive sampling
D. Dependent drawing
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: Calculate probabilities for successive draws with replacement by treating draws as independent and multiplying probabilities.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sampling With Replacement): On each of two independent draws with replacement, P(A) = 0.4. What is P(A on both draws)?
A. 0.16
B. 0.8
C. 0.4
D. 0.64
**Answer**: A

#### LO 10: Define sampling without replacement as selecting items without returning them, so each can be chosen only once and draws are dependent.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sampling Without Replacement): Fill in the blank: In sampling without replacement, selected members are ____.
**Answer**: not replaced

#### LO 11: Explain how not replacing selections changes the sample space and affects subsequent probabilities, creating dependence.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sampling Without Replacement): Why does sampling without replacement create dependence between draws?
A. Removing an item changes the pool for later draws.
B. It ensures P(A AND B) = P(A)P(B).
C. Events cannot occur together in a single trial.
D. Each draw resets the sample space to the original.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 12: Define mutually exclusive events as events that cannot occur together and satisfy P(A AND B) = 0.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Mutually Exclusive Events): Fill in the blank: Mutually exclusive events satisfy P(A AND B) = ____.
**Answer**: 0

#### LO 13: Justify whether two events are mutually exclusive by showing that they share no outcomes or that P(A AND B) = 0.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Mutually Exclusive Events): Given P(A AND B) = 0, what conclusion is justified?
A. A and B are mutually exclusive.
B. A and B are independent.
C. A and B share some outcomes.
D. P(A|B) = P(A) necessarily.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 14: Demonstrate independence by calculating and verifying one condition: P(A|B) = P(A), P(B|A) = P(B), or P(A AND B) = P(A)P(B).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Showing Independence): P(A) = 0.6, P(B) = 0.5, and P(A AND B) = 0.3. Which single calculation verifies independence?
A. Check that P(A AND B) = P(A)P(B).
B. Check both P(A|B) = P(A) and P(B|A) = P(B).
C. Check that P(A AND B) = 0.
D. Check that P(A) + P(B) = 1.1.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: Justify a conclusion of independence or dependence by comparing computed probabilities to the required independence condition based on available information.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Showing Independence): Given P(A) = 0.5, P(B) = 0.4, and P(A|B) = 0.25, what is the correct conclusion?
A. Dependent, because P(A|B) ≠ P(A).
B. Independent, because P(A AND B) = 0.2.
C. Independent, because P(B|A) = P(B).
D. Mutually exclusive, because P(A AND B) = 0.
**Answer**: A


---
## Section: seg_41

#### LO 1: state the multiplication rule P(A AND B) = P(B)P(A|B) and its independent-events form P(A AND B) = P(A)P(B).
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Multiplication Rule): Complete: For events A and B, P(A AND B) = ______; if A and B are independent, P(A AND B) = P(A)P(B).
**Answer**: P(B)P(A|B)

#### LO 2: calculate a joint probability using P(A AND B) = P(B)P(A|B), applying P(A AND B) = P(A)P(B) when events are independent.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Multiplication Rule): If P(B) = 0.60 and P(A|B) = 0.50, what is P(A AND B)?
A. 0.30
B. 0.60
C. 0.50
D. 0.10
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: compare the conditional form P(B)P(A|B) with the independent form P(A)P(B) to determine which applies in a situation.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Multiplication Rule): You know P(B) and P(A|B), and nothing indicates independence. Which expression should you use to find P(A AND B)?
A. P(A)P(B)
B. P(B)P(A|B)
C. P(A) + P(B)
D. P(A AND B) / P(B)
**Answer**: B

#### LO 4: define conditional probability as P(A|B) = P(A AND B) / P(B).
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Conditional Probability): Fill in: The probability of A given B is P(A|B) = ______.
**Answer**: P(A AND B) / P(B)

#### LO 5: calculate conditional probabilities from joint and marginal probabilities using P(A|B) = P(A AND B) / P(B).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Conditional Probability): If P(A AND B) = 0.20 and P(B) = 0.50, what is P(A|B)?
A. 0.40
B. 0.70
C. 0.10
D. 0.20
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: interpret conditional probabilities in context to describe how knowing one event affects the likelihood of another.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Conditional Probability): Suppose P(A) = 0.30 and P(A|B) = 0.60. Which interpretation is best?
A. Knowing B makes A more likely.
B. Knowing B makes A less likely.
C. A and B are independent.
D. A and B are mutually exclusive.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: state the addition rule P(A OR B) = P(A) + P(B) - P(A AND B) and its simplification P(A OR B) = P(A) + P(B) for mutually exclusive events.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Addition Rule): Complete: For events A and B, P(A OR B) = P(A) + P(B) - ______.
**Answer**: P(A AND B)

#### LO 8: calculate probabilities of unions using the addition rule, including the mutually exclusive case.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Addition Rule): If P(A) = 0.40, P(B) = 0.50, and P(A AND B) = 0.20, what is P(A OR B)?
A. 0.70
B. 0.90
C. 0.30
D. 0.20
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: state that events A and B are independent if P(A|B) = P(A), which implies P(A AND B) = P(A)P(B).
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Independent Events): Fill in: Events A and B are independent if ______, which implies P(A AND B) = P(A)P(B).
**Answer**: P(A|B) = P(A)

#### LO 10: calculate P(A AND B) for independent events using P(A)P(B).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Independent Events): If A and B are independent with P(A) = 0.40 and P(B) = 0.20, what is P(A AND B)?
A. 0.08
B. 0.60
C. 0.20
D. 0.32
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: define mutually exclusive events as those for which P(A AND B) = 0.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Mutually Exclusive Events): Fill in: A and B are mutually exclusive if ______.
**Answer**: P(A AND B) = 0

#### LO 12: calculate P(A OR B) for mutually exclusive events using P(A) + P(B).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Mutually Exclusive Events): If A and B are mutually exclusive with P(A) = 0.30 and P(B) = 0.25, what is P(A OR B)?
A. 0.55
B. 0.05
C. 0.075
D. 0.30
**Answer**: A


---
## Section: seg_43

#### LO 1: define a contingency table and its purpose in organizing two-variable data to facilitate probability and conditional probability calculations.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Contingency Table): A contingency table is a ______ that facilitates calculating probabilities, including conditional probabilities, for variables that may be dependent.
**Answer**: tabular display of sample values for two variables

#### LO 2: calculate marginal, joint, and conditional probabilities using data organized in a contingency table.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Contingency Table): In a 2×2 table with counts: A=Yes,B=Yes:20; A=Yes,B=No:30; A=No,B=Yes:10; A=No,B=No:40. What is the joint probability P(A=Yes AND B=Yes)?
A. 0.20
B. 0.30
C. 0.50
D. 0.70
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: calculate conditional probabilities by reducing the sample space to outcomes satisfying the given condition in a contingency table.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Conditional Probability): Using the table: A=Yes,B=Yes:12; A=Yes,B=No:8; A=No,B=Yes:18; A=No,B=No:12 (total 50). What is P(A=Yes | B=Yes)?
A. 0.40
B. 0.24
C. 0.60
D. 0.20
**Answer**: A

#### LO 4: identify the conditioning event and the corresponding reduced sample space in a conditional probability statement.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Conditional Probability): A conditional probability is evaluated under a ______ by reducing the sample space to outcomes that satisfy it.
**Answer**: stated condition

#### LO 5: interpret conditional probability notation and results in context, such as P(A|B) using contingency table data.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Conditional Probability): Which statement best interprets P(A|B)?
A. Probability of A given that B has occurred, evaluated by reducing the sample space to outcomes where B occurs.
B. Probability that A and B both occur.
C. Probability that at least one of A or B occurs.
D. Probability of A without regard to any condition.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: calculate P(A AND B) from joint frequencies or probabilities in a contingency table.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Intersection (And) Of Events): In a table with 50 observations: X&M:15, X&N:5, Y&M:25, Y&N:5. What is P(X AND M)?
A. 0.30
B. 0.20
C. 0.50
D. 0.40
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: identify the table cell that represents the intersection of two events A and B.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Intersection (And) Of Events): In a 2×2 table with rows A=Yes/No and columns B=Yes/No, which cell represents A AND B?
A. A=Yes, B=Yes
B. A=Yes, B=No
C. A=No, B=Yes
D. Row total for A=Yes
**Answer**: A

#### LO 8: compare the joint probability P(A AND B) to the product P(A)P(B) to analyze relationships between events.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Intersection (And) Of Events): In a sample of 100, A occurs 50 times, B occurs 60 times, and both A and B occur 40 times. Which comparison is correct?
A. P(A AND B) > P(A)P(B)
B. P(A AND B) = P(A)P(B)
C. P(A AND B) < P(A)P(B)
D. Cannot be compared with the given information
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: calculate P(A OR B) using the addition rule P(A) + P(B) − P(A AND B) with contingency table values.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Union (Or) Of Events): If P(A)=0.50, P(B)=0.60, and P(A AND B)=0.40 from a contingency table, what is P(A OR B)?
A. 0.70
B. 0.50
C. 0.30
D. 0.90
**Answer**: A

#### LO 10: calculate P(A)P(B) and P(A AND B) from a contingency table to support a test of independence.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Independence Of Events): In a table with 200 observations, A occurs 80 times, B occurs 100 times, and both A and B occur 40 times. Which is correct?
A. P(A)P(B)=0.20 and P(A AND B)=0.20
B. P(A)P(B)=0.40 and P(A AND B)=0.20
C. P(A)P(B)=0.50 and P(A AND B)=0.25
D. P(A)P(B)=0.20 and P(A AND B)=0.25
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: compare P(A AND B) with P(A)P(B) to decide whether two events are independent.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Independence Of Events): In a sample of 100, A occurs 30 times, B occurs 40 times, and both A and B occur 10 times. Are A and B independent?
A. No, because P(A AND B) ≠ P(A)P(B).
B. Yes, because P(A AND B) = P(A)P(B).
C. Yes, because P(A AND B) > P(A)P(B).
D. Cannot be determined without more information.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 12: calculate a joint probability using the multiplication rule P(A AND B) = P(B|A)P(A) or P(A|B)P(B).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Multiplication Rule For Joint Probability): If P(A)=0.50 and P(B|A)=0.30, what is P(A AND B)?
A. 0.15
B. 0.30
C. 0.80
D. 0.65
**Answer**: A

#### LO 13: calculate missing probability entries and row/column totals in a probability contingency table and verify that the grand total equals 1.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Probability Contingency Table): In a probability contingency table: X&M=0.25, X&N=0.15 (row total X=0.40); Y&M=0.35, Y&N=?; grand total must equal 1. What is P(Y AND N)?
A. 0.25
B. 0.35
C. 0.40
D. 0.60
**Answer**: A


---
## Section: seg_45

#### LO 1: Define a tree diagram as a branching graph that enumerates experiment outcomes with branches labeled by frequencies or probabilities.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Tree Diagram): A tree diagram is a special graph to determine and visualize all outcomes of an experiment, consisting of ____.
**Answer**: branches labeled with frequencies or probabilities

#### LO 2: Calculate probabilities of events by using a tree diagram to enumerate paths and sum appropriate path values.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Tree Diagram): Flip a coin with P(H)=0.5 and P(T)=0.5, then spin a spinner that lands Red with probability 0.3 and Blue with 0.7 regardless of the coin result. Using a tree diagram, what is the probability the spinner is Red?
A. 0.30
B. 0.15
C. 0.50
D. 0.60
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: Define with replacement as returning a selection to the pool before the next draw so branch probabilities remain the same across draws.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: With Replacement): In repeated draws, "with replacement" means ____.
**Answer**: returning the first selection to the pool before making the next selection

#### LO 4: Calculate probabilities for repeated draws with replacement using constant branch probabilities on each level of the tree.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: With Replacement): A bag has 3 red and 1 blue chip. Two draws with replacement. What is P(both red)?
A. 3/8
B. 9/16
C. 3/4
D. 1/2
**Answer**: B

#### LO 5: Differentiate between with- and without-replacement scenarios by examining how second-draw branch probabilities and sample space sizes change.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Without Replacement): Which statement correctly distinguishes with- from without-replacement in a two-draw tree?
A. With replacement, second-draw branch probabilities stay the same; without replacement, they change due to a decreased sample size.
B. With replacement, the sample size decreases; without replacement, it stays the same.
C. With replacement, second-draw probabilities depend on the first draw; without replacement, they are independent.
D. There is no difference in second-draw probabilities between the two.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: Calculate probabilities for repeated draws without replacement by updating branch probabilities to reflect the reduced sample space.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Without Replacement): A bag has 3 red and 1 blue chip. Two draws without replacement. What is P(both red)?
A. 9/16
B. 1/2
C. 3/4
D. 3/8
**Answer**: B

#### LO 7: Describe how multiplying branch probabilities yields the probability shown at a terminal node of a tree diagram.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Branch Multiplication In Tree Diagrams): In a tree diagram, the value at an outcome node is obtained by ____.
**Answer**: multiplying the probabilities on the corresponding branches

#### LO 8: Calculate the value at an outcome node by multiplying the values along the corresponding branches in a tree diagram.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Branch Multiplication In Tree Diagrams): A two-step tree has first-level branches A (0.6) and B (0.4). From A, branch to X with 0.5; from B, branch to X with 0.3. What is the probability of the path A then X?
A. 0.30
B. 0.50
C. 0.12
D. 0.80
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: Interpret P(A|B) as the probability of event A given that event B has occurred.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Conditional Probability Notation): In conditional probability notation, P(A|B) denotes ____.
**Answer**: the probability of event A given event B

#### LO 10: Calculate conditional probabilities by restricting to the reduced sample space where the given event occurs.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Conditional Probability): In an experiment with 50 equally likely outcomes, B occurs in 20 outcomes and A AND B occurs in 6. What is P(A|B)?
A. 0.12
B. 0.30
C. 0.60
D. 0.24
**Answer**: B

#### LO 11: Describe the components of a Venn diagram, with the rectangle as the sample space and circles or ovals as events.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Venn Diagram): In a Venn diagram, the rectangle represents the ____ and circles or ovals represent events.
**Answer**: sample space S

#### LO 12: Describe the intersection of two events as the set of outcomes common to both, represented by the overlapping region in a Venn diagram.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Event Intersection (AND)): The intersection A AND B is the ____; in a Venn diagram, it is the overlapping region.
**Answer**: set of outcomes common to both events

#### LO 13: Calculate P(A OR B) by applying the addition rule P(A OR B) = P(A) + P(B) − P(A AND B).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Addition Rule For Two Events): If P(A)=0.4, P(B)=0.5, and P(A AND B)=0.2, what is P(A OR B)?
A. 0.9
B. 0.7
C. 0.3
D. 0.2
**Answer**: B

#### LO 14: Calculate the probability or count of a union A OR B using a Venn diagram or the addition rule.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Event Union (OR)): In a group of 100 items, 60 are in A, 50 are in B, and 20 are in both. What is P(A OR B)?
A. 0.90
B. 0.80
C. 1.10
D. 0.70
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: State the addition rule for two events as P(A OR B) = P(A) + P(B) − P(A AND B).
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Addition Rule For Two Events): State the addition rule for two events: P(A OR B) = ____.
**Answer**: P(A) + P(B) − P(A AND B)

#### LO 16: Identify the region inside the sample space but outside all event regions as representing outcomes in neither event.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Neither Event Region): In a Venn diagram with events shown as circles inside a rectangle, which area represents outcomes in neither event?
A. The overlap of the event regions
B. Outside the rectangle representing the sample space
C. Inside the sample space but outside all event regions
D. The union of the event regions
**Answer**: C

## Section: seg_47

#### LO 1: define conditional probability P(A|B) as the probability that event A occurs given that event B has already occurred.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Conditional Probability): Conditional probability is the ______.
**Answer**: likelihood that an event will occur given that another event has already occurred

#### LO 2: calculate conditional probabilities from experiment data, tree diagrams, or contingency tables.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Conditional Probability): Given P(A AND B) = 0.12 and P(B) = 0.30, what is P(A|B)?
A. 0.04
B. 0.36
C. 0.12
D. 0.40
**Answer**: D

#### LO 3: define dependent events as events that are not independent.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Dependent Events): Dependent events are ______.
**Answer**: events that are not independent

#### LO 4: differentiate dependent from independent events using criteria such as P(A|B) ≠ P(A) or P(A AND B) ≠ P(A)P(B).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Dependent Events): If P(A) = 0.40, P(B) = 0.50, and P(A AND B) = 0.18, how should you classify A and B?
A. Independent
B. Dependent
C. Mutually exclusive
D. Complements
**Answer**: B

#### LO 5: define independent events using equivalent conditions P(A|B) = P(A), P(B|A) = P(B), or P(A AND B) = P(A)P(B).
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Independent Events): Independent events are events where the occurrence of one has ______ on the other.
**Answer**: no effect on the other

#### LO 6: define mutually exclusive events as events that cannot occur together and satisfy P(A AND B) = 0.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Mutually Exclusive Events): Mutually exclusive events are events that ______; P(A AND B) = 0.
**Answer**: cannot occur at the same time

#### LO 7: define sampling with replacement as replacing each selected member so it can be chosen again.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sampling With Replacement): Sampling with replacement means ______.
**Answer**: after selection, a member is replaced and may be chosen more than once

#### LO 8: calculate probabilities in with-replacement scenarios using independence across draws.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sampling With Replacement): On each draw, P(A) = 0.30. With replacement on two draws, what is P(A on both draws)?
A. 0.09
B. 0.30
C. 0.60
D. 0.21
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: define sampling without replacement as selecting each member at most once.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sampling Without Replacement): Sampling without replacement means ______.
**Answer**: each member may be chosen only once

#### LO 10: calculate probabilities in without-replacement scenarios by updating counts after each draw.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sampling Without Replacement): From a set of 5 items with 2 labeled A and 3 not A, two items are drawn without replacement. What is P(both A)?
A. 0.10
B. 0.16
C. 0.40
D. 0.20
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: define the AND event as outcomes in both A and B at the same time.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: And Event): The AND event consists of ______.
**Answer**: outcomes that are in both A and B at the same time

#### LO 12: calculate P(A AND B) using the multiplication rule or independence when appropriate.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: And Event): If P(A|B) = 0.50 and P(B) = 0.40, what is P(A AND B)?
A. 0.90
B. 0.20
C. 0.10
D. 0.40
**Answer**: B

#### LO 13: define the complement of event A as all outcomes not in A.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Complement Event): The complement of event A consists of ______.
**Answer**: all outcomes that are not in event A

#### LO 14: calculate P(A OR B) using the addition rule with adjustment for overlap when events are not mutually exclusive.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Or Event): If P(A) = 0.60, P(B) = 0.50, and P(A AND B) = 0.20, what is P(A OR B)?
A. 0.90
B. 1.10
C. 0.30
D. 0.80
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: state the multiplication rule P(A AND B) = P(A|B)P(B).
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Multiplication Rule): The multiplication rule states: ______.
**Answer**: P(A AND B) = P(A|B)P(B)

#### LO 16: state the addition rule P(A OR B) = P(A) + P(B) − P(A AND B).
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Addition Rule): The addition rule states: ______.
**Answer**: P(A OR B) = P(A) + P(B) − P(A AND B)


---
## Section: seg_51

#### LO 1: Define a discrete probability distribution function as a model for a discrete random variable whose probabilities are each between zero and one, inclusive, and sum to one.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Discrete Probability Distribution Function (PDF)): A discrete probability distribution function is a ____ for a discrete random variable where each probability is between zero and one, inclusive, and the sum of all probabilities is one.
**Answer**: probability model

#### LO 2: Construct a discrete probability distribution for a given discrete random variable by assigning probabilities between zero and one that sum to one.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Discrete Probability Distribution Function (PDF)): Which option gives a valid discrete probability distribution for X = {0, 1, 2}?
A. P(0)=0.2, P(1)=0.5, P(2)=0.3
B. P(0)=0.2, P(1)=0.6, P(2)=0.3
C. P(0)=-0.1, P(1)=0.7, P(2)=0.4
D. P(0)=0, P(1)=0, P(2)=0
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: Explain the two defining characteristics of a discrete probability distribution function: each probability is between zero and one, inclusive, and the sum of the probabilities is one.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Discrete Probability Distribution Function (PDF)): In a discrete PDF, each probability is between ____.
**Answer**: zero and one, inclusive

#### LO 4: Define P(x) as the probability that the random variable X takes the value x.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: P(x) Notation): P(x) denotes ____.
**Answer**: the probability that the random variable X takes the value x

#### LO 5: Interpret P(x) as the chance that X equals x in a described context.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: P(x) Notation): If X is the number of items purchased by a customer, what does P(2) represent?
A. The probability that X equals 2
B. The probability that X is greater than 2
C. The value of X that occurs most often
D. The expected number of items purchased
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: Construct a two-column table labeled x and P(x) that lists the values of X and their corresponding probabilities, ensuring the probabilities sum to one.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Probability Distribution Table (PDF Table)): Which is a correct two-column table labeled x and P(x) for X taking values {1, 2, 3}?
A. x: [1, 2, 3]; P(x): [0.3, 0.4, 0.3]
B. x: [1, 2, 3]; P(x): [0.5, 0.5, 0.5]
C. x: [1, 2, 3]; P(x): [0.3, -0.1, 0.8]
D. x: [1, 2, 3]; P(x): [0.2, 0.3]
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: List the possible values of a discrete random variable X and their corresponding P(x) in a PDF table.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Probability Distribution Table (PDF Table)): For X that can be 0 or 1, which listing correctly pairs each value with its P(x) in a PDF table?
A. (x, P(x)) = {(0, 0.7), (1, 0.3)}
B. (x, P(x)) = {(0, 0.7), (2, 0.3)}
C. (x, P(x)) = {(0, 0.7), (1, 0.5)}
D. (x, P(x)) = {(0, 1.2), (1, -0.2)}
**Answer**: A

## Section: seg_53

#### LO 1: define the expected value (μ) as the long-term average of a random variable obtained by summing x·P(x) across all possible values
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Expected Value (Mean)): The expected value (μ) is the ______ of an experiment, computed by summing x·P(x) over all values of a random variable.
**Answer**: long-term average outcome

#### LO 2: calculate the expected value by multiplying each outcome x by its probability P(x) and adding the products
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Expected Value (Mean)): Which expression correctly computes μ for a discrete random variable?
A. Add x·P(x) across all values.
B. Add (x − μ)²·P(x) across all values.
C. Add x divided by P(x) across all values.
D. Take the square root of the sum of x·P(x) across all values.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: interpret the expected value as the long-term average outcome expected over many repeated trials in the given context
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Expected Value (Mean)): In context, the expected value is best interpreted as the ______ observed over many repeated trials.
**Answer**: long-term average outcome

#### LO 4: construct an expected value table with columns for x, P(x), and x·P(x) to organize and compute μ
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Expected Value Table): Which set of columns should an expected value table include to organize and compute μ?
A. x, P(x), and x·P(x)
B. x, cumulative P(x), and x²
C. P(x), (x − μ)², and (x − μ)²·P(x)
D. x, frequency, and median
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: define the standard deviation (σ) of a probability distribution as the square root of the sum of (x – μ)²·P(x) over all values to measure spread
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Standard Deviation Of A Probability Distribution): The standard deviation (σ) of a probability distribution is the ______, computed as the square root of the sum of (x − μ)²·P(x) over all values.
**Answer**: measure of spread

#### LO 6: calculate the standard deviation by first finding μ, then summing (x – μ)²·P(x) across values and taking the square root
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Standard Deviation Of A Probability Distribution): Which procedure correctly calculates σ for a probability distribution?
A. Find μ, compute (x − μ)²·P(x) for each value, sum these, then take the square root.
B. Multiply each x by P(x) and add the products.
C. Find μ and then sum (x − μ)·P(x) without squaring.
D. Sum (x − μ)²·P(x) and do not take the square root.
**Answer**: A
