## Section: seg_55

#### LO 1: define a binomial experiment by its fixed number of trials n, two outcomes with probabilities p and q where p + q = 1, and independent, identical trials
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Binomial Experiment): In a binomial experiment, the two outcome probabilities p and q satisfy ______.
**Answer**: p + q = 1

#### LO 2: classify scenarios as binomial or not by checking fixed n, two outcomes, constant p and q with p + q = 1, and independence of trials
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Binomial Experiment): Which scenario is a binomial experiment?
A. Flip a fair coin 10 times and count the number of heads.
B. Roll a die until a six appears, recording the number of rolls.
C. Draw 5 cards without replacement and count the number of hearts.
D. Give 12 questions where the chance of a correct answer changes after each question.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: define p as the probability of success on one trial and q as the probability of failure with p + q = 1
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Probability Of Success (p) And Failure (q)): In binomial settings, p denotes the probability of success and q denotes the probability of failure, with ______.
**Answer**: p + q = 1

#### LO 4: calculate q from a given p (or p from q) using p + q = 1
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Probability Of Success (p) And Failure (q)): If p = 0.65 in a binomial setting, what is q?
A. 0.35
B. 0.65
C. 1.65
D. -0.65
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: define X as the number of successes in the n independent trials of a binomial experiment
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Binomial Random Variable (X)): In a binomial experiment, X is the ______ in the n independent trials.
**Answer**: number of successes

#### LO 6: state the mean of a binomial distribution as μ = n p
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Binomial Distribution Mean): The mean of a binomial distribution is ______.
**Answer**: μ = n p

#### LO 7: calculate the mean μ of a binomial distribution given n and p
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Binomial Distribution Mean): For a binomial distribution with n = 40 and p = 0.20, what is μ?
A. 6
B. 8
C. 10
D. 12
**Answer**: B

#### LO 8: interpret μ = n p as the expected number of successes in n trials
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Binomial Distribution Mean): In a binomial distribution, μ = n p. What does μ represent?
A. The expected number of successes in n trials
B. The number of trials n
C. The probability of success p
D. The variance σ²
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: define the variance of a binomial distribution as σ² = n p q
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Binomial Distribution Variance): The variance of a binomial distribution is ______.
**Answer**: σ² = n p q

#### LO 10: calculate the variance σ² given n, p, and q
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Binomial Distribution Variance): For n = 12, p = 0.3, and q = 0.7, what is the variance σ²?
A. 2.52
B. 3.6
C. 0.21
D. 8.4
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: calculate the standard deviation σ using σ = sqrt(n p q)
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Binomial Distribution Standard Deviation): For n = 25, p = 0.4, and q = 0.6, what is the standard deviation σ?
A. 2.45
B. 6
C. 1.55
D. 2.0
**Answer**: A

#### LO 12: state the formula for the standard deviation of a binomial distribution as σ = sqrt(n p q)
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Binomial Distribution Standard Deviation): The standard deviation of a binomial distribution is ______.
**Answer**: σ = sqrt(n p q)

#### LO 13: state the notation X ~ B(n, p) for a binomial random variable with number of trials n and success probability p
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Binomial Distribution Notation): Standard notation for a binomial random variable X with n trials and success probability p is ______.
**Answer**: X ~ B(n, p)

#### LO 14: translate a verbal binomial scenario into X ~ B(n, p) by identifying n and p
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Binomial Distribution Notation): A process has 15 independent, identical trials with success probability 0.2. Which notation correctly specifies X?
A. X ~ B(15, 0.2)
B. X ~ B(0.2, 15)
C. X ~ B(15, 0.8)
D. X ~ B(20, 0.2)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: use binompdf(n, p, x) on a calculator to compute P(X = x) for a binomial distribution
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Binompdf): Which command computes P(X = 3) for a binomial distribution with n = 10 and p = 0.4?
A. binompdf(10, 0.4, 3)
B. binomcdf(10, 0.4, 3)
C. binompdf(10, 3, 0.4)
D. binomcdf(10, 0.4, 2)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 16: use binomcdf(n, p, x) on a calculator to compute P(X ≤ x) for a binomial distribution
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Binomcdf): Which command computes P(X ≤ 4) for a binomial distribution with n = 12 and p = 0.5?
A. binomcdf(12, 0.5, 4)
B. binompdf(12, 0.5, 4)
C. binomcdf(12, 0.5, 5)
D. binompdf(12, 0.5, 3)
**Answer**: A

## Section: seg_57

#### LO 1: define a geometric experiment by its three characteristics: repeated independent Bernoulli trials until the first success, an unbounded number of possible trials, and constant probabilities p and q with p + q = 1
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Geometric Experiment): A geometric experiment is a sequence of ______ repeated until the first success, with an unbounded number of possible trials and constant p and q where p + q = 1.
**Answer**: one or more Bernoulli trials

#### LO 2: calculate the probability that the first success occurs on a specified trial using the constant success probability p and failure probability q
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Geometric Experiment): What is the probability that the first success occurs on the 4th trial, given constant p (success) and q (failure)?
A. q^3 p
B. p^3 q
C. q^4
D. p^4
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: define p as the probability of success on each trial and q as the probability of failure with q = 1 − p
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Probability Of Success (p) And Failure (q)): In this context, p is the probability of success on each trial; q equals ______.
**Answer**: 1 − p

#### LO 4: calculate q from a given p (and p from a given q) and use these values consistently across trials in geometric settings
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Probability Of Success (p) And Failure (q)): If p = 0.30, what is q?
A. 0.70
B. 0.30
C. 1.30
D. −0.70
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: define X as the number of independent trials until the first success, taking values 1, 2, 3, …
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Geometric Random Variable (X)): Define X: X is the number of ______ until the first success, taking values 1, 2, 3, …
**Answer**: independent trials

#### LO 6: calculate probabilities such as P(X = k) or cumulative probabilities for X given the success probability p
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Geometric Random Variable (X)): For X counting trials until the first success with success probability p, which expression equals P(X = 3)?
A. q^2 p
B. p^3
C. 1 − q^3
D. q p^2
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: define the notation X ~ G(p) to denote a geometric distribution with parameter p
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Geometric Distribution Notation): The notation ______ denotes a geometric distribution with parameter p.
**Answer**: X ~ G(p)

#### LO 8: interpret X ~ G(p) as meaning X counts trials until the first success with success probability p on each trial
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Geometric Distribution Notation): What does X ~ G(p) mean?
A. X counts trials until the first success with success probability p on each trial
B. X counts successes in a fixed number of trials with probability p
C. X is a continuous variable with density G(p)
D. X has a binomial distribution with parameter p
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: calculate the mean μ of a geometric distribution using μ = 1/p
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Mean Of Geometric Distribution): If X ~ G(p) with p = 0.20, what is the mean μ?
A. 5
B. 0.20
C. 4
D. 1.25
**Answer**: A

#### LO 10: state the formula for the mean of a geometric distribution as μ = 1/p
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Mean Of Geometric Distribution): The mean of a geometric distribution is μ = ______.
**Answer**: 1/p

#### LO 11: interpret μ = 1/p as the expected number of trials required to obtain the first success
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Mean Of Geometric Distribution): In a geometric distribution, μ = 1/p represents the expected number of ______ required to obtain the first success.
**Answer**: trials

#### LO 12: state the formula for the variance of a geometric distribution as σ² = (1/p)(1/p − 1)
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Variance Of Geometric Distribution): The variance of a geometric distribution is σ² = ______.
**Answer**: (1/p)(1/p − 1)

#### LO 13: calculate the variance σ² of a geometric distribution from a given p using σ² = (1/p)(1/p − 1)
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Variance Of Geometric Distribution): If p = 0.25, what is the variance σ²?
A. 12
B. 3
C. 4
D. 16
**Answer**: A

#### LO 14: state the formula for the standard deviation of a geometric distribution as σ = √[(1/p)(1/p − 1)]
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Standard Deviation Of Geometric Distribution): The standard deviation of a geometric distribution is σ = ______.
**Answer**: √[(1/p)(1/p − 1)]

#### LO 15: calculate the standard deviation σ of a geometric distribution from a given p using σ = √[(1/p)(1/p − 1)]
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Standard Deviation Of Geometric Distribution): If p = 0.50, what is the standard deviation σ?
A. √2
B. 2
C. 1/√2
D. √(1/2)
**Answer**: A

## Section: seg_59

#### LO 1: List the defining characteristics of a hypergeometric experiment, including two groups, a designated first group of interest, sampling without replacement, dependent selections, and not being Bernoulli trials.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Hypergeometric Experiment): In a hypergeometric experiment, sampling is performed ______, making selections dependent rather than independent.
**Answer**: without replacement

#### LO 2: Classify sampling scenarios as hypergeometric or not based on whether they involve two groups, a first group of interest, sampling without replacement, and dependent selections.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Hypergeometric Experiment): An urn has 7 items in the first group of interest and 13 in a second group. You draw 5 without replacement and count how many from the first group appear. How should this be classified?
A. A. Hypergeometric (two groups, designated first group of interest, without replacement, dependent selections)
B. B. Not hypergeometric because sampling is with replacement (independent selections)
C. C. Not hypergeometric because there is only one group
D. D. Not hypergeometric because it consists of Bernoulli trials
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: Classify a scenario’s outcome distribution as hypergeometric when sampling without replacement from two groups with a designated group of interest.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Hypergeometric Probability Distribution): When sampling without replacement from two groups with a designated group of interest and dependent selections, which distribution governs the outcomes?
A. A. Hypergeometric Probability Distribution
B. B. A distribution for independent Bernoulli trials with replacement
C. C. A distribution that requires only one group
D. D. Not a distribution because no designated group is needed
**Answer**: A

#### LO 4: Identify the appropriate values of r, b, n, and x for modeling a described scenario with a hypergeometric distribution.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Hypergeometric Probability Distribution): A lot has 12 items in the first group of interest and 18 in the second group. You choose 10 without replacement and observe 4 from the first group. Which values correctly identify r, b, n, and x?
A. A. r = 12, b = 18, n = 10, x = 4
B. B. r = 18, b = 12, n = 10, x = 4
C. C. r = 30, b = 10, n = 12, x = 4
D. D. r = 12, b = 18, n = 4, x = 10
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: Define the group of interest as the group referenced in the probability question within a hypergeometric setting.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Group Of Interest (First Group)): In a hypergeometric setting, the group of interest is the ______ among the two from which the counted items are drawn for the probability question.
**Answer**: designated group

#### LO 6: Define X as the number of items drawn from the group of interest in the sample.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Random Variable X): Random Variable X is the ______ in the sample.
**Answer**: number of items from the group of interest

#### LO 7: Define the notation X ~ H(r, b, n) as indicating that X has a hypergeometric distribution with first group size r, second group size b, and sample size n.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Hypergeometric Notation): The notation X ~ H(r, b, n) means X has a ______.
**Answer**: hypergeometric distribution

#### LO 8: Interpret X ~ H(r, b, n) in a scenario by mapping the scenario’s quantities to r, b, and n.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Hypergeometric Notation): A jar contains 9 items in the first group of interest and 16 in the second group. You plan to draw 7 without replacement. Which notation correctly represents this?
A. A. X ~ H(9, 16, 7)
B. B. X ~ H(16, 9, 7)
C. C. X ~ H(9, 7, 16)
D. D. X ~ H(25, 7, 9)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: Define r, b, and n as the sizes of the first group, second group, and chosen sample, respectively.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Parameters (r, b, n)): In hypergeometric parameters, r is the size of the ______, b is the size of the second group, and n is the size of the chosen sample.
**Answer**: group of interest (first group)

#### LO 10: State the mean of a hypergeometric distribution as mu = n r / (r + b).
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Mean Of Hypergeometric Distribution): The mean of a hypergeometric distribution is mu = ______.
**Answer**: n r / (r + b)

#### LO 11: Calculate the expected value for a hypergeometric distribution using mu = n r / (r + b) in applied examples.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Mean Of Hypergeometric Distribution): If X ~ H(r, b, n) with r = 15, b = 25, and n = 8, what is the expected value mu?
A. A. 3
B. B. 8
C. C. 5
D. D. 4.5
**Answer**: A

## Section: seg_61

#### LO 1: state the conditions under which a process is modeled by a Poisson distribution
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Poisson Distribution): In a Poisson process, events occur at a ____ and independently of the time since the last event.
**Answer**: known average rate

#### LO 2: identify the interval of interest in scenarios that count events over time or space
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Interval Of Interest): A sensor counts vehicles passing a bridge during each morning rush period. What is the interval of interest?
A. The morning rush period
B. The total number of vehicles all year
C. The time between successive vehicles
D. The average vehicle speed on the bridge
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: calculate the Poisson mean for the specified interval of interest by scaling a given average rate
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Interval Of Interest): Calls arrive at an average rate of 4 per hour. For a 3-hour interval of interest, what is the Poisson mean for that interval?
A. 4
B. 3
C. 12
D. 1.33
**Answer**: C

#### LO 4: define the Poisson random variable X as the number of occurrences in the interval of interest with values 0, 1, 2, …
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Random Variable For Poisson): In the Poisson model, X equals ____ and takes values 0, 1, 2, …
**Answer**: the number of occurrences in the interval of interest

#### LO 5: state the notation X ~ P(μ) and that μ (or λ) is the mean number of occurrences for the interval of interest
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Poisson Notation And Parameter): Use the notation X ~ P(μ). Here, μ (also denoted λ) is ____ for the interval of interest.
**Answer**: the mean number of occurrences

#### LO 6: calculate the Poisson mean μ for a given scenario and express the model as X ~ P(μ)
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Poisson Notation And Parameter): A counter records an average of 2 events per hour, and the interval of interest is 3 hours. Which model is correct?
A. X ~ P(6)
B. X ~ P(2)
C. X ~ P(3)
D. X ~ P(1.5)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: identify when a binomial setting can be approximated by a Poisson model based on large n and small p
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Poisson Approximation To The Binomial): A binomial distribution can be approximated by a Poisson model when ____; under this approximation, the Poisson mean is μ = np.
**Answer**: p is small and n is large

#### LO 8: compare probabilities from the binomial distribution and the Poisson approximation to judge their closeness when n is large and p is small
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Poisson Approximation To The Binomial): In which setting would Poisson and binomial probabilities be closest?
A. n = 2000, p = 0.002
B. n = 50, p = 0.5
C. n = 30, p = 0.2
D. n = 100, p = 0.9
**Answer**: A

## Section: seg_63

#### LO 1: Compare the empirical relative frequencies with the discrete distribution’s theoretical probabilities to judge model fit.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Discrete Distribution): Which action best aligns with judging model fit using a discrete distribution?
A. Compare the class relative frequencies to the discrete distribution’s theoretical probabilities for X.
B. Construct only the empirical histogram and make no comparison to probabilities.
C. List x and P(x) in a theoretical PDF chart but ignore the class outcomes.
D. Calculate μ and σ from the theoretical distribution and skip checking the data.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 2: Compare the theoretical distribution with empirical and simulation distributions in shape, center, and spread.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Theoretical Distribution): Which task matches this comparison objective?
A. Assess similarities and differences in shape, center, and spread between the theoretical distribution and the empirical and simulation results.
B. Compute only P(X = x) from the theoretical distribution.
C. Record empirical outcomes without comparing to any model.
D. Focus solely on the simulation distribution without reference to theory.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: Calculate relative frequencies for each x from the class card-draw data.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Empirical Data): Which data should be used to calculate relative frequencies for each x?
A. Data obtained from conducting the card-picking experiment and recording outcomes.
B. The distribution that provides expected probabilities for values of X.
C. A distribution produced by technology-generated simulation for comparison with a theoretical distribution.
D. A table listing x and P(x) for the theoretical distribution.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 4: Compare the simulation distribution to the theoretical distribution and to the empirical data.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Simulation Distribution): Which distribution is produced by technology for such a comparison?
A. A distribution produced by technology-generated simulation for comparison with a theoretical distribution.
B. The distribution that provides expected probabilities for values of X.
C. A graph constructed for the empirical data and for the theoretical distribution.
D. Data obtained from conducting the card-picking experiment and recording outcomes.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: Compare short-run relative frequencies to long-term probabilities as the number of trials increases.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Long-Term Probabilities): Which description best matches long-term probabilities?
A. Probabilities considered over many repetitions of the experiment.
B. The calculated standard deviation from the empirical data.
C. A probability determined by theory for an event in the experiment.
D. The number of diamonds picked in ten trials.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: Calculate theoretical probabilities for events in the card experiment, such as drawing a diamond or obtaining a given count of diamonds.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Theoretical Probability): Which statement defines a theoretical probability for this experiment?
A. A probability determined by theory for an event in the experiment.
B. Probabilities considered over many repetitions of the experiment.
C. The calculated frequency of each x relative to the total number of trials.
D. The mean of the theoretical distribution.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: Compare observed values of X across datasets to the expectations from its theoretical distribution.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Random Variable X): What does the random variable X represent for this comparison?
A. The number of diamonds picked in ten trials.
B. The calculated mean from the empirical data.
C. Probabilities considered over many repetitions of the experiment.
D. A table listing x and P(x) for the theoretical distribution.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 8: Identify the appropriate n and p that complete the notation X ~ B(n, p) for this experiment.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Distribution Notation X ~ B( , )): In X ~ B(n, p), this is notation indicating the assumed distribution of X with ____ to be specified.
**Answer**: two values

#### LO 9: Construct the theoretical PDF chart listing each x and its corresponding P(x) for X.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Theoretical PDF Chart): The theoretical PDF chart is a table listing ____ for the theoretical distribution.
**Answer**: x and P(x)

#### LO 10: Calculate probabilities using P(X = x), P(1 < X < 4), and P(X ≥ 8) notation.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Probability Notation P(x)): Which option correctly uses the notation to denote a probability for X?
A. P(X = 3)
B. x̄
C. μ
D. σ
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: Calculate relative frequency for each possible x from the empirical class data.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Relative Frequency): Relative frequency is the calculated frequency of each x relative to the ____ in the class data.
**Answer**: total number of trials

#### LO 12: Construct histograms for the empirical data and for the theoretical distribution.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Histogram): Which description matches a histogram in this context?
A. A graph constructed for the empirical data and for the theoretical distribution.
B. The distribution that provides expected probabilities for values of X.
C. Notation used to denote the probability of X taking specified values or ranges.
D. Data obtained from conducting the card-picking experiment and recording outcomes.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 13: Compare the shapes and spreads of the empirical and theoretical histograms, noting similarities and differences.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Histogram): Which task aligns with comparing the histograms?
A. Compare the shapes and spreads of the empirical and theoretical histograms, noting similarities and differences.
B. Calculate μ and σ from the theoretical distribution only.
C. Build a table of x and P(x) without using any graphs.
D. Record raw outcomes without summarizing.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 14: Calculate the sample mean x̄ of the number of diamonds from the empirical data.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Mean (x̄)): Which description matches x̄ in this activity?
A. The calculated mean from the empirical data.
B. The mean of the theoretical distribution.
C. The calculated standard deviation from the empirical data.
D. The standard deviation of the theoretical distribution.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: Compare x̄ to the theoretical mean μ and the simulation mean.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Mean (x̄)): Which comparison aligns with this objective?
A. Compare the calculated mean from the empirical data to the mean of the theoretical distribution and to the simulation mean.
B. Compare only the standard deviations across all sources.
C. Compare relative frequencies to theoretical probabilities only.
D. Construct a histogram without making any comparisons.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 16: Calculate the sample standard deviation s of the number of diamonds from the empirical data.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Standard Deviation (s)): Which description matches s in this activity?
A. The calculated standard deviation from the empirical data.
B. The standard deviation of the theoretical distribution.
C. The mean of the theoretical distribution.
D. The calculated mean from the empirical data.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 17: Compare s to the theoretical standard deviation σ and the simulation standard deviation.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Standard Deviation (s)): Which comparison aligns with this objective?
A. Compare the calculated standard deviation from the empirical data to the theoretical standard deviation and to the simulation standard deviation.
B. Compare only the means across all sources.
C. Compare pairs of x and P(x) values only.
D. Record empirical outcomes without any comparisons.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 18: Calculate the theoretical mean μ for X using the distribution’s parameters.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Theoretical Mean (μ)): Which description matches μ in this context?
A. The mean of the theoretical distribution.
B. The calculated mean from the empirical data.
C. The standard deviation of the theoretical distribution.
D. The calculated standard deviation from the empirical data.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 19: Compare μ to the empirical mean x̄ and the simulation mean.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Theoretical Mean (μ)): Which comparison aligns with this objective?
A. Compare the mean of the theoretical distribution to the empirical mean and the simulation mean.
B. Compare only standard deviations across sources.
C. Compare only histograms without reference to means.
D. Construct a theoretical PDF chart without any comparison.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 20: Calculate the theoretical standard deviation σ for X using the distribution’s parameters.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Theoretical Standard Deviation (σ)): Which description matches σ in this context?
A. The standard deviation of the theoretical distribution.
B. The calculated standard deviation from the empirical data.
C. The mean of the theoretical distribution.
D. The calculated mean from the empirical data.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 21: Compare σ to the empirical standard deviation s and the simulation standard deviation.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Theoretical Standard Deviation (σ)): Which comparison aligns with this objective?
A. Compare the theoretical standard deviation to the empirical and simulation standard deviations.
B. Compare only means across the three sources.
C. Compare only probabilities from the theoretical distribution.
D. Record class outcomes without any comparison.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 22: Compare key similarities and differences among the theoretical, empirical, and simulation distributions to evaluate fit.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Comparison Of Distributions): Which task matches this objective?
A. Analyze similarities and differences among the theoretical, empirical, and simulation distributions.
B. Compute only a single theoretical probability.
C. Construct one histogram without comparing to any other distribution.
D. Record outcomes without summarizing or comparing.
**Answer**: A

## Section: seg_65

#### LO 1: define Bernoulli trials as an experiment with two possible outcomes (success and failure) and constant probabilities p and q for each trial.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Bernoulli Trials): Bernoulli trials are an experiment with ______ and constant probabilities p and q for each trial.
**Answer**: two possible outcomes (success and failure)

#### LO 2: describe the characteristics that make a sequence of trials Bernoulli (two outcomes, constant p and q).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Bernoulli Trials): Which set of characteristics makes a sequence of trials Bernoulli?
A. Two outcomes per trial with constant probabilities p and q.
B. Multiple outcomes per trial and changing probabilities.
C. Two outcomes per trial but probabilities depend on previous outcomes.
D. A fixed number of trials with probabilities that vary from trial to trial.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: define a binomial experiment as n fixed, independent Bernoulli trials with success probability p (and failure q).
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Binomial Experiment): A binomial experiment is a statistical experiment with a ______, each having two outcomes (success/failure) with probabilities p and q.
**Answer**: fixed number n of independent, identically conducted trials

#### LO 4: classify the Lucky Dice matching experiment as binomial by specifying n and p for X, the number of matches.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Binomial Experiment): A matching game is played 15 independent times; on each trial, a match occurs with probability 0.3. Let X be the number of matches. How should this be classified?
A. Binomial with n = 15 and p = 0.3.
B. Bernoulli with n = 15 and p = 0.3.
C. Binomial with n = 0.3 and p = 15.
D. Not binomial because trials have only two outcomes.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: calculate binomial probabilities, the mean μ = np, and the standard deviation σ = sqrt(npq) for a given n and p.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Binomial Experiment): For a binomial experiment with n = 10 and p = 0.2, what are μ and σ?
A. μ = 2, σ ≈ 1.26
B. μ = 2, σ ≈ 0.40
C. μ = 8, σ ≈ 1.26
D. μ = 2, σ ≈ 1.79
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: define the binomial probability distribution for X ~ B(n, p) as the distribution of the number of successes in n Bernoulli trials.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Binomial Probability Distribution): The binomial probability distribution for X ~ B(n, p) is the distribution of the ______.
**Answer**: number of successes in n Bernoulli trials

#### LO 7: classify a random variable as binomial by verifying Bernoulli conditions and a fixed number of trials.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Binomial Probability Distribution): Which random variable is binomial?
A. The number of successes in 12 independent trials where the success probability is constant p each time.
B. The number of successes until the first failure occurs, with constant p.
C. The number of successes in 20 trials where p increases each trial.
D. The sum of outcomes from a trial with three possible outcomes per trial.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 8: calculate P(X = x) = (n choose x) p^x q^(n − x) for scenarios such as the Lucky Dice game.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Binomial Probability Distribution): Let X ~ B(n = 3, p = 0.5). What is P(X = 2)?
A. 0.375
B. 0.25
C. 0.50
D. 0.125
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: construct a PDF table for X (matches) and Y (profit) that lists outcomes and associated probabilities summing to 1.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Probability Distribution Function (PDF)): To construct a valid PDF for X with outcomes {0, 1, 2} and probabilities P(0) = 0.2, P(1) = 0.5, P(2) = ?, which value of P(2) makes the probabilities sum to 1?
A. 0.3
B. 0.2
C. 0.5
D. 1.3
**Answer**: A

#### LO 10: identify whether a given table or formula qualifies as a discrete PDF based on probability bounds and total probability 1.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Probability Distribution Function (PDF)): Which of the following qualifies as a discrete PDF?
A. Outcomes {0, 1, 2}; probabilities {0.4, 0.4, 0.2}.
B. Outcomes {0, 1, 2}; probabilities {0.5, 0.5, 0.5}.
C. Outcomes {0, 1}; probabilities {0.7, −0.2}.
D. Outcomes {1, 2, 3}; probabilities {0.6, 1.2, −0.8}.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: define a random variable as a variable whose value is determined by the outcome of a random experiment.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Random Variable (RV)): A random variable is a variable whose value is ______.
**Answer**: determined by the outcome of a random experiment

#### LO 12: describe the distinction between a random variable (e.g., X or Y) and its observed values (x or y) in the Lucky Dice context.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Random Variable (RV)): Which statement correctly distinguishes a random variable from its observed values?
A. X denotes the random variable; x denotes a specific observed value after the experiment.
B. x denotes the random variable; X denotes an observed value after the experiment.
C. Both X and x always refer to observed values.
D. X refers only to probabilities, not outcomes.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 13: calculate expected values μx and μy from a PDF using μ = Σ xP(x) or, for binomial X, μ = np.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Expected Value): Given a PDF for X with outcomes {0, 1, 2} and probabilities {0.1, 0.6, 0.3}, what is μ = Σ xP(x)?
A. 1.2
B. 0.9
C. 1.0
D. 1.5
**Answer**: A

#### LO 14: explain expected value as the long-term average outcome (matches or profit) over many repetitions of the game.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Expected Value): Expected value is the ______ outcome over many repetitions of the game.
**Answer**: long-term average (mean)

#### LO 15: define expected value of a discrete random variable as μ = Σ xP(x).
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Expected Value): For a discrete random variable, the expected value is defined as ______.
**Answer**: μ = Σ xP(x)

#### LO 16: define the standard deviation of a probability distribution as a measure of how far outcomes typically vary from the mean.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Standard Deviation Of A Probability Distribution): The standard deviation of a probability distribution is a measure of ______.
**Answer**: how far the outcomes of a statistical experiment are from the mean of the distribution

#### LO 17: calculate the standard deviation using σ = sqrt(Σ (x − μ)^2 P(x)) or, for binomial X, σ = sqrt(npq).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Standard Deviation Of A Probability Distribution): For a discrete RV with outcomes {0, 1} and probabilities {0.7, 0.3}, what is σ?
A. Approximately 0.46
B. Approximately 0.30
C. Approximately 0.70
D. Approximately 0.21
**Answer**: A

#### LO 18: compare empirical and theoretical standard deviations for the Lucky Dice outcomes to evaluate variability and fit.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Standard Deviation Of A Probability Distribution): In repeated plays, the theoretical standard deviation is 2.0. An empirical standard deviation from many trials is 2.1. What is the best conclusion?
A. The empirical SD is close to the theoretical SD, suggesting a good fit.
B. The large difference indicates the model is invalid.
C. Standard deviations should be identical in any finite sample.
D. The empirical SD must always be less than the theoretical SD.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 19: compare theoretical probabilities with observed relative frequencies from repeated trials to illustrate the law of large numbers.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Law Of Large Numbers): A process has theoretical success probability 0.4. After 10 trials, the relative frequency is 0.3; after 1000 trials, it is 0.401. Which statement illustrates the law of large numbers?
A. As trials increase, the relative frequency approaches the theoretical probability.
B. Relative frequencies stay far from the theoretical probability as trials increase.
C. The theoretical probability changes to match the relative frequency.
D. The relative frequency must equal the theoretical probability after exactly 100 trials.
**Answer**: A


---
## Section: seg_69

#### LO 1: define a continuous probability density function as a function f(x) whose total area equals 1 and whose interval areas equal probabilities
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Continuous Probability Density Function (PDF)): A continuous probability density function is a function f(x) where interval areas equal probabilities and the total area is ____.
**Answer**: 1

#### LO 2: calculate probabilities over intervals by computing the area under f(x), such as using base × height for a constant f(x) on a finite interval
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Continuous Probability Density Function (PDF)): If f(x) = c on the finite interval [a, b], what is P(a < X < b)?
A. (b − a) × c
B. c
C. (b + a) × c
D. 1 / (b − a)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: describe how the area under f(x) between two x-values represents P(a < X < b) and why the total area equals 1
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Continuous Probability Density Function (PDF)): Which statement about a continuous PDF is correct?
A. P(a < X < b) equals the area under f(x) between a and b, and the total area under f(x) is 1.
B. P(a < X < b) equals f(b) − f(a), and the total area can exceed 1.
C. P(a < X < b) equals the length b − a, and the total area equals the length of the support.
D. P(a < X < b) is the area above f(x), and the total area is 0.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 4: state that for a continuous distribution P(X = x) = 0
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Zero Probability At A Point): For a continuous distribution, the probability at an exact value satisfies P(X = x) = ____.
**Answer**: 0

#### LO 5: explain that a single point has zero width, so the area (and probability) at X = x is 0
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Zero Probability At A Point): Why is P(X = x) = 0 for a continuous distribution?
A. A single point has zero width, so the area (and probability) at that point is 0.
B. The total area under f(x) can exceed 1.
C. f(x) is always 0 for continuous distributions.
D. Probabilities are determined by heights, not areas.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: calculate P(X = c) as 0 in continuous examples by recognizing the base of the rectangle is zero
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Zero Probability At A Point): Given a continuous density f(x), what is P(X = c)?
A. 0
B. f(c)
C. 1 / f(c)
D. 1
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: define the cumulative distribution function as P(X ≤ x) (equivalently P(X < x) for continuous variables) giving area to the left under the density
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Cumulative Distribution Function (CDF)): The cumulative distribution function F(x) equals P(X ≤ x), giving the ____ under the density.
**Answer**: area to the left

#### LO 8: describe how the CDF provides the area to the left and that P(X ≤ x) equals P(X < x) for continuous distributions
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Cumulative Distribution Function (CDF)): Which is true for the CDF of a continuous variable?
A. F(x) = P(X ≤ x) = P(X < x), the area to the left under the density.
B. F(x) = P(X ≤ x) = P(X > x), the area to the right.
C. F(x) = P(X = x), the probability at a point.
D. F(x) equals the height f(x) at x.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: calculate P(X ≤ x) or P(X < x) by finding the area to the left under f(x)
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Cumulative Distribution Function (CDF)): To find P(X ≤ x0) for a continuous variable, which area do you compute?
A. The area to the left under f(x) up to x0.
B. The area to the right under f(x) from x0.
C. The total area under f(x) minus 1.
D. The area above f(x) and below y = 1.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 10: state that for continuous distributions P(X > x) = 1 − P(X < x)
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Right-Tail Probability (Complement Rule)): For continuous distributions, the right-tail probability satisfies P(X > x) = ____.
**Answer**: 1 − P(X < x)

#### LO 11: calculate right-tail probabilities using the complement rule P(X > x) = 1 − P(X < x)
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Right-Tail Probability (Complement Rule)): If for a continuous variable P(X < x0) = 0.7, then P(X > x0) equals:
A. 0.3
B. 0.7
C. 1.7
D. Cannot be determined
**Answer**: A


---
## Section: seg_71

#### LO 1: calculate probabilities for outcomes within a specified interval under a uniform distribution using the constant density 1/(b − a) and the base × height area.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Uniform Distribution): For a uniform distribution on [2, 8], what is P(3 < X < 5)?
A. 1/3
B. 2/3
C. 1/6
D. 3/4
**Answer**: A

#### LO 2: state that a uniform distribution assigns equal likelihood to all values in a specified interval [a, b], inclusive.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Uniform Distribution): In a uniform distribution, all outcomes within the specified interval are ______.
**Answer**: equally likely

#### LO 3: describe the characteristics of a uniform distribution, including constant density over [a, b] and equal likelihood of all values in the interval.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Uniform Distribution): Which best describes a uniform distribution on [a, b]?
A. All values in [a, b] are equally likely.
B. Values near the center are more likely.
C. Probabilities increase linearly with x.
D. Only discrete values are possible.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 4: identify the notation X ~ U(a, b) for a uniform distribution and that a and b are the lowest and highest possible values of x.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Uniform Distribution Notation): The notation for a uniform distribution on [a, b] is ______.
**Answer**: X ~ U(a, b)

#### LO 5: describe what the notation X ~ U(a, b) conveys, including that the variable is uniformly distributed on the interval [a, b].
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Uniform Distribution Notation): What does X ~ U(a, b) indicate?
A. X is uniformly distributed on [a, b], with a the lowest and b the highest possible values.
B. X is normally distributed with mean a and standard deviation b.
C. X takes only the values a and b.
D. X is uniform but only on (a, ∞).
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: define the uniform probability density function as f(x) = 1/(b − a) for a ≤ x ≤ b.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Probability Density Function (Uniform)): For a uniform distribution on [a, b], the density is f(x) = ______ for a ≤ x ≤ b.
**Answer**: 1/(b − a)

#### LO 7: calculate the constant density height 1/(b − a) for a given interval [a, b] to use in probability computations.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Probability Density Function (Uniform)): For a uniform distribution on [2, 10], what is the constant density height?
A. 1/8
B. 1/10
C. 8
D. 1/4
**Answer**: A

#### LO 8: calculate the mean of a uniform distribution using μ = (a + b)/2.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Mean Of A Uniform Distribution): For a uniform distribution on [2, 8], what is the mean μ?
A. 5
B. 4
C. 3
D. 6
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: state the formula for the mean of a uniform distribution: μ = (a + b)/2.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Mean Of A Uniform Distribution): The mean of a uniform distribution is μ = ______.
**Answer**: (a + b)/2

#### LO 10: interpret the mean of a uniform distribution as the midpoint of [a, b] and as the long-run average value in context.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Mean Of A Uniform Distribution): In a uniform distribution on [a, b], which interpretation of μ is correct?
A. The midpoint of [a, b] and the long-run average value.
B. The most frequent single value only.
C. The lower bound a.
D. The area under the density.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: calculate the standard deviation of a uniform distribution using σ = (b − a)/√12.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Standard Deviation Of A Uniform Distribution): For a uniform distribution on [2, 14], what is the standard deviation σ?
A. √12
B. 12
C. √14
D. 12/12
**Answer**: A

#### LO 12: state the formula for the standard deviation of a uniform distribution: σ = (b − a)/√12.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Standard Deviation Of A Uniform Distribution): The standard deviation of a uniform distribution is σ = ______.
**Answer**: (b − a)/√12

#### LO 13: identify whether interval endpoints are included or excluded in a uniform distribution problem and adjust the stated interval accordingly.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Interval Endpoints Inclusivity): In uniform distribution problems, what should you determine about the interval endpoints before computing probabilities?
A. Whether they are included or excluded.
B. Whether a and b are integers.
C. Whether the variable is discrete.
D. Whether the density integrates to 2.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 14: interpret how inclusive or exclusive endpoints define the support [a, b] used in computing probabilities.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Interval Endpoints Inclusivity): Before computing probabilities, confirm whether the interval ______.
**Answer**: includes or excludes its endpoints

#### LO 15: calculate P(c < X < d) for a uniform distribution as (d − c)/(b − a) when a ≤ c < d ≤ b.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Probability Over An Interval (Uniform)): For a uniform distribution on [0, 10], what is P(3 < X < 7)?
A. 0.4
B. 0.7
C. 0.3
D. 0.5
**Answer**: A

#### LO 16: calculate the pth percentile k by solving (k − a)/(b − a) = p, yielding k = a + p(b − a).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Percentile In A Uniform Distribution): For a uniform distribution on [2, 10], what is the 25th percentile (p = 0.25)?
A. 4
B. 6
C. 2
D. 8
**Answer**: A

#### LO 17: interpret a percentile as the value below which a specified proportion p of outcomes fall in a uniform distribution.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Percentile In A Uniform Distribution): In a uniform distribution, a percentile k satisfies P(X < k) = ______.
**Answer**: p

#### LO 18: define the percentile k in a uniform distribution as the value satisfying P(X < k) = p on [a, b].
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Percentile In A Uniform Distribution): In a uniform distribution, a percentile k is obtained by the ______ on [a, b].
**Answer**: area under the constant density

#### LO 19: calculate conditional probabilities by restricting the support to the conditioning interval to form a new uniform density or by using P(A|B) = P(A AND B)/P(B).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Conditional Probability In A Uniform Distribution): For X uniform on [0, 10], what is P(X > 7 | X > 5)?
A. 0.6
B. 0.3
C. 0.4
D. 0.2
**Answer**: A

#### LO 20: describe how conditioning on a sub-interval (e.g., X > c) changes the sample space and yields a new uniform distribution on that interval.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Conditional Probability In A Uniform Distribution): Conditioning on X > c restricts the support and yields a ______.
**Answer**: new uniform density on that interval


---
## Section: seg_73

#### LO 1: calculate probabilities such as P(X < x), P(X > x), and P(a < X < b) for waiting-time scenarios modeled by an exponential distribution.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Exponential Distribution): In an exponential waiting-time model, which expression represents the probability of waiting longer than t?
A. P(X > t)
B. P(X < t)
C. P(X = t)
D. P(0 < X < t)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 2: state that the exponential distribution models time until an event or the time between events in contexts like service durations and device lifetimes.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Exponential Distribution): The exponential distribution models the ____ between events.
**Answer**: waiting time

#### LO 3: define the decay parameter m of an exponential distribution as m = 1/μ.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Decay Parameter (m)): The decay parameter m is defined by m = ____.
**Answer**: 1/μ

#### LO 4: calculate the decay parameter m from a given mean μ and calculate μ from a given m.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Decay Parameter (m)): If the mean is μ = 4, what is the decay parameter m?
A. 1/4
B. 4
C. ln(4)
D. e^(−4)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: state the distribution notation X ~ Exp(m) to indicate an exponential distribution with decay parameter m.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Distribution Notation): Use ____ to denote that X follows an exponential distribution with decay parameter m.
**Answer**: X ~ Exp(m)

#### LO 6: state the probability density function f(x) = m e^(−m x) for x ≥ 0.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Probability Density Function (Exponential)): The exponential pdf (for x ≥ 0) is f(x) = ____.
**Answer**: m e^(−mx)

#### LO 7: compute the value of the exponential pdf f(x) for a given x and m.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Probability Density Function (Exponential)): For m = 2 and x = 3, what is f(3)?
A. 2 e^(−6)
B. e^(−6)
C. 6 e^(−2)
D. 2 e^(−3)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 8: state the cumulative distribution function P(X < x) = 1 − e^(−m x).
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Cumulative Distribution Function (Exponential)): The exponential CDF is P(X < x) = ____.
**Answer**: 1 − e^(−mx)

#### LO 9: calculate cumulative and interval probabilities using the CDF, including P(X < x) and P(a < X < b) = F(b) − F(a).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Cumulative Distribution Function (Exponential)): With m = 0.5, what is P(X < 2)?
A. 1 − e^(−1)
B. e^(−1)
C. 1 − e^(−2)
D. e^(−0.5)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 10: state the survival function P(X > x) = e^(−m x).
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Survival Function (Exponential)): The exponential survival function is P(X > x) = ____.
**Answer**: e^(−mx)

#### LO 11: calculate right-tail probabilities P(X > x) using the survival function.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Survival Function (Exponential)): With m = 3, what is P(X > 1)?
A. e^(−3)
B. 1 − e^(−3)
C. e^(−1)
D. 3 e^(−1)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 12: state that the mean equals the standard deviation for an exponential distribution and that μ = 1/m and σ = μ.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Mean And Standard Deviation (Exponential)): In an exponential distribution, the mean μ equals the ____ and μ = 1/m.
**Answer**: standard deviation σ

#### LO 13: compute μ, σ, or m given any one of these quantities for an exponential distribution.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Mean And Standard Deviation (Exponential)): If m = 0.25, what are μ and σ?
A. μ = 4, σ = 4
B. μ = 0.25, σ = 4
C. μ = 4, σ = 0.25
D. μ = 2, σ = 2
**Answer**: A

#### LO 14: use k = ln(1 − A)/(-m) to find percentiles such as the median or the 80th percentile.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Percentile Formula (Exponential)): For m = 2 and left-tail probability A = 0.80, the percentile k equals:
A. ln(0.2)/(-2)
B. ln(0.8)/(-2)
C. 1 − e^(−4)
D. e^(−2)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: state the memoryless property P(X > r + t | X > r) = P(X > t) for r, t ≥ 0.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Memoryless Property (Exponential)): The memoryless property states P(X > r + t | X > r) = ____ (for r, t ≥ 0).
**Answer**: P(X > t)

#### LO 16: use the memoryless property to compute conditional waiting-time probabilities without regard to elapsed time.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Memoryless Property (Exponential)): Which equality follows from memorylessness: P(X > 10 | X > 7) = ?
A. P(X > 3)
B. P(X > 10)
C. P(X > 7)
D. P(X < 3)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 17: describe how exponential interarrival times with mean μ lead to Poisson event counts per unit time with mean λ = 1/μ, and conversely.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Relationship To Poisson Distribution): With exponential interarrival times of mean μ, the Poisson event rate satisfies λ = ____.
**Answer**: 1/μ

#### LO 18: compute Poisson probabilities for event counts given an exponential mean μ (via λ = 1/μ) or convert between λ and μ.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Relationship To Poisson Distribution): If the mean interarrival time is μ = 0.5 hours, what is λ (events per hour)?
A. 2
B. 0.5
C. e^(−0.5)
D. ln(2)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 19: infer from Poisson event-count information that interarrival times are exponentially distributed and determine the corresponding parameter.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Relationship To Poisson Distribution): If event counts per hour are Poisson with mean λ = 4, what is the mean interarrival time μ?
A. 0.25 hours
B. 4 hours
C. 0.25 minutes
D. 1 hour
**Answer**: A


---
## Section: seg_75

#### LO 1: state the defining properties of a probability density function, including nonnegativity, total area equal to one, and that P(a < X < b) equals the area under f(x) between a and b
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Probability Density Function (PDF)): Complete the definition: For a continuous random variable, a ______ function f(x) with total area 1 where the area under f(x) between a and b equals P(a < X < b).
**Answer**: nonnegative

#### LO 2: calculate probabilities such as P(a < X < b) by finding the area under a given pdf between the bounds
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Probability Density Function (PDF)): Let f(x) = 0.5 for 0 ≤ x ≤ 2 and 0 otherwise. What is P(0.5 < X < 1.5)?
A. 0.25
B. 0.5
C. 1
D. 0.75
**Answer**: B

#### LO 3: interpret the area under a pdf between two points as the probability that the variable falls in that interval
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Probability Density Function (PDF)): The area under f(x) between a and b equals ______.
**Answer**: P(a < X < b)

#### LO 4: define the cumulative distribution function F(x) as P(X ≤ x) for a random variable
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Cumulative Distribution Function (CDF)): Fill in: A function F(x) = ______ that gives the probability a random variable is less than or equal to x.
**Answer**: P(X ≤ x)

#### LO 5: calculate cumulative and interval probabilities using a given cdf, for example P(c < X < d) = F(d) − F(c)
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Cumulative Distribution Function (CDF)): Suppose F(x) = 0 for x < 0, F(x) = x/10 for 0 ≤ x ≤ 10, and F(x) = 1 for x > 10. What is P(3 < X < 7)?
A. 0.3
B. 0.4
C. 0.7
D. 0.5
**Answer**: B

#### LO 6: calculate probabilities, percentiles, the mean, and the standard deviation for X ~ U(a, b) using f(x) = 1/(b − a) and F(x) = (x − a)/(b − a)
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Uniform Distribution): If X ~ U(1, 5), what is P(X ≤ 3)?
A. 0.25
B. 0.5
C. 0.75
D. 1
**Answer**: B

#### LO 7: compare empirical summaries and graphical shapes from random numbers on [0,1] to the theoretical values and shape expected from U(0,1)
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Uniform Distribution): Which description best matches the theoretical pdf shape for U(0,1)?
A. Constant height on [0,1], zero elsewhere
B. Increasing linearly over [0,1]
C. Bell-shaped centered at 0.5
D. Peaks near 0 and 1 with a dip in the middle
**Answer**: A

#### LO 8: define the exponential distribution X ~ Exp(m) with pdf f(x) = m e^(−mx), cdf P(X ≤ x) = 1 − e^(−mx) for x ≥ 0, mean and standard deviation 1/m, and the memoryless property
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Exponential Distribution): For x ≥ 0, the cdf is P(X ≤ x) = ______.
**Answer**: 1 − e^(−mx)

#### LO 9: calculate probabilities, percentiles, the mean, and the standard deviation for an exponential model given m or μ
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Exponential Distribution): For X ~ Exp(m) with m = 2, what is P(X ≤ 1)?
A. 1 − e^(−2)
B. e^(−2)
C. 1 − e^(−1)
D. e^(−1)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 10: define the decay parameter m as the rate in the exponential pdf f(x) = m e^(−mx) and as the reciprocal of the mean m = 1/μ
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Decay Parameter): In the exponential pdf f(x) = ______, the rate m equals 1/μ.
**Answer**: m e^(−mx)

#### LO 11: calculate the decay parameter from a given mean (m = 1/μ) and use it to compute probabilities such as P(X > x) = e^(−mx)
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Decay Parameter): An exponential model has mean μ = 5. What is P(X > 3)?
A. e^(−3/5)
B. 1 − e^(−3/5)
C. e^(−5/3)
D. 1 − e^(−5/3)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 12: describe how changes in m alter the exponential distribution’s shape, with larger m producing faster decay of tail probabilities and a smaller mean
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Decay Parameter): If the decay parameter m increases, what happens to the exponential distribution?
A. Tail probabilities decay faster and the mean decreases
B. Tail probabilities decay slower and the mean increases
C. Tail probabilities decay faster and the mean increases
D. The shape does not change
**Answer**: A

#### LO 13: define conditional probability as the probability of an event occurring given that another event has already occurred
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Conditional Probability): Conditional probability is the likelihood of an event occurring given that ______.
**Answer**: another event has already occurred

#### LO 14: calculate conditional probabilities in uniform and exponential settings, including using ratios of interval lengths and the memoryless property P(X > x + k|X > x) = P(X > k)
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Conditional Probability): For X ~ Exp(m), what is P(X > 7 | X > 5)?
A. e^(−2m)
B. e^(−5m)
C. e^(−7m)
D. 1 − e^(−2m)
**Answer**: A

## Section: seg_79

#### LO 1: define the standard normal distribution as Z ~ N(0, 1), a normal distribution of z-scores with mean 0 and standard deviation 1
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Standard Normal Distribution): The standard normal distribution is denoted ____.
**Answer**: Z ~ N(0, 1)

#### LO 2: calculate the raw score x associated with a given z on the standard normal scale using x = μ + zσ
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Standard Normal Distribution): If μ = 50, σ = 10, and z = 1.2, what is x using x = μ + zσ?
A. 62
B. 61.2
C. 48.8
D. 50
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: interpret positions on the standard normal distribution by recognizing that positive z-scores are to the right of the mean 0, negative z-scores to the left, and z = 0 at the mean
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Standard Normal Distribution): On the standard normal distribution, where does z = -1.5 lie?
A. Right of 0
B. At 0
C. Left of 0
D. Cannot be determined
**Answer**: C

#### LO 4: calculate a z-score using z = (x − μ)/σ for a normally distributed variable
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Z-Score): For X ~ N(100, 20) and x = 130, what is z = (x − μ)/σ?
A. 1.5
B. -1.5
C. 30
D. 0.15
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: interpret the sign and magnitude of a z-score as the number of standard deviations a value is above or below the mean
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Z-Score): What does z = -2.3 indicate?
A. 2.3 standard deviations above the mean
B. 2.3 standard deviations below the mean
C. Equal to the mean
D. 0.23 standard deviations below the mean
**Answer**: B

#### LO 6: define the notation X ~ N(μ, σ) as indicating a normal random variable with mean μ and standard deviation σ
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Normal Distribution Notation): The notation X ~ N(μ, σ) indicates a normally distributed random variable with ____.
**Answer**: mean μ and standard deviation σ

#### LO 7: interpret X ~ N(μ, σ) by identifying the distribution, its mean, and its standard deviation
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Normal Distribution Notation): For X ~ N(10, 3), identify μ and σ.
A. μ = 10, σ = 3
B. μ = 3, σ = 10
C. μ = 10, σ = 9
D. μ = 10, σ = √3
**Answer**: A

#### LO 8: calculate standardized values with z = (x − μ)/σ and invert the transformation with x = μ + zσ
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Standardization (Z-Transformation)): Which pair correctly shows standardization and its inverse?
A. z = (x − μ)/σ; x = μ + zσ
B. z = (x − μ)σ; x = μ + z/σ
C. z = (x/σ) − μ; x = μ − zσ
D. z = (μ − x)/σ; x = μ − zσ
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: interpret standardization as converting X ~ N(μ, σ) to the standard normal distribution Z ~ N(0, 1) to enable comparison across scales
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Standardization (Z-Transformation)): Standardization converts X ~ N(μ, σ) to ____.
**Answer**: Z ~ N(0, 1)

#### LO 10: state that in a normal distribution about 68%, 95%, and 99.7% of values lie within ±1σ, ±2σ, and ±3σ of the mean, respectively
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Empirical Rule (68-95-99.7 Rule)): In a normal distribution, about ____ of values lie within ±2σ of the mean.
**Answer**: 95%

#### LO 11: calculate approximate intervals μ ± σ, μ ± 2σ, and μ ± 3σ (with corresponding z-scores ±1, ±2, ±3) for a given normal distribution
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Empirical Rule (68-95-99.7 Rule)): For a normal distribution with μ = 20 and σ = 4, what is the interval μ ± 2σ?
A. [16, 24]
B. [12, 28]
C. [8, 32]
D. [20, 28]
**Answer**: B

#### LO 12: calculate and compare z-scores from different normal distributions to place values on a common standardized scale
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Comparing Values Using Z-Scores): Value A: 85 from N(70, 10). Value B: 920 from N(900, 40). Which has the higher z-score?
A. Value A (z = 1.5)
B. Value B (z = 0.5)
C. Both equal
D. Cannot determine
**Answer**: A

#### LO 13: interpret equal or similar z-scores from different normal distributions as indicating the same relative position with respect to their means and standard deviations
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Comparing Values Using Z-Scores): Two scores from different normal distributions both have z = 1.2. Which statement is correct?
A. They are 1.2 standard deviations above their respective means.
B. They have the same raw value.
C. They are at the mean.
D. They are 1.2 standard deviations below their respective means.
**Answer**: A

## Section: seg_81

#### LO 1: define P(X < x) as the area to the left of the vertical line at x for a normal variable.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Area To The Left): For a normal variable X, P(X < x) equals the ______.
**Answer**: area to the left of the vertical line at x

#### LO 2: calculate P(X < x) for a specified x and normal distribution using technology or tables.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Area To The Left): To compute P(X < x) for X ~ N(μ, σ), which normalcdf setup is correct?
A. normalcdf(-1E99, x, μ, σ)
B. normalcdf(x, 1E99, μ, σ)
C. normalcdf(0, x, μ, σ)
D. normalcdf(x, 0, μ, σ)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: interpret a shaded left-tail region on a normal curve as the probability P(X < x).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Area To The Left): A normal curve shows the region shaded to the left of x. Which probability does this represent?
A. P(X < x)
B. P(X > x)
C. P(X = x)
D. 1 − P(X < x)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 4: calculate P(X > x) as 1 − P(X < x) or by using appropriate bounds with normalcdf.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Area To The Right): Which is a correct way to compute P(X > x) for a normal distribution?
A. 1 − P(X < x)
B. P(X < x)
C. normalcdf(-1E99, x, μ, σ)
D. The area to the left of x
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: state that P(X > x) equals the area to the right of x and equals 1 − P(X < x).
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Area To The Right): For a normal distribution, P(X > x) equals the ______ and equals 1 − P(X < x).
**Answer**: area to the right of the vertical line at x

#### LO 6: translate a right-tail probability into its equivalent left-tail form (and vice versa) using P(X > x) = 1 − P(X < x).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Area To The Right): Which expression equals P(X > a) for a normal variable X?
A. 1 − P(X < a)
B. P(X < a)
C. 1 − P(X > a)
D. P(X ≤ a)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: state that for continuous distributions P(X < x) = P(X ≤ x) and P(X > x) = P(X ≥ x).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Inequality Equivalence In Continuous Distributions): Which statement is true for continuous distributions?
A. P(X < x) = P(X ≤ x) and P(X > x) = P(X ≥ x)
B. P(X < x) = P(X ≥ x) and P(X > x) = P(X ≤ x)
C. P(X < x) = P(X > x)
D. P(X ≤ x) = P(X ≥ x)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 8: calculate probabilities for a normal distribution treating strict and non-strict inequalities as equivalent.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Inequality Equivalence In Continuous Distributions): For a normal (continuous) distribution, which pair of probabilities are equal?
A. P(X < c) and P(X ≤ c)
B. P(X < c) and P(X ≥ c)
C. P(X > c) and P(X < c)
D. P(X ≥ c) and P(X ≤ c)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: interpret X ~ N(μ, σ) as X being normally distributed with mean μ and standard deviation σ.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Normal Distribution Notation): The notation X ~ N(μ, σ) means X is ______ with mean μ and standard deviation σ.
**Answer**: normally distributed

#### LO 10: identify the mean μ and standard deviation σ from the notation X ~ N(μ, σ).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Normal Distribution Notation): In X ~ N(μ, σ), what does σ denote?
A. The standard deviation
B. The mean
C. A percentile
D. A quartile
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: use normalcdf(lower, upper, mean, standard deviation) to compute normal probabilities for intervals and tails.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Normalcdf Function): What is the correct syntax for normalcdf?
A. normalcdf(lower value, upper value, mean, standard deviation)
B. normalcdf(mean, standard deviation, lower value, upper value)
C. normalcdf(upper value, lower value, mean, standard deviation)
D. normalcdf(mean, lower value, upper value, standard deviation)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 12: describe the syntax and parameters of normalcdf, including using ±1E99 as bounds for extreme tails.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Normalcdf Function): Which normalcdf call correctly computes P(X > x) for X ~ N(μ, σ)?
A. normalcdf(x, 1E99, μ, σ)
B. normalcdf(-1E99, x, μ, σ)
C. normalcdf(-1E99, 1E99, μ, σ)
D. normalcdf(μ, σ, -1E99, 1E99)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 13: use invNorm(area to the left, mean, standard deviation) to find the value k corresponding to a given percentile.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Invnorm Function): What is the correct syntax for invNorm to find the value k for a given percentile?
A. invNorm(area to the left, mean, standard deviation)
B. invNorm(mean, standard deviation, area to the left)
C. invNorm(standard deviation, mean, area to the left)
D. invNorm(area to the right, mean, standard deviation)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 14: interpret the output of invNorm as the critical value k with the specified area to its left.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Invnorm Function): What does invNorm(area, μ, σ) return?
A. The value k with the specified area to its left
B. The area to the left of k
C. The standard deviation σ
D. The mean μ
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: calculate a stated percentile for a normal distribution using invNorm with the given mean and standard deviation.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Percentile): Which input to invNorm finds the 90th percentile for X ~ N(μ, σ)?
A. invNorm(0.90, μ, σ)
B. invNorm(0.10, μ, σ)
C. invNorm(0.90, σ, μ)
D. invNorm(μ, σ, 0.90)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 16: interpret a percentile as the value at or below which the stated percentage of observations fall.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Percentile): A percentile is the value ______ which the stated percentage of observations fall.
**Answer**: at or below

#### LO 17: define a percentile as the value k such that a stated percentage of observations are at or below k and the remainder at or above.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Percentile): A percentile is the value k such that a stated percentage of observations are ______ k and the remainder are at or above k.
**Answer**: at or below

#### LO 18: define Q1 as the 25th percentile and Q3 as the 75th percentile of a distribution.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Quartiles): Q1 is the 25th percentile and Q3 is the ______ of a distribution.
**Answer**: 75th percentile

#### LO 19: calculate Q1 and Q3 for a normal distribution using invNorm with areas 0.25 and 0.75.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Quartiles): Which invNorm setups correctly compute Q1 and Q3 (respectively) for X ~ N(μ, σ)?
A. Q1 = invNorm(0.25, μ, σ), Q3 = invNorm(0.75, μ, σ)
B. Q1 = invNorm(0.75, μ, σ), Q3 = invNorm(0.25, μ, σ)
C. Q1 = invNorm(0.50, μ, σ), Q3 = invNorm(0.90, μ, σ)
D. Q1 = invNorm(μ, σ, 0.25), Q3 = invNorm(μ, σ, 0.75)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 20: interpret quartiles in context as cutpoints with 25% of observations at or below Q1 and 75% at or below Q3.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Quartiles): Which interpretation of quartiles is correct?
A. 25% of observations are at or below Q1 and 75% are at or below Q3
B. 50% of observations are at or below Q1 and 50% are at or below Q3
C. 75% of observations are at or above Q1 and 25% are at or above Q3
D. Q1 and Q3 are the minimum and maximum values
**Answer**: A

#### LO 21: define the interquartile range (IQR) as Q3 − Q1.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Interquartile Range (IQR)): The interquartile range (IQR) is defined as ______.
**Answer**: Q3 − Q1

#### LO 22: calculate the IQR from computed values of Q1 and Q3 for a normal distribution.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Interquartile Range (IQR)): Given Q1 and Q3 for a normal distribution, the IQR equals:
A. Q3 − Q1
B. Q1 + Q3
C. Q1 − Q3
D. (Q3 + Q1) / 2
**Answer**: A

## Section: seg_83

#### LO 1: Use a stratified sampling method by lap (races 1 to 20) with a random number generator to select six lap times from each stratum.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Stratified Sampling): Which choice best describes the required stratified sampling method for laps 1–20?
A. Divide by lap (races 1 to 20) and use a random number generator to select six lap times from each stratum.
B. Select six lap times total from laps 1–20 using a random number generator.
C. Select six lap times from only one lap using a random number generator.
D. Divide by lap but choose six lap times deterministically (e.g., smallest times).
**Answer**: A

#### LO 2: Construct a histogram of the sampled lap times using five to six intervals with scaled axes.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Histogram): A histogram is a graph of the data using _____ with scaled axes.
**Answer**: five to six intervals

#### LO 3: Describe the general shape of the histogram and the smooth curve through the bar tops in one to two sentences.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Histogram): To describe the general shape and a smooth curve through the bar tops, the histogram should be constructed with which setup?
A. Five to six intervals with scaled axes.
B. Unscaled axes and variable-width intervals.
C. Exactly three intervals with unscaled axes.
D. More than ten intervals without scaling the axes.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 4: Calculate the sample mean x̄ of the selected lap times to two decimal places.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sample Mean (X̄)): In this context, what does x̄ represent?
A. The mean of the sample of lap times to be calculated.
B. The standard deviation of the sample of lap times.
C. The interquartile range reported from Q1 to Q3.
D. The parameters in the distribution notation X ~ Distribution(Parameters).
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: Calculate the sample standard deviation s of the selected lap times to two decimal places.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sample Standard Deviation (S)): In this context, what does s represent?
A. The standard deviation of the sample of lap times to be calculated.
B. The mean of the sample of lap times.
C. A graph of the data using five to six intervals with scaled axes.
D. The difference Q3 – Q1.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: Calculate the interquartile range IQR = Q3 – Q1 from the sampled lap times and report its endpoints.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Interquartile Range (IQR)): The interquartile range (IQR) is defined as _____ and is reported as going from Q1 to Q3.
**Answer**: Q3 – Q1

#### LO 7: State the IQR as going from Q1 to Q3 for the sampled lap times.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Interquartile Range (IQR)): How should the IQR be stated for the sampled lap times?
A. As going from Q1 to Q3.
B. As Q3 + Q1.
C. As from x̄ to s.
D. As from s to x̄.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 8: Compare the empirical IQR from the data to the IQR from the theoretical normal approximation.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Interquartile Range (IQR)): When comparing IQRs, which two quantities are being contrasted?
A. The empirical IQR from the data and the IQR from the theoretical normal approximation.
B. The sample mean and the number of histogram intervals.
C. The distribution notation and the empirical probability.
D. The sample standard deviation and the histogram’s scaled axes.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: State the approximate theoretical distribution using notation X ~ Normal(x̄, s) based on the sample.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Distribution Notation (X ~ Distribution(Parameters))): Distribution notation specifies the approximate theoretical distribution of X and its _____.
**Answer**: parameters

#### LO 10: Use distribution notation X ~ Normal(x̄, s) when reporting model-based percentiles and probabilities for lap times.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Distribution Notation (X ~ Distribution(Parameters))): When reporting model-based percentiles and probabilities for lap times, which notation should you use?
A. X ~ Normal(x̄, s).
B. x̄ alone without any distribution.
C. An unlabeled histogram.
D. "Q1 to Q3" only.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: Use the theoretical normal distribution to compute percentiles (15th, 85th), the median, and probabilities such as P(X > 130) for lap times.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Theoretical Distribution): Under the theoretical distribution, a model is used to compute _____ and probabilities for lap times, using a normal approximation based on sample data.
**Answer**: percentiles

#### LO 12: Explain the meaning of a percentile (such as the 85th) in the context of lap times under the theoretical distribution.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Theoretical Distribution): Under the theoretical distribution, computations are based on a _____ derived from the sample data.
**Answer**: normal approximation

#### LO 13: Use a normal approximation based on the sample mean and standard deviation to model the lap time distribution.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Normal Approximation): What modeling approach should be used to approximate the lap time distribution?
A. Use a normal distribution based on the sample data to approximate the theoretical distribution.
B. Use only the histogram’s bar heights without any model.
C. Use only the IQR reported from Q1 to Q3.
D. Use only empirical probabilities from the data.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 14: Compare results from the normal approximation to empirical results for lap times.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Normal Approximation): What comparison is required when evaluating the normal approximation?
A. Compare results from the normal approximation to empirical results for lap times.
B. Compare x̄ to s.
C. Compare the number of histogram intervals to the IQR.
D. Compare parameters in the notation to the histogram axes.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: Calculate the empirical probability that a randomly chosen lap time exceeds a specified value (e.g., 130 seconds) and round to four decimal places.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Empirical Probability): An empirical probability is calculated from the _____.
**Answer**: collected lap time data

#### LO 16: Compare the empirical probability from the sample to the corresponding probability from the theoretical normal model.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Empirical Probability): What are you comparing when contrasting empirical and model-based probabilities?
A. The empirical probability from the sample and the corresponding probability from the theoretical normal model.
B. The sample mean and the interquartile range.
C. The histogram intervals and the scaled axes.
D. The distribution notation and the sample standard deviation.
**Answer**: A
