## Section: seg_115

#### LO 1: Define the null hypothesis (H0) as a statement of no difference between sample and population means or proportions (difference equals 0) that uses a symbol with equality.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Null Hypothesis (H0)): The null hypothesis (H0) states that the ______ and includes an equality symbol.
**Answer**: difference equals 0

#### LO 2: Differentiate H0 from Ha by noting that H0 asserts no difference and includes an equality symbol, whereas Ha contradicts H0 and excludes equality.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Null Hypothesis (H0)): Which statement correctly differentiates H0 from Ha?
A. H0 asserts no difference and includes an equality symbol; Ha contradicts H0 and excludes equality.
B. H0 asserts a difference and excludes equality; Ha asserts no difference and includes equality.
C. Both H0 and Ha include equality symbols.
D. H0 contradicts Ha and excludes equality; Ha includes equality.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: Define the alternative hypothesis (Ha) as a claim about the population that contradicts H0 and never includes an equality symbol, using ≠, >, or < as appropriate.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Alternative Hypothesis (Ha)): The alternative hypothesis (Ha) ______ and uses ≠, >, or < as appropriate.
**Answer**: never includes an equality symbol

#### LO 4: State the two possible decisions in hypothesis testing: reject H0 if the sample information favors Ha, or do not reject H0 if the evidence is insufficient to reject H0.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Decision Options In Hypothesis Testing): What are the two possible decisions in hypothesis testing?
A. Accept H0 or accept Ha
B. Reject H0 or do not reject H0
C. Prove H0 or disprove H0
D. Support Ha or support H0
**Answer**: B

#### LO 5: Explain when to reject H0 versus when to not reject H0 based on whether the sample evidence favors Ha or is insufficient.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Decision Options In Hypothesis Testing): When should you reject H0 versus not reject H0?
A. Reject H0 if sample information favors Ha; otherwise, do not reject H0.
B. Reject H0 only when evidence is insufficient; otherwise accept H0.
C. Always reject H0 when Ha uses '>'.
D. Do not reject H0 if the sample favors Ha; reject H0 when evidence is insufficient.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: Translate verbal test statements into symbolic hypotheses by placing equality only in H0 and using ≠, >, or < in Ha according to the test wording.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Symbol Conventions For Hypotheses): When translating verbal test statements to symbols, where should the equality symbol appear?
A. Only in H0; Ha uses ≠, >, or < according to wording.
B. Only in Ha; H0 uses ≠, >, or < according to wording.
C. In both H0 and Ha for clarity.
D. In neither H0 nor Ha.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: State that H0 uses symbols with equality (=, ≤, ≥) while Ha uses ≠, <, or >, depending on the test wording.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Symbol Conventions For Hypotheses): In hypothesis notation, H0 uses symbols with ______, while Ha uses ≠, <, or >.
**Answer**: equality (=, ≥, ≤)

#### LO 8: Explain why using '=' in H0 is acceptable even when Ha uses '>' or '<' because the decision is only to reject or not reject H0.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Acceptable Equality Notation In H0): Using '=' in H0 is acceptable even when Ha uses '>' or '<' because the decision is only to ______.
**Answer**: reject or not reject H0

#### LO 9: State that including '=' in H0 is acceptable even when Ha is one-sided ('>' or '<').
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Acceptable Equality Notation In H0): In a one-sided test where Ha is '>' or '<', is it acceptable to include '=' in H0?
A. Yes; it's acceptable because the decision is only to reject or not reject H0.
B. No; H0 must use ≤ or ≥ instead of '=' in one-sided tests.
C. No; H0 must mirror Ha with the opposite inequality and no equality.
D. Yes; but only if Ha uses ≠.
**Answer**: A


---
## Section: seg_117

#### LO 1: Define Type I error as rejecting the null hypothesis when the null hypothesis is true, with probability α.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Type I Error): A Type I error is _____.
**Answer**: rejecting the null hypothesis when the null hypothesis is true

#### LO 2: Describe real-world contexts and consequences of committing a Type I error in hypothesis testing.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Type I Error): Which scenario best illustrates a Type I error and its consequence?
A. Concluding an effect exists when it truly does not, leading to unnecessary action.
B. Failing to detect a real effect, missing a needed action.
C. Correctly rejecting a false null hypothesis, taking appropriate action.
D. Correctly not rejecting a true null hypothesis, avoiding unnecessary action.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: Define Type II error as not rejecting the null hypothesis when the null hypothesis is false, with probability β.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Type II Error): A Type II error is _____.
**Answer**: not rejecting the null hypothesis when the null hypothesis is false

#### LO 4: Describe real-world contexts and consequences of committing a Type II error in hypothesis testing.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Type II Error): Which outcome describes a Type II error and a likely consequence?
A. Detecting an effect that doesn't exist, triggering unnecessary changes.
B. Not detecting a real effect, resulting in a missed opportunity to act.
C. Correctly concluding no effect when there is none, maintaining the status quo.
D. Correctly detecting a real effect and acting on it.
**Answer**: B

#### LO 5: Define alpha (α) as the probability of a Type I error—the probability of rejecting a true null hypothesis.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Alpha (α)): Alpha (α) is the _____.
**Answer**: probability of a Type I error

#### LO 6: Describe how α measures the chance of a Type I error and why it is chosen to be small.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Alpha (α)): Why is α typically chosen to be small?
A. To limit the chance of rejecting a true null hypothesis (Type I error).
B. To increase the chance of a Type II error as much as possible.
C. To maximize power by definition because α = 1 − β.
D. Because α equals β in all well-designed tests.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: Define beta (β) as the probability of a Type II error—the probability of not rejecting a false null hypothesis.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Beta (β)): Beta (β) is the _____.
**Answer**: probability of a Type II error

#### LO 8: Describe how β measures the chance of a Type II error and its relationship to test power (1 − β).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Beta (β)): Which statement correctly relates β and test power?
A. Power equals 1 − β, so decreasing β increases power.
B. Power equals β, so increasing β increases power.
C. β measures the chance of a Type I error, so it is unrelated to power.
D. Power and β are unrelated quantities in hypothesis testing.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: Define the power of a test as the probability of rejecting the null hypothesis when it is false, equal to 1 − β.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Power Of The Test): The power of a test is the _____.
**Answer**: probability of rejecting the null hypothesis when it is false

#### LO 10: Describe why high power is desirable and how increasing sample size can increase power.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Power Of The Test): Why is high power desirable, and how can it be increased?
A. High power means a higher chance of rejecting false nulls; increasing sample size can increase power.
B. High power mainly reduces Type I errors and typically decreases with larger samples.
C. High power equals choosing a small α; sample size does not affect power.
D. High power means lower β but cannot be influenced by study design choices like sample size.
**Answer**: A


---
## Section: seg_119

#### LO 1: identify when a Student's t-distribution is appropriate for testing a single population mean, including an unknown population standard deviation, a simple random sample, and approximate normality or sufficiently large sample size
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Student's T-Distribution): Which scenario calls for a Student's t-distribution to test a single population mean?
A. The population standard deviation is unknown; a simple random sample; the population is approximately normal (or the sample is sufficiently large).
B. The population standard deviation is known; a simple random sample; the population is normal or the sample is large.
C. The sample is a convenience sample; the population standard deviation is unknown; the sample size is small and non-normal.
D. The sample size is large but the data are counts with two outcomes per trial.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 2: use a Student's t-distribution and the sample standard deviation to perform a hypothesis test for a single population mean under the required assumptions
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Student's T-Distribution): When the population standard deviation is unknown and assumptions hold, which test and spread measure should you use to test a single population mean?
A. Student's t-test with the sample standard deviation s
B. z-test with the population standard deviation σ
C. z-test with the sample standard deviation s
D. Chi-square test with the sample variance
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: identify scenarios where a z-test for a single population mean is appropriate, given a known population standard deviation, a simple random sample, and either a normal population or a sufficiently large sample
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Z-Test For A Single Population Mean): Which scenario is appropriate for a z-test for a single population mean?
A. Known population standard deviation; simple random sample; normal population or sufficiently large sample size
B. Unknown population standard deviation; convenience sample; skewed small-sample distribution
C. Unknown population standard deviation; simple random sample; small sample from a non-normal population
D. Known population standard deviation; dependent trials with success/failure outcomes
**Answer**: A

#### LO 4: describe the requirements for using the normal distribution to test a single population mean, including a simple random sample, a known population standard deviation, and normality or large-sample conditions
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Z-Test For A Single Population Mean): For a z-test of a single population mean, the ______ must be known.
**Answer**: population standard deviation

#### LO 5: differentiate between conditions that call for a z-test and those that call for a t-test when testing a single population mean
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Z-Test For A Single Population Mean): You have a simple random sample of n = 18 from an approximately normal population, and the population standard deviation is unknown. Which test should you use to test the population mean?
A. Student's t-test for a single population mean
B. z-test for a single population mean
C. Normal test for a single population proportion
D. A test based on the normal distribution for counts only
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: describe how the normal distribution is used to test a single population proportion when binomial conditions hold and np > 5 and nq > 5
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Normal Distribution For A Single Population Proportion): When can you use the normal distribution to test a single population proportion?
A. When binomial conditions hold and both np > 5 and nq > 5
B. When the population standard deviation is known and the sample mean is approximately normal
C. Only when p = 0.5 regardless of sample size
D. When trials are not independent but the sample size is large
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: state the normal-approximation criteria np > 5 and nq > 5 for testing a single population proportion
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Normal Distribution For A Single Population Proportion): State the criteria for the normal approximation in a single-proportion test: _____.
**Answer**: np > 5 and nq > 5

#### LO 8: calculate np and nq for a given sample to check whether the normal approximation is appropriate for a single-proportion test
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Normal Distribution For A Single Population Proportion): Given n = 60, p = 0.20, and q = 0.80, what are np and nq?
A. np = 12, nq = 48
B. np = 48, nq = 12
C. np = 30, nq = 30
D. np = 5, nq = 55
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: define the binomial conditions as a fixed number of independent trials with success or failure outcomes and the same probability of success p
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Binomial Conditions): The binomial conditions require a fixed number of ______.
**Answer**: independent trials

#### LO 10: define the population mean μ as the parameter of interest in hypothesis tests of a single mean
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Population Mean (μ)): In single-mean hypothesis tests, μ is the _____ representing the population mean.
**Answer**: parameter representing the population mean

#### LO 11: describe how the population mean μ is estimated by the sample mean x̄ when testing a single population mean
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Population Mean (μ)): When testing a single population mean, which statistic estimates μ?
A. x̄ (sample mean)
B. p′ (sample proportion)
C. σ (population standard deviation)
D. q = 1 − p
**Answer**: A

#### LO 12: define the sample mean x̄ as the point estimate of the population mean μ used in tests of a single mean
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sample Mean (x̄) As Point Estimate): The sample mean x̄ is the _____ of μ used in testing a single population mean.
**Answer**: estimated value of μ

#### LO 13: define the population proportion p as the parameter of interest in tests of a single proportion
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Population Proportion (p)): In tests of a single proportion, p is the _____ representing the population proportion.
**Answer**: parameter representing the population proportion

#### LO 14: describe how p relates to q = 1 − p and serves as the mean of the sampling distribution of p′ under the normal approximation
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Population Proportion (p)): Under the normal approximation for a single proportion, which statement is correct?
A. q = 1 − p, and p is the mean of the sampling distribution of p′
B. q = p, and q is the mean of the sampling distribution of p′
C. q = 1 + p, and p is the variance of the sampling distribution of p′
D. q = 1 − p, and q is the median of the sampling distribution of p′
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: define the sample proportion p′ as x/n, where x is the number of successes and n is the sample size
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sample Proportion (p′)): The sample proportion p′ is defined as _____.
**Answer**: x/n

#### LO 16: calculate the sample proportion p′ from observed successes x and sample size n
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sample Proportion (p′)): If x = 18 successes in a sample of n = 60, what is p′?
A. 0.30
B. 0.18
C. 0.60
D. 3.33
**Answer**: A

#### LO 17: identify the need for a simple random sample when conducting hypothesis tests for a single mean or a single proportion
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Simple Random Sample): Hypothesis tests for a single mean or a single proportion require a _____.
**Answer**: simple random sample


---
## Section: seg_121

#### LO 1: define a rare event in hypothesis testing as a sample outcome that would be very unlikely if the null hypothesis were true.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Rare Event): A rare event is a sample outcome that would be ______ if the null hypothesis were true, prompting doubt about the null assumption.
**Answer**: very unlikely

#### LO 2: explain why observing a rare event leads you to doubt the null hypothesis.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Rare Event): Why does observing a rare event lead you to doubt H0?
A. Because such an outcome is very unlikely if H0 is true, prompting doubt about the null assumption.
B. Because α always equals the p-value when H0 is true.
C. Because H0 is always false in practice.
D. Because the significance level determines the sample size.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: define the null hypothesis as an assumption about a population property to be tested.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Null Hypothesis (H0)): The null hypothesis (H0) is ______ that is subjected to testing.
**Answer**: an assumption about a population property

#### LO 4: interpret the role of the null hypothesis in framing the test and the meaning of the p-value.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Null Hypothesis (H0)): Which best describes H0 and the p-value?
A. H0 is an assumption about a population property to be tested; the p-value is the probability, if H0 is true, of results as extreme or more extreme than those observed.
B. H0 is a directional claim; the p-value is the preset probability of a Type I error.
C. H0 is the probability of a rare event; the p-value is the population property being tested.
D. H0 is a conclusion about Ha; the p-value measures sample size.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: translate verbal claims (for example, 'is more than') into a symbolic alternative hypothesis that contradicts H0.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Alternative Hypothesis (Ha)): A verbal claim says a parameter "is more than" a stated value. Which Ha aligns with this?
A. Ha: the parameter is greater than the stated value.
B. Ha: the parameter equals the stated value.
C. Ha: the parameter is less than the stated value.
D. Ha: the parameter is not equal to the stated value.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: state an appropriate alternative hypothesis that directly contradicts the stated null hypothesis.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Alternative Hypothesis (Ha)): If H0 states the parameter equals a specific value, which Ha directly contradicts H0?
A. Ha: the parameter is not equal to that value.
B. Ha: the parameter equals that value.
C. Ha: the parameter is greater than that value only.
D. Ha: the parameter is less than that value only.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: calculate a p-value from sample results as the probability, under H0, of obtaining results as extreme or more extreme than those observed.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: p-Value): The p-value is the ______ that, if the null hypothesis is true, results from another randomly selected sample would be as extreme or more extreme than those observed.
**Answer**: probability

#### LO 8: compare a computed p-value with α to assess the strength of evidence against H0.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: p-Value): How does the p-value relate to evidence against H0?
A. A smaller p-value indicates stronger evidence against H0.
B. A larger p-value indicates stronger evidence against H0.
C. Evidence strength is determined solely by α, not the p-value.
D. The p-value must equal α to indicate strong evidence.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: define the significance level α as the preset probability of a Type I error used as a decision threshold.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Significance Level (Alpha, α)): The significance level α is the ______ used as a threshold for decision-making.
**Answer**: preset probability of a Type I error

#### LO 10: compare α and the p-value to decide whether results are statistically significant.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Significance Level (Alpha, α)): When are results statistically significant?
A. When the p-value is less than α.
B. When the p-value is greater than α.
C. When α is less than or equal to the p-value.
D. When the p-value equals α.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: define a Type I error as rejecting the null hypothesis when the null hypothesis is true.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Type I Error): A Type I error is ______ the null hypothesis when the null hypothesis is true.
**Answer**: rejecting

#### LO 12: describe how the chosen α quantifies the chance of making a Type I error.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Type I Error): What does the chosen α quantify?
A. The probability of making a Type I error.
B. The probability of making a Type II error.
C. The exact p-value for the test.
D. The direction of the alternative hypothesis.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 13: state the decision rule: if α > p-value, reject H0; if α ≤ p-value, do not reject H0.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: p-Value Decision Rule): Which statement is the correct p-value decision rule?
A. If α > p-value, reject H0; if α ≤ p-value, do not reject H0.
B. If α < p-value, reject H0; if α ≥ p-value, do not reject H0.
C. If α = p-value, always reject H0.
D. If α ≥ p-value, always do not reject H0.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 14: compare cases where α is greater than, less than, or equal to the p-value to determine the correct decision about H0.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: p-Value Decision Rule): Which comparison leads to rejecting H0?
A. α > p-value
B. α < p-value
C. α = p-value
D. Decision does not depend on comparing α and the p-value
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: use the p-value decision rule to make and report a reject or do-not-reject decision for H0.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: p-Value Decision Rule): Given p-value = 0.02 and α = 0.05, what is the decision?
A. Reject H0.
B. Do not reject H0.
C. Accept H0 as true.
D. Increase α until the result is not significant.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 16: define statistical significance as occurring when p-value < α and nonsignificance when p-value ≥ α.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Statistical Significance): Results are statistically significant when ______ and not significant when p-value ≥ α.
**Answer**: p-value < α

#### LO 17: compare significant versus not significant outcomes based on the relationship between p-value and α.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Statistical Significance): Which relationship indicates results are not statistically significant?
A. p-value ≥ α
B. p-value < α
C. α > p-value
D. p-value is extremely small
**Answer**: A

#### LO 18: interpret a do-not-reject decision as insufficient evidence to cast serious doubt on H0 rather than proof that H0 is true.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Interpretation Of Non-Rejection): Not rejecting H0 does not mean H0 is true; it indicates ______.
**Answer**: insufficient evidence to cast serious doubt on H0

#### LO 19: state that non-rejection of H0 indicates inadequate evidence for Ha in the context of the problem.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Interpretation Of Non-Rejection): If you do not reject H0, which statement is appropriate?
A. There is inadequate evidence for Ha.
B. H0 has been proven true.
C. α must have been chosen incorrectly.
D. The p-value automatically equals α.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 20: interpret statistical results by writing a conclusion about the hypotheses in simple, non-technical terms for the given context.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Conclusion Statement): If results are statistically significant (p-value < α), which plain-language conclusion fits?
A. There is sufficient evidence to doubt the null assumption in favor of the alternative.
B. The null hypothesis is proven false beyond any doubt.
C. There is no need to compare the p-value to α.
D. The result proves the alternative hypothesis is true.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 21: state a clear, context-specific conclusion that aligns with the p-value–α comparison and the hypotheses.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Conclusion Statement): Given p-value = 0.12 and α = 0.05, which conclusion aligns with the comparison and the hypotheses?
A. Do not reject H0; there is insufficient evidence for Ha.
B. Reject H0; there is strong evidence for Ha.
C. Accept H0 as true.
D. The results are statistically significant at α = 0.05.
**Answer**: A


---
## Section: seg_123

#### LO 1: define the level of significance (α) as the preset probability threshold chosen before data collection, commonly 0.05 when unspecified.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Level Of Significance (Alpha)): The level of significance (α) is the preconceived threshold chosen before data collection; if unspecified, a common standard is ____.
**Answer**: 0.05

#### LO 2: compare preset α levels (e.g., 0.01 versus 0.05) in terms of how stringent they are for rejecting H0.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Level Of Significance (Alpha)): Which preset α is more stringent for rejecting H0?
A. 0.10
B. 0.05
C. 0.01
D. 0.20
**Answer**: C

#### LO 3: calculate the p-value as the appropriate left-tail, right-tail, or two-tailed area under the test distribution for a given sample result.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: P-Value): For a right-tailed test, a sample yields z = 0. What is the p-value (area in the right tail)?
A. 0.50
B. 1.00
C. 0.00
D. 0.05
**Answer**: A

#### LO 4: interpret the p-value as the probability, assuming H0 is true, of observing a result as extreme or more than the sample.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: P-Value): The p-value is the probability, assuming H0 is true, of obtaining a result as ______ or more than the sample.
**Answer**: extreme or more

#### LO 5: classify a hypothesis test as left-tailed, right-tailed, or two-tailed based on the form of Ha.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Left-, Right-, And Two-Tailed Tests): Given Ha: μ < μ0, classify the test.
A. Right-tailed
B. Left-tailed
C. Two-tailed
D. Not a valid test
**Answer**: B

#### LO 6: identify the tail or tails associated with the p-value from a stated alternative hypothesis or p-value sketch.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Left-, Right-, And Two-Tailed Tests): For Ha: p ≠ p0, the p-value is associated with which tail(s)?
A. Left tail only
B. Right tail only
C. Both tails
D. Neither tail
**Answer**: C

#### LO 7: classify the test direction from the alternative hypothesis and recognize that it contains no equal sign.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Alternative Hypothesis): Which alternative hypothesis is valid and indicates a right-tailed test?
A. μ > μ0
B. μ ≥ μ0
C. μ = μ0
D. μ ≤ μ0
**Answer**: A

#### LO 8: formulate an alternative hypothesis using <, >, or ≠ to reflect the research claim without including an equal sign.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Alternative Hypothesis): A research claim states the mean is less than 10. Choose a correct Ha without an equal sign.
A. μ ≤ 10
B. μ < 10
C. μ = 10
D. μ ≥ 10
**Answer**: B

#### LO 9: compare the p-value to α to decide whether to reject or not reject H0.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Decision Rule Using P-Values): Given p-value = 0.03 and α = 0.05, what is the decision?
A. Reject H0
B. Do not reject H0
C. Accept H0
D. Increase α
**Answer**: A

#### LO 10: state the decision rule that H0 is rejected when p-value < α and not rejected when p-value > α.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Decision Rule Using P-Values): According to the decision rule using p-values, reject H0 when ______.
**Answer**: p-value < α

#### LO 11: use the comparison of p-value and α to reach a conclusion in a hypothesis test.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Decision Rule Using P-Values): With p-value = 0.12 and α = 0.05, conclude:
A. Reject H0
B. Do not reject H0
C. H0 is true
D. Lower the p-value
**Answer**: B

#### LO 12: explain how smaller p-values increase confidence in rejecting H0 and larger p-values increase confidence in not rejecting H0.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Evidence Strength From P-Value): Smaller p-values provide more confidence in ______ H0.
**Answer**: rejecting

#### LO 13: identify the parameter value used in test calculations (such as μ or p) as coming from H0 and not from the sample.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Null Hypothesis Parameter Value): When computing a test statistic, which parameter value is used?
A. The sample estimate (x̄ or p′)
B. The value specified in H0 (μ0 or p0)
C. A midpoint of sample and H0 values
D. Any plausible value
**Answer**: B

#### LO 14: use the null hypothesis parameter value when computing the test statistic or p-value.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Null Hypothesis Parameter Value): In a one-proportion test statistic, which value goes into the standard deviation formula pq/n?
A. p′ from the sample
B. p0 from H0
C. Any p between 0 and 1
D. q only
**Answer**: B

#### LO 15: define a Type I error as rejecting the null hypothesis when the null hypothesis is true.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Type I Error): A Type I error is ______ the null hypothesis when the null hypothesis is true.
**Answer**: rejecting

#### LO 16: explain the consequences of a Type I error and why a small α may be chosen to limit it in high-stakes contexts.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Type I Error): Why might a small α be chosen to limit Type I error?
A. It makes rejecting H0 harder, reducing false rejections
B. It increases the p-value
C. It guarantees H0 is true
D. It eliminates Type II error
**Answer**: A

#### LO 17: define a Type II error as not rejecting the null hypothesis when the null hypothesis is false.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Type II Error): A Type II error is ______ the null hypothesis when the null hypothesis is false.
**Answer**: not rejecting

#### LO 18: identify when a z-test for a single mean is appropriate (population standard deviation known and normal model applies).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Z-Test For A Single Mean): When is a z-test for a single mean appropriate?
A. σ known and normal model applies
B. σ unknown with small sample
C. Non-normal data with unknown σ
D. Proportion data
**Answer**: A

#### LO 19: calculate the test statistic and p-value for a single-mean z-test using μ0, σ, x̄, and n.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Z-Test For A Single Mean): For μ0=100, σ=20, n=16, and x̄=100 in a right-tailed z-test, compute z and the p-value.
A. z = 0, p-value = 0.50
B. z = 1, p-value = 0.16
C. z = 0, p-value = 0.05
D. z = 2, p-value = 0.02
**Answer**: A

#### LO 20: interpret the z-test p-value to make a conclusion at a stated level of significance.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Z-Test For A Single Mean): If a single-mean z-test yields p-value = 0.08 at α = 0.05, conclude:
A. Reject H0
B. Do not reject H0
C. Accept Ha as true
D. Increase α to 0.08
**Answer**: B

#### LO 21: identify that the sampling distribution of X̄ is normal with mean μ and standard deviation σ/√n when σ is known.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sampling Distribution Of The Sample Mean (Known σ)): When σ is known, X̄ is ______ with mean μ and standard deviation σ/√n.
**Answer**: normal

#### LO 22: calculate probabilities for X̄ using N(μ, σ/√n) to obtain a p-value for a mean test.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sampling Distribution Of The Sample Mean (Known σ)): Given μ=50, σ=10, n=25, and observed X̄=50 in a right-tailed mean test, what is the p-value using N(μ, σ/√n)?
A. 0.50
B. 0.05
C. 1.00
D. 0.00
**Answer**: A

#### LO 23: identify when a t-test for a single mean is appropriate (population standard deviation unknown and data from a normal distribution).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: T-Test For A Single Mean): When is a t-test for a single mean appropriate?
A. σ unknown and data from a normal distribution
B. σ known with large n
C. For proportions only
D. Any non-normal data regardless of n
**Answer**: A

#### LO 24: calculate the t test statistic, degrees of freedom, and p-value for a single-mean t-test from sample data.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: T-Test For A Single Mean): For H0: μ=10 with sample x̄=10, s=2, n=10 (normal data), compute t, df, and the right-tailed p-value.
A. t=0, df=9, p-value=0.50
B. t=1, df=10, p-value=0.16
C. t=0, df=10, p-value=0.05
D. t=2, df=9, p-value=0.02
**Answer**: A

#### LO 25: interpret the t-test p-value to make a conclusion at a stated level of significance.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: T-Test For A Single Mean): A single-mean t-test gives p-value = 0.03 at α=0.05. Conclusion?
A. Reject H0
B. Do not reject H0
C. Increase sample size
D. Accept H0
**Answer**: A

#### LO 26: calculate the degrees of freedom for a single-mean t-test as n − 1.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Degrees Of Freedom (t-Test)): For a single-mean t-test, the degrees of freedom are df = ____.
**Answer**: n − 1

#### LO 27: state the degrees of freedom for a single-mean t-test given the sample size.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Degrees Of Freedom (t-Test)): What are the degrees of freedom for a single-mean t-test with n=12?
A. 12
B. 11
C. 10
D. 13
**Answer**: B

#### LO 28: calculate the p-value for a one-proportion z-test from p0, n, and x (or p′).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: One-Proportion Z-Test): For H0: p=0.40 with n=100 and x=40 in a right-tailed one-proportion z-test, what is the p-value?
A. 0.50
B. 0.05
C. 1.00
D. 0.00
**Answer**: A

#### LO 29: state appropriate null and alternative hypotheses for a single population proportion test using p.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: One-Proportion Z-Test): State appropriate H0 and Ha for testing whether p differs from 0.60.
A. H0: p = 0.60; Ha: p ≠ 0.60
B. H0: p ≠ 0.60; Ha: p = 0.60
C. H0: p < 0.60; Ha: p ≤ 0.60
D. H0: p ≥ 0.60; Ha: p ≤ 0.60
**Answer**: A

#### LO 30: interpret the one-proportion z-test p-value to make a conclusion at a stated level of significance.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: One-Proportion Z-Test): A one-proportion z-test yields p-value = 0.12 at α=0.10. Conclusion?
A. Reject H0
B. Do not reject H0
C. Accept Ha
D. Reduce n to lower p-value
**Answer**: B

#### LO 31: state that P′ ~ N(p, pq/n) with q = 1 − p under the normal approximation.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sampling Distribution Of The Sample Proportion): Under the normal approximation, P′ ~ N(____, pq/n) with q = 1 − p.
**Answer**: p

#### LO 32: calculate tail probabilities for P′ using mean p and standard deviation √(pq/n) to compute a p-value.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sampling Distribution Of The Sample Proportion): With p=0.30, q=0.70, n=100, and observed P′=0.30 in a right-tailed test, what is the p-value using mean p and SD √(pq/n)?
A. 0.50
B. 0.05
C. 1.00
D. 0.00
**Answer**: A

#### LO 33: define the sample proportion as p′ = x/n.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sample Proportion (P′)): The sample proportion is defined as p′ = ____.
**Answer**: x/n

#### LO 34: calculate p′ from given counts of successes and the sample size.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sample Proportion (P′)): Compute p′ when x=25 successes out of n=100.
A. 0.20
B. 0.25
C. 0.75
D. 0.50
**Answer**: B

#### LO 35: identify the conditions for using the one-proportion z-test: sufficiently large np and nq, two independent outcomes, and a fixed probability of success.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Conditions For One-Proportion Z-Test): One-proportion z-test conditions include ______, two independent outcomes, and a fixed probability of success.
**Answer**: sufficiently large np and nq

#### LO 36: calculate np and nq to check that the normal approximation conditions are met.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Conditions For One-Proportion Z-Test): For n=200 and p=0.3, compute np and nq to check the normal approximation.
A. np=60, nq=140
B. np=140, nq=60
C. np=70, nq=130
D. np=30, nq=170
**Answer**: A

## Section: seg_125

#### LO 1: define a hypothesis as a statement about a population parameter with a null and an alternative
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Hypothesis): A hypothesis is _____; the statement assumed true is the null hypothesis and the contradictory statement is the alternative hypothesis.
**Answer**: a statement about a population parameter

#### LO 2: formulate null and alternative hypotheses for a claim about a single mean or a single proportion
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Hypothesis): For the claim "the population proportion p equals 0.40," which set of hypotheses is appropriate?
A. H0: p = 0.40; H1: p ≠ 0.40
B. H0: p ≠ 0.40; H1: p = 0.40
C. H0: p < 0.40; H1: p ≥ 0.40
D. H0: p ≤ 0.40; H1: p > 0.40
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: define the null hypothesis as the statement assumed true that includes equality (=, ≤, or ≥)
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Null Hypothesis): The null hypothesis is the hypothesis _____ and tested; it must include equality.
**Answer**: assumed to be true

#### LO 4: state the alternative hypothesis using <, >, or ≠ to contradict the null
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Alternative Hypothesis): The alternative hypothesis is the _____ to the null; written with <, >, or ≠.
**Answer**: contradictory claim

#### LO 5: classify a hypothesis test as left-tailed, right-tailed, or two-tailed based on the alternative hypothesis
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Alternative Hypothesis): If the alternative hypothesis is H1: μ > μ0, the test is:
A. left-tailed
B. right-tailed
C. two-tailed
D. cannot be determined
**Answer**: B

#### LO 6: interpret the test outcome by comparing the p-value to α and writing a contextual conclusion
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Hypothesis Testing): Given p-value = 0.03 and α = 0.05, what is the decision and conclusion?
A. Reject H0; conclude evidence supports the alternative
B. Reject H0; conclude evidence supports the null
C. Do not reject H0; conclude evidence supports the alternative
D. Do not reject H0; conclude evidence is insufficient to support the alternative
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: define the level of significance α as the preset probability of a Type I error
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Level Of Significance): The level of significance α is the probability of a Type I error (_____); it is preset before testing.
**Answer**: rejecting a true null

#### LO 8: compare α to a p-value to decide whether to reject or not reject H0
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Level Of Significance): If α = 0.01 and the p-value = 0.04, what is the decision?
A. Reject H0
B. Do not reject H0
C. Increase α until rejection occurs
D. Conclude the null is true
**Answer**: B

#### LO 9: define the p-value as the probability of observing the sample result or more extreme assuming H0 is true
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: p-Value): The p-value is the probability of the observed result (or more extreme) occurring by chance assuming _____ is true.
**Answer**: the null hypothesis

#### LO 10: interpret smaller p-values as stronger evidence against the null hypothesis in context
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: p-Value): Smaller p-values indicate _____ against the null hypothesis.
**Answer**: stronger evidence

#### LO 11: define a Type I error as rejecting a true null hypothesis
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Type I Error): A Type I error is _____.
**Answer**: rejecting the null hypothesis when it is true

#### LO 12: differentiate between Type I and Type II errors in a given testing scenario
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Type I Error): A test rejects H0, but in truth H0 is true. What occurred?
A. Type I error
B. Type II error
C. No error
D. High power
**Answer**: A

#### LO 13: define a Type II error as not rejecting a false null hypothesis
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Type II Error): A Type II error is _____.
**Answer**: not rejecting the null hypothesis when it is false

#### LO 14: define the power of a test as 1 − β
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Power Of The Test): The power of a test is _____.
**Answer**: 1 − β

#### LO 15: calculate the power of a test given β or related information
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Power Of The Test): If β = 0.35, what is the power of the test?
A. 0.35
B. 0.50
C. 0.65
D. 1.35
**Answer**: C

#### LO 16: interpret power as the likelihood of correctly supporting a true alternative hypothesis and explain why higher power is desirable
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Power Of The Test): Which best describes power and why higher power is desirable?
A. It is the likelihood of correctly supporting a true alternative hypothesis; higher power means we are more likely to detect true effects.
B. It is the likelihood of rejecting any null; higher power increases Type I errors.
C. It is the likelihood of failing to reject false nulls; higher power makes misses more common.
D. It is the likelihood of observing a rare event; higher power reduces α.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 17: define the binomial distribution as the number of successes in n independent Bernoulli trials with success probability p
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Binomial Distribution): A binomial distribution is a discrete distribution for the number of _____ in a fixed number of independent Bernoulli trials with success probability p.
**Answer**: successes

#### LO 18: describe the conditions for a binomial model and its parameters and probability formula
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Binomial Distribution): Which set of conditions matches a binomial model?
A. Fixed number of independent Bernoulli trials with constant p; count successes.
B. Trials with changing p and dependence allowed; count time until first success.
C. Continuous outcomes with mean μ and standard deviation σ.
D. Any repeated measurements with varying probabilities and a sample size determined by the data.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 19: define the normal distribution N(μ, σ) and the standard normal N(0, 1)
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Normal Distribution): A normal distribution is a continuous distribution with mean μ and standard deviation σ; if μ = 0 and σ = 1, it is the _____.
**Answer**: standard normal distribution

#### LO 20: define the Student's t-distribution and its characteristics, including symmetry, heavier tails, and dependence on degrees of freedom
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Student's t-Distribution): The Student's t-distribution is continuous, symmetric, centered at zero, _____ than the normal, approaching the standard normal as sample size increases; each distribution is defined by degrees of freedom.
**Answer**: flatter

#### LO 21: define the Central Limit Theorem for sample means and sample sums
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Central Limit Theorem): For sufficiently large samples, the distributions of sample means and sample sums are approximately _____ regardless of population shape; the mean of sample means equals the population mean.
**Answer**: normal

#### LO 22: describe how for sufficiently large n, the distributions of X̄ and ΣX approximate normal with specified means and standard errors
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Central Limit Theorem): Which statement about X̄ and ΣX for sufficiently large n is correct?
A. They are approximately normal regardless of population shape; the mean of sample means equals the population mean.
B. They are exactly normal for any n; the mean of sample means exceeds the population mean.
C. They are approximately uniform; their variance equals σ/√n.
D. They remain skewed if the population is skewed; the mean of sample means is undefined.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 23: define the standard error of the mean as σ divided by the square root of n
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Standard Error Of The Mean): The standard error of the mean is the standard deviation of the sampling distribution of the sample mean, _____.
**Answer**: σ/√n

#### LO 24: calculate the standard error of the mean for given σ and sample size n
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Standard Error Of The Mean): If σ = 10 and n = 4, what is the standard error of the mean?
A. 5
B. 2.5
C. 10
D. 4
**Answer**: A

#### LO 25: compare the p-value to α to determine whether to reject H0 or not reject H0
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Decision Rule Using p-Value): With α = 0.01 and p-value = 0.02, what is the decision?
A. Reject H0
B. Do not reject H0
C. Increase α until H0 is rejected
D. Accept H0 as true
**Answer**: B

#### LO 26: use the rule α > p-value to reject H0 and α ≤ p-value to not reject H0 in making a decision
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Decision Rule Using p-Value): If α = 0.10 and the p-value = 0.08, what is the correct decision?
A. Reject H0
B. Do not reject H0
C. Raise α to 0.15
D. Conclude the null is true
**Answer**: A

#### LO 27: state the decision rule that relates α and the p-value
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Decision Rule Using p-Value): State the rule: If α > p-value, _____ H0; if α ≤ p-value, do not reject H0.
**Answer**: reject

#### LO 28: identify when a one-sample t-test is appropriate based on sampling conditions and unknown σ
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Student's t-Test For A Single Mean): Which scenario calls for a one-sample t-test for a single mean?
A. Simple random sample, approximately normal population (or large n), unknown σ
B. Simple random sample, large sample size, known σ
C. Nonrandom sample, unknown σ
D. Binomial data with np and nq > 5
**Answer**: A

#### LO 29: identify when a z-test for a single mean is appropriate given a simple random sample and known σ or large n
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Normal Test For A Single Mean): When is a z-test for a single mean appropriate?
A. Simple random sample with approximately normal population or large n and known σ
B. Simple random sample with unknown σ
C. Proportion data meeting binomial conditions
D. Nonrandom sample with known σ
**Answer**: A

#### LO 30: identify when a normal test for a single proportion is appropriate using binomial conditions with np and nq greater than five
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Normal Test For A Single Proportion): A normal test for a single proportion is appropriate when:
A. Binomial conditions hold and both np and nq are greater than five
B. The population is approximately normal with known σ
C. σ is unknown for a mean and n is small
D. Trials are dependent and p varies from trial to trial
**Answer**: A

#### LO 31: define a rare event as a low-probability outcome whose occurrence is important in hypothesis testing
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Rare Event): A rare event is an event with _____ that occurs; its occurrence informs decisions to reject or not reject a null hypothesis.
**Answer**: low probability

#### LO 32: define degrees of freedom for the t-distribution as one less than the number of data items
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Degrees Of Freedom): For the t-distribution, degrees of freedom are _____.
**Answer**: one less than the number of data items

#### LO 33: calculate degrees of freedom as n minus 1 for a one-sample t-test
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Degrees Of Freedom): In a one-sample t-test with n = 18, what are the degrees of freedom?
A. 18
B. 17
C. 16
D. 19
**Answer**: B

## Section: seg_129

#### LO 1: calculate the Welch two-sample t-test statistic, approximate degrees of freedom, and p-value for two independent samples with unknown and possibly unequal population standard deviations.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Aspin-Welch T-Test): For two independent samples with unknown and possibly unequal population standard deviations, which procedure provides a t statistic, approximate df, and p-value?
A. Aspin-Welch T-Test
B. Two-Sample t Test Statistic
C. Degrees Of Freedom For Welch’s Test
D. Non-Pooled Variances
**Answer**: A

#### LO 2: interpret the Welch two-sample t-test results (t, df, p-value) to decide whether the population means differ and state a context-specific conclusion.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Aspin-Welch T-Test): When interpreting Welch two-sample test results, which quantities are used to decide whether the population means differ?
A. t, df, and p-value
B. Only X̄1 − X̄2
C. Only sample standard deviations
D. Cohen’s d alone
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: list the assumptions: two independent simple random samples from distinct populations; normal population distributions for small samples; large samples need not be normal.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Assumptions For Independent Two-Sample Test): Fill in the blank: The independent two-sample test assumes ______ from distinct populations, and for small samples the population distributions should be normal.
**Answer**: Two independent simple random samples

#### LO 4: describe when population normality is required (small samples) versus less critical (large samples) and the need for independent simple random samples from distinct populations.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Assumptions For Independent Two-Sample Test): Fill in the blank: For large samples in the independent two-sample setting, population ______ is not required.
**Answer**: normality

#### LO 5: define the standard error of X̄1 − X̄2 as the estimated standard deviation of the difference between sample means.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Standard Error Of The Difference In Means): Fill in the blank: The standard error of X̄1 − X̄2 is the ______ of X̄1 − X̄2 computed from the sample standard deviations and sizes.
**Answer**: estimated standard deviation

#### LO 6: calculate the standard error of X̄1 − X̄2 as sqrt(s1^2/n1 + s2^2/n2).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Standard Error Of The Difference In Means): Which formula gives SE(X̄1 − X̄2) for two independent samples?
A. sqrt(s1^2/n1 + s2^2/n2)
B. sqrt(s1^2 + s2^2)
C. sqrt(s1/n1 + s2/n2)
D. (s1 + s2)/sqrt(n1 + n2)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: explain how the standard error quantifies variability in the difference of sample means and why it is used to standardize that difference.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Standard Error Of The Difference In Means): The standard error of X̄1 − X̄2 is used to standardize the difference of sample means because it accounts for what?
A. Variability in the difference
B. Central tendency of each sample
C. The exact population difference
D. Sampling bias
**Answer**: A

#### LO 8: calculate the two-sample t statistic as [(x̄1 − x̄2) − (μ1 − μ2)] / sqrt(s1^2/n1 + s2^2/n2).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Two-Sample t Test Statistic): Which expression is the two-sample t statistic?
A. [(x̄1 − x̄2) − (μ1 − μ2)] / sqrt(s1^2/n1 + s2^2/n2)
B. (x̄1 − x̄2) / (μ1 − μ2)
C. [(μ1 − μ2) − (x̄1 − x̄2)] / (s1 + s2)
D. (x̄1 − x̄2) / sqrt(s1^2 + s2^2)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: explain that the two-sample t statistic standardizes the difference of sample means using the estimated standard error under the null hypothesis.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Two-Sample t Test Statistic): Fill in the blank: The two-sample t statistic is the ______ divided by the standard error, yielding a t-score.
**Answer**: standardized difference

#### LO 10: state the formula for the two-sample t statistic used in Welch’s test.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Two-Sample t Test Statistic): Which term forms the numerator of the two-sample t statistic?
A. (x̄1 − x̄2) − (μ1 − μ2)
B. (x̄1 + x̄2) − (μ1 − μ2)
C. (μ1 − μ2) − (x̄1 − x̄2)
D. x̄1 − x̄2 only
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: calculate the approximate degrees of freedom for Welch’s test using the Welch–Aspin formula from s1, s2, n1, and n2.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Degrees Of Freedom For Welch’s Test): Which inputs are used in the Welch–Aspin approximation for degrees of freedom?
A. s1, s2, n1, and n2
B. Only x̄1 and x̄2
C. Only a pooled variance
D. X̄1 − X̄2 alone
**Answer**: A

#### LO 12: describe that Welch’s degrees of freedom are approximate, may be non-integer, and are typically computed by software.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Degrees Of Freedom For Welch’s Test): Fill in the blank: Welch’s degrees of freedom are an ______ value that may be non-integer and are typically computed by software.
**Answer**: approximate

#### LO 13: identify that sample variances must not be pooled when comparing two means with unknown and possibly unequal population standard deviations.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Non-Pooled Variances): Fill in the blank: In Welch’s two-sample comparison with possibly unequal standard deviations, the sample variances are ______.
**Answer**: not pooled

#### LO 14: calculate the Welch two-sample standard error and degrees of freedom without pooling variances.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Non-Pooled Variances): When computing Welch’s standard error and degrees of freedom, how are the sample variances handled?
A. They are not pooled.
B. They are pooled.
C. They are ignored.
D. They are forced to be equal.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: state that the t approximation is very good when both n1 and n2 are at least five and that when n1 + n2 > 30 the normal distribution can approximate the t.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Distributional Approximation Conditions): Fill in the blank: The t-approximation is very good when both n1 and n2 are at least ______; when n1 + n2 > 30, the normal distribution can approximate the t.
**Answer**: five

#### LO 16: define the random variable as X̄1 − X̄2, the difference between the sample means of the two groups.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Random Variable For Two-Sample Comparison): Fill in the blank: For two-sample comparison, the random variable is ______, the difference between the sample means.
**Answer**: X̄1 − X̄2

#### LO 17: calculate Cohen’s d as (x̄1 − x̄2) / s_pooled.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Cohen’s d): Which formula defines Cohen’s d?
A. (x̄1 − x̄2) / s_pooled
B. [(x̄1 − x̄2) − (μ1 − μ2)] / sqrt(s1^2/n1 + s2^2/n2)
C. (x̄1 − x̄2) / sqrt(s1^2/n1 + s2^2/n2)
D. s_pooled / (x̄1 − x̄2)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 18: classify a computed Cohen’s d as small, medium, or large using the 0.2, 0.5, and 0.8 benchmarks.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Cohen’s d): According to the 0.2, 0.5, and 0.8 benchmarks, a computed Cohen’s d of 0.6 is best classified as:
A. Small
B. Medium
C. Large
D. Cannot be classified without s_pooled
**Answer**: B

#### LO 19: interpret the magnitude of a Cohen’s d by relating it to the small, medium, and large effect size standards.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Cohen’s Standard Effect Sizes): Fill in the blank: Cohen’s standard effect sizes provide ______ for interpreting Cohen’s d where 0.2 is small, 0.5 is medium, and 0.8 is large.
**Answer**: benchmarks

#### LO 20: state Cohen’s standard thresholds for effect size: 0.2 (small), 0.5 (medium), 0.8 (large).
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Cohen’s Standard Effect Sizes): Which set matches Cohen’s standard thresholds for interpreting Cohen’s d?
A. 0.2 (small), 0.5 (medium), 0.8 (large)
B. 0.2 (medium), 0.5 (small), 0.8 (large)
C. 0.2 (large), 0.5 (medium), 0.8 (small)
D. 0.2 (small), 0.5 (large), 0.8 (medium)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 21: define the pooled standard deviation for Cohen’s d as the square root of the weighted average of sample variances from both groups.
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Pooled Standard Deviation (For Cohen’s d)): Fill in the blank: The pooled standard deviation (for Cohen’s d) is a ______ derived from both samples used in the denominator of Cohen’s d.
**Answer**: combined estimate of variability

#### LO 22: calculate the pooled standard deviation from s1, s2, n1, and n2 for use in Cohen’s d.
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Pooled Standard Deviation (For Cohen’s d)): Which inputs are required to calculate the pooled standard deviation for use in Cohen’s d?
A. s1, s2, n1, and n2
B. Only X̄1 and X̄2
C. Only n1 and n2
D. Only the standard error of X̄1 − X̄2
**Answer**: A

## Section: seg_131

#### LO 1: formulate a two-sample hypothesis test for μ1 − μ2 with known population standard deviations, including appropriate H0 and Ha based on problem wording
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Independent Means With Known Population Standard Deviations): With known σ1, σ2 and normal populations, which hypotheses are appropriate for the claim μ1 is greater than μ2?
A. H0: μ1 ≤ μ2; Ha: μ1 > μ2
B. H0: μ1 ≥ μ2; Ha: μ1 < μ2
C. H0: μ1 = μ2; Ha: μ1 ≠ μ2
D. H0: μ1 ≥ μ2; Ha: μ1 > μ2
**Answer**: A

#### LO 2: interpret the outcome of a two-sample z test for μ1 − μ2 by relating the p-value to α and stating the contextual conclusion
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Independent Means With Known Population Standard Deviations): At α = 0.01, a two-sample z test yields p-value = 0.02. What is the decision?
A. Do not reject H0
B. Reject H0
C. Accept Ha
D. Increase α until p-value < α
**Answer**: A

#### LO 3: define X̄1 − X̄2 as the difference between the sample means from two independent groups
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Random Variable X̄1 − X̄2): X̄1 − X̄2 is the ______ used to assess the difference in population means.
**Answer**: difference between the sample means

#### LO 4: calculate x̄1 − x̄2 from given sample means to quantify the observed difference
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Random Variable X̄1 − X̄2): Given x̄1 = 10 and x̄2 = 7, compute x̄1 − x̄2.
A. 3
B. -3
C. 17
D. 0
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: define the sampling distribution of X̄1 − X̄2 as normal with mean μ1 − μ2 and parameter (σ1)²/n1 + (σ2)²/n2
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sampling Distribution Of X̄1 − X̄2): The sampling distribution of X̄1 − X̄2 is ______ with mean μ1 − μ2 and parameter (σ1)²/n1 + (σ2)²/n2.
**Answer**: Normal

#### LO 6: calculate the parameter (σ1)²/n1 + (σ2)²/n2 of the sampling distribution of X̄1 − X̄2 when σ1, σ2, n1, and n2 are known
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sampling Distribution Of X̄1 − X̄2): Compute the parameter (σ1)²/n1 + (σ2)²/n2 for σ1 = 4, σ2 = 3, n1 = 16, n2 = 9.
A. 2
B. 1
C. 4
D. 0.5
**Answer**: A

#### LO 7: define the standard deviation of X̄1 − X̄2 as (σ1)²/n1 + (σ2)²/n2 when both population standard deviations are known
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Standard Deviation Of X̄1 − X̄2): The standard deviation of X̄1 − X̄2 is ______.
**Answer**: (σ1)²/n1 + (σ2)²/n2

#### LO 8: calculate (σ1)²/n1 + (σ2)²/n2 for given σ1, σ2, n1, and n2
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Standard Deviation Of X̄1 − X̄2): For σ1 = 10, σ2 = 6, n1 = 25, n2 = 9, compute (σ1)²/n1 + (σ2)²/n2.
A. 8
B. 10
C. 4
D. 40
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: define the two-sample z test statistic as z = [(x̄1 − x̄2) − (μ1 − μ2)] ÷ [(σ1)²/n1 + (σ2)²/n2]
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Z-Test Statistic For Two Population Means (Known σ1, σ2)): Complete the formula: z = [(x̄1 − x̄2) − (μ1 − μ2)] ÷ ______.
**Answer**: [(σ1)²/n1 + (σ2)²/n2]

#### LO 10: calculate the z test statistic for comparing two means using x̄1, x̄2, the hypothesized μ1 − μ2, σ1, σ2, n1, and n2
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Z-Test Statistic For Two Population Means (Known σ1, σ2)): Given x̄1 = 12, x̄2 = 9, hypothesized μ1 − μ2 = 0, σ1 = 4, σ2 = 3, n1 = 16, n2 = 9, compute z.
A. 1.5
B. 3
C. 0.75
D. -1.5
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: state H0: μ1 ≤ μ2 and Ha: μ1 > μ2 for a right-tailed comparison when the claim is that group 1 is greater
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Hypotheses For Comparing Two Means): For a right-tailed comparison where group 1 is claimed greater, the alternative hypothesis is ______.
**Answer**: μ1 > μ2

#### LO 12: translate comparative phrases such as “is more effective” or “older than” into Ha: μ1 > μ2 with H0: μ1 ≤ μ2
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Hypotheses For Comparing Two Means): Claim: Group 1 is more effective than Group 2. Which hypotheses match this?
A. H0: μ1 ≤ μ2; Ha: μ1 > μ2
B. H0: μ1 ≥ μ2; Ha: μ1 < μ2
C. H0: μ1 = μ2; Ha: μ1 ≠ μ2
D. H0: μ1 ≥ μ2; Ha: μ1 > μ2
**Answer**: A

#### LO 13: translate a stated “greater than” research claim into selecting a right-tailed test for μ1 − μ2
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Right-Tailed Test Determination): When the alternative uses “>”, select a ______ test.
**Answer**: right-tailed

#### LO 14: identify when a right-tailed test is appropriate based on an alternative hypothesis using “>”
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Right-Tailed Test Determination): If Ha: μ1 > μ2, which tail is appropriate?
A. Right-tailed
B. Left-tailed
C. Two-tailed
D. Not enough information
**Answer**: A

#### LO 15: compare α with the p-value and conclude not to reject H0 when α < p-value
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: p-Value Decision Rule): Decision rule: do not reject H0 when ______.
**Answer**: α < p-value

#### LO 16: use the p-value decision rule at a stated α to decide whether to reject H0 for μ1 − μ2
**Multiple Choice** (concept: p-Value Decision Rule): At α = 0.05, the p-value is 0.12. What is the decision?
A. Do not reject H0
B. Reject H0
C. Accept Ha
D. Cannot decide
**Answer**: A

#### LO 17: state the decision rule: do not reject H0 when α < p-value
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: p-Value Decision Rule): State the rule for not rejecting H0 using α and p-value: ______.
**Answer**: α < p-value

#### LO 18: identify the assumptions required for validity: two independent groups and both populations are normal
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Assumptions For Validity): Assumptions for validity: ______ and both populations are normal.
**Answer**: two independent groups

## Section: seg_133

#### LO 1: list the required conditions for comparing two independent population proportions: independent simple random samples, at least five successes and five failures in each sample, and populations at least ten to twenty times the sample sizes
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Conditions For Comparing Two Independent Population Proportions): Fill in the blanks: In each sample, there should be at least five ______ and five ______.
**Answer**: successes and failures

#### LO 2: describe how these conditions support valid two-proportion inference and help prevent oversampling and incorrect results
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Conditions For Comparing Two Independent Population Proportions): Fill in the blank: To help prevent oversampling, each population should be ______.
**Answer**: at least ten or 20 times the sample size

#### LO 3: state the purpose and setup of a hypothesis test for two independent population proportions, including the typical null hypothesis H0: pA = pB
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Hypothesis Test For Two Independent Population Proportions): Fill in the blank: The purpose is to determine whether a difference in estimated sample proportions reflects a ______.
**Answer**: true difference in population proportions

#### LO 4: calculate the p-value for a two-proportion hypothesis test from sample counts using the pooled standard error and normal model
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Hypothesis Test For Two Independent Population Proportions): To compute the p-value from sample counts in a two-proportion test, which approach should you use?
A. Use the pooled standard error with a normal model.
B. Use an unpooled standard error with a t-model for means.
C. Use a chi-square goodness-of-fit test with categories for the two groups.
D. Use a median test with a sign model.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 5: compare the observed difference in sample proportions to what is expected under the null hypothesis to assess whether a population difference is plausible
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Hypothesis Test For Two Independent Population Proportions): What does a two-proportion hypothesis test assess?
A. Whether the observed difference could be due to chance under H0 by comparing it to what is expected under H0.
B. Whether the sample sizes are equal in the two groups.
C. Whether the population variances are identical.
D. Whether the sample counts are integers.
**Answer**: A

#### LO 6: define the null hypothesis for two proportions as equality of population proportions, H0: pA = pB (equivalently pA − pB = 0)
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Null Hypothesis For Two Proportions): Fill in the blank: For two proportions, the null hypothesis states ______.
**Answer**: H0: pA = pB

#### LO 7: define the random variable as the difference in sample proportions, P′A − P′B
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Random Variable For Two-Proportion Test): Fill in the blank: The random variable for a two-proportion test is ______.
**Answer**: P′A − P′B

#### LO 8: calculate the observed value of the random variable P′A − P′B from sample data
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Random Variable For Two-Proportion Test): Given counts xA, nA and xB, nB, the observed value of P′A − P′B equals:
A. xA/nA − xB/nB
B. xA − xB
C. (xA + xB)/(nA + nB)
D. (xA/nA)/(xB/nB)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 9: define the pooled proportion used under the null hypothesis as pc = (xA + xB) / (nA + nB)
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Pooled Proportion): Fill in the blank: Under H0, the pooled proportion is pc = ______.
**Answer**: (xA + xB) / (nA + nB)

#### LO 10: calculate the pooled proportion pc from given sample successes and sizes
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Pooled Proportion): Which expression correctly computes the pooled proportion pc?
A. (xA + xB)/(nA + nB)
B. xA/nA − xB/nB
C. (xA/nA + xB/nB)/2
D. xA xB / (nA nB)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 11: describe the sampling distribution of P′A − P′B as approximately normal with mean 0 and variance pc(1 − pc)(1/nA + 1/nB) under the null hypothesis
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Sampling Distribution Of Difference In Sample Proportions): Fill in the blank: Under H0, the sampling distribution of P′A − P′B is ______.
**Answer**: approximately normal with mean 0

#### LO 12: calculate the standard error sqrt[pc(1 − pc)(1/nA + 1/nB)] for the difference in sample proportions
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sampling Distribution Of Difference In Sample Proportions): What is the standard error for P′A − P′B under H0?
A. sqrt[pc(1 − pc)(1/nA + 1/nB)]
B. sqrt[pA(1 − pA)/nA + pB(1 − pB)/nB]
C. sqrt[(p′A − p′B)]
D. pc(1 − pc)(1/nA + 1/nB)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 13: calculate the two-proportion z test statistic using z = [(p′A − p′B) − (pA − pB)] / sqrt[pc(1 − pc)(1/nA + 1/nB)]
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Two-Proportion Z-Test Statistic): Which formula gives the two-proportion z test statistic?
A. z = [(p′A − p′B) − (pA − pB)] / sqrt[pc(1 − pc)(1/nA + 1/nB)]
B. z = (p′A + p′B) / sqrt[(pA + pB)/2]
C. z = (xA − xB) / sqrt[nA + nB]
D. z = (p′A − p′B) · sqrt[pc(1 − pc)(1/nA + 1/nB)]
**Answer**: A

#### LO 14: state the formula for the two-proportion z test statistic
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Two-Proportion Z-Test Statistic): Fill in the blank: The two-proportion z statistic is z = ______.
**Answer**: [(p′A − p′B) − (pA − pB)] / sqrt[pc(1 − pc)(1/nA + 1/nB)]

#### LO 15: identify whether a test is two-tailed, left-tailed, or right-tailed from phrases such as is a difference, less than, or more popular
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Tail Selection For Two-Proportion Tests): The alternative says there is a difference between pA and pB. Which tail is appropriate?
A. Two-tailed (≠)
B. Left-tailed (<)
C. Right-tailed (>)
D. No tail (equality)
**Answer**: A

#### LO 16: classify a research question into the correct tail direction by matching the alternative hypothesis to <, >, or ≠
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Tail Selection For Two-Proportion Tests): If the alternative is H1: pA > pB, which tail is used?
A. Right-tailed (>)
B. Left-tailed (<)
C. Two-tailed (≠)
D. Undetermined tail
**Answer**: A

#### LO 17: compare the p-value to the significance level α to decide whether to reject or not reject the null hypothesis
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Decision Rule Using P-Value And Alpha): Fill in the blank: Compare the p-value to the ______ to decide whether to reject or not reject H0.
**Answer**: significance level α

#### LO 18: use the p-value and α to make and report a reject or do not reject decision for H0 in context
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Decision Rule Using P-Value And Alpha): If the p-value is greater than α, what is the decision?
A. Do not reject H0
B. Reject H0
C. Accept H1 as true
D. Conclude causation
**Answer**: A

#### LO 19: state the decision rule: reject H0 if the p-value is less than or equal to α; otherwise do not reject H0
**Fill-in-the-blank** (concept: Decision Rule Using P-Value And Alpha): Fill in the blank: Reject H0 if the p-value is ______ α; otherwise do not reject H0.
**Answer**: less than or equal to

#### LO 20: calculate a sample proportion p′ = x / n for each group
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sample Proportion): Which formula calculates a sample proportion p′?
A. x / n
B. (xA + xB) / (nA + nB)
C. n / x
D. x − n
**Answer**: A

#### LO 21: compare two sample proportions to obtain the observed difference used in the two-proportion test
**Multiple Choice** (concept: Sample Proportion): Given p′A and p′B, which quantity is used as the observed difference?
A. p′A − p′B
B. p′A + p′B
C. p′A × p′B
D. p′A / p′B
**Answer**: A
